#2889. Demis Hassabis’ AI company raises $5 billion, months after he left as Google DeepMind CEO

Isomorphic Labs, an artificial intelligence drug discovery startup founded by former Google DeepMind chief Demis Hassabis, has raised a $5 billion Series C, the company said on Tuesday.

Isomorphic's round comes three-and-a-half months after Hassabis stepped back as CEO of DeepMind, Google's AI lab, which he co-founded before the Big Tech giant acquired it in 2014.

The round was co-led by Greenoaks and Sequoia Capital, alongside Andreessen Horowitz, with investment from Alphabet, Google's parent. The fresh funds will help fund the design and development of new medicines using AI.

Drug discovery startups have seen record funding poured into them by investors in 2026 as companies look to capitalize on advances in AI. OpenAI and Anthropic have also been investing in tools to develop new medicines using the technology.

Hassabis has been CEO of Isomorphic Labs since the startup spun out of DeepMind in 2021.

"When we founded Isomorphic, our conviction was that biology could be understood and modelled at the most fundamental molecular level and radically accelerated through AI," said Hassabis.

"This round enables us to accelerate our progress: building foundational biology models, deepening our systemic disease understanding, and driving our first therapeutic programs towards clinical trials to deliver tangible impact for patients," he added.

Isomorphic is building a proprietary drug design engine and therapeutic candidate pipeline designed with AI, which it says will help it deliver the next generation of medicines.

Anthropic announced it was launching an AI drug discovery program in June and OpenAI partnered with Novo Nordisk in April.

Drug discovery startups have raised $23.2 billion in 2026, according to deal-counting platform Dealroom, a record figure.

Isomorphic last raised in May, with a $2.1 billion Series B.

#2887. Diving deeper into Cavs 126-115 win over Bulls: Cavaliers are discovering hidden depth

CLEVELAND — It wasn't the prettiest game, but the Cleveland Cavaliers did enough to get past a struggling Chicago Bulls team 126-115 thanks to a 39-point performance from Donovan Mitchell.

39 points on 16-of-25 shooting, 4 rebounds, 3 assists

Mitchell showed once again why he's one of the best scorers in the league. He repeatedly got into the teeth of the defense to create high-quality looks, going 11-for-14 on shots in the paint.

That was all the Cavs needed to get past the Bulls.

27 points on 10-of-18 shooting, 6 assists, 2 rebounds, 1 steal

Powell has a difficult task. His teammates are all slotted one or two roles higher than they would be on a more functional team. This often forces Powell to create in a cramped half-court and playmake for teammates that the defense is mostly fine with leaving open.

Despite those limitations, Powell has been a useful player this season, and was again on Monday. He went 7-for-9 on shots in the paint. A 0-for-6 shooting performance from three kept this from being an even better night.

17 points on 6-of-13 shooting, 3 rebounds, 2 assists

It's incredibly difficult to shift between roles as seamlessly as Proctor has done in the last week. He's gone from being the primary on-ball creator in the win over the Dallas Mavericks to being a mostly off-ball shooter and second-side facilitator alongside the veteran backcourt. Vastly different things are being asked of him depending on the role.

On Monday, Proctor was a good off-ball player. He initiated actions when the ball swung to him and wasn't afraid to shoot when he had openings. The Cavs need tertiary players who can attack a defense that's overloaded to stop dribble penetration from Mitchell and James Harden. Proctor has shown over the past few weeks that he can do that. He'll be a mainstay in the rotation as long as that continues.

Afterward, head coach Kenny Atkinson said that he's still figuring out what Proctor's role is when the team is fully healthy.

"It's starting to reveal itself," Atkinson said. "We all know when the playoffs come and all that they're going to take Don and James away, and you need that other ball handler to get in the paint and make plays."

Mitchell echoed that point when asked about Proctor's game. Someone who can ball-handle like that can help in the postseason

"If he's going to continue to score, then we'll take the buckets," Mitchell said. "And then if he makes the right plays, which he has been, it's just a plus for us."

This was the fourth straight game Proctor has scored 15 or more points.

Anything bought from the links helps support Fear the Sword. You can buy the Browns throwback shirt HERE. You can also shop all of Homage's Cleveland gear HERE.

4 points, 8 assists, 4 rebounds, 6 turnovers

The Bulls desperately needed someone who could create off the dribble. Giddey is one of the few players who has the talent to actually do that. However, he wasn't able to do that here, resulting in the Bulls benching him down the stretch.

4 points, 6 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 steal, 1 block

Omier plays basketball like the linebacker at your college that you run into at open gym. Nobody wants to try to stay in front of a guy who's that physical and single-mindedly focused on running through whoever is standing in their way. And they're stronger than everyone else they're playing with, which makes it even more unpleasant.

Unlike this annoying guy you run into at open gym, Omier can actually play basketball.

It's incredibly difficult to make it in the league as a 6'5" forward — especially if you don't have superb on-ball skills. Omier can shoot some, but nobody would say he's a stretch big. He is, however, a tank that is willing to bulldoze his opponent to get to his spots.

Omier did that here. The Cavs were sleepwalking through the first quarter with some pretty uninspiring basketball, then Omier entered the game and changed that by simply moving people out of the way.

"He's got a magnet for hands, strength, quickness to the ball," Atkinson said. "I know it's just two games, but it's pretty impressive."

Guys like this are incredibly important over an 82-game season. Sometimes you just need someone to come in and light a fire under your team.

"I'm just glad we don't have to box him out in camp anymore," Mitchell joked.

9 points, 10 assists, 4 rebounds, 1 steal

This was a good outing from an offensive process standpoint. Harden wasn't an efficient scorer (3-for-10), but he wasn't forcing the issue. More importantly, he kept the offense moving. He wasn't dominating the ball, was looking to hit outlet passes when they were available, and limited his turnovers (two). This led to him leading the team in plus/minus with a +15.

"This is starting to be similar to last year where we're playing a lot of different guys. … Last year there's a real silver lining to that. We've learned a lot about our guys. … You want to win games while you're discovering these guys, and that's a good thing about these past two games that we found a way to win the game, but now we're discovering different players and different lineups."

– Kenny Atkinson on what he's learned about the team's depth

It's been an uneven start to the season. However, learning more about guys like Meleek Thomas, Proctor, and others could be a blessing. The Cavs have gotten a good look at how they handle actually meaningful minutes. These reps can be invaluable for guys trying to find their place in the league, and you can't replicate them anywhere else.

Mitchell went 8-for-10 on shots in the paint.

"I love when he gets downhill like that and in the paint," Atkinson said. "I know he's a great pull-up three-point shooter. Just when he gets in the paint for us, it just causes so much havoc."

This wasn't a remarkable game for Cleveland. The Bulls are simply a bad team. They don't have the talent to compete against skilled teams on a night-to-night basis — especially when they aren't hitting their threes. Putting them away with a relatively stress-free victory is exactly what you want.

There wasn't a whole lot from this game that you'd feel comfortable extrapolating further, but that's okay. Sometimes you just need to pick up wins when you play games like this, and that's what the Cavs did.

#2881. Nets’ comeback bid falls short against LeBron James-less 76ers

Few NBA teams can rival the 76ers in terms of star power these days, even when LeBron James is out of their lineup.

The Nets have a few intriguing present and future pieces on their roster, but they definitely are not among that select group of teams.

James was out of uniform for the tail end of back-to-back games, but the Nets comeback bid from a 24-point hole and an ugly first-half defensive performance ultimately fell short in a 135-123 loss Monday night to the reloaded Sixers at Barclays Center.

Philadelphia 76ers center Joel Embiid (21) fights for the ball with Brooklyn Nets forward Julius Randle (30) in New York, Monday, Nov. 16, 2026. AP Photo/Yuki Iwamura

"We found the energy, we made the run, but we fell short," head coach Jordi Fernández said. "At the end of the day, especially against a team like that, you want to end up playing like this from the beginning.

"So I can say that I'm proud of the second half that we played. Now, our challenge is trying to put four quarters together."

Joel Embiid recorded a season-high 37 points, Jaylen Brown finished with 31 points and 13 rebounds, and Tyrese Maxey added 24 as the Sixers improved to 12-3. They are now 4-0 in games played without James, the four-time league MVP and the NBA's all-time leading scorer.

Julius Randle matched his season-high 33 points, Michael Porter Jr. contributed 25, and Nolan Traore tied his career best with 23 for the Nets (3-10).

Jordi Fernández ditched the supersize lineup he used in recent games, going back to Traore at the point while shifting Egor Dëmin back to shooting guard as Danny Wolf returned to a reserve role.

Porter was back among the starting unit after sitting out Sunday's loss in Memphis for personal reasons, replacing Noah Clowney.

Dëmin and Mikel Brown Jr., the team's past two lottery picks, remained on separate units. The linchpins of the organization's rebuilding plan came into the game having totaled just 18 minutes on the court together this season, with Dëmin missing six games due to a bone bruise and Brown subjected to a minutes restriction early in his rookie campaign.

Anfernee Simons buried the first five points of the second quarter to stretch a 33-26 lead to 12.

Philadelphia 76ers center Joel Embiid (21) shoots over Brooklyn Nets center Day'Ron Sharpe (20) during an NBA game in New York, Monday, Nov. 16, 2026. AP Photo/Yuki Iwamura

The Sixers dominated boards early, building a 19-8 edge as the lead ballooned to 46-28 on a three-point play by Dean Wade barely two minutes into the period.

The Nets trailed by as many as 24 before halftime following a wide-open Maxey trey with a little over five minutes remaining.

The Sixers led 78-59 at intermission, just three off the Nets franchise record for points allowed in the first half set by the Knicks with 81 in 2023.

"We were getting scored on every single possession," Dëmin said. "That's not something we're proud of."

The Nets' 13-2 spurt helped them climb back to within 10 thanks to an and-one by Traore plus a Dëmin 3-pointer midway through the third quarter.

Brooklyn Nets forward Julius Randle drives past Philadelphia 76ers center Joel Embiid and guard Jaylen Brown on Monday, Nov. 16, 2026, in Brooklyn, N.Y. JASON SZENES FOR NEW YORK POST

Traore's weaving layup, a 3-pointer by Clowney and a bucket by Porter cut the lead to six with under three minutes left in the period. But Jabari Walker rattled in a 3-pointer at the buzzer for a 10-point Philly cushion, 105-95, entering the final quarter.

Two more long-range buckets by Wolf and Randle cut the Nets deficit back to five in the opening minutes of the fourth.

Dëmin knocked down another triple to eventually pull them within four with 1:14 to play, but Embiid responded with his second 3-pointer as the Sixers closed out the game.

"We were a more connected group in the second half on both ends of the floor," Randle said. "Maybe we should punch first. I think in the second half, we punched first and set the tone.

"I think we have to start every game like that, punching first."

#2878. Future Hogs Week 11: College football players who could help the Commanders

The college football games from Week 11 are unlikely to change the College Football Playoff landscape much because 11 of the top 12 teams in the playoff rankings won. Georgia easily dispatched of Missouri, Utah barely beat Arizona in one of the year's most exciting games, Alabama and its defense smothered Vanderbilt, and UCLA remained undefeated by beating Illinois. LSU's playoff hopes are over after Texas scored a last-minute touchdown to escape Baton Rouge with a win. The top team in the ACC, Miami, needed the second half to pull away from Duke. The only team in the top 12 to lose was Kentucky. The Wildcats just couldn't make enough plays to beat Florida. It's likely Texas Tech will replace Kentucky in playoff rankings.

After having their playoff hopes dashed, LSU could play spoiler in Week 12 by knocking Tennessee out of contention. The top team, Georgia, will go on the road to face South Carolina. Utah will also be on the road to take on TCU. UCLA will leave sunny southern California for Ann Arbor to face Michigan. SMU, who got blown out in Week 11 by Wake Forest, will look to bounce back against Notre Dame.

Here are a few players who had a good week and might be able to help out the Commanders. Let me know what you think about these prospects and feel free to post players that stood out to you:

While Boise State has been able to rely on its run game much of the year, Broncos' quarterback Maddux Madsen had his best game of the season. While Maddux is short for an NFL QB at only 5'10", he weighs 210 pounds. His height doesn't appear to affect his vision as he is able to find receivers all over the field. Madsen has enough arm to push the ball down the field, but it looks like he has to put a lot of air under his deeper passes. His passes are accurate with good ball placement. While he's not a dual threat, Madsen can scramble effectively and gain positive yardage with well-timed QB runs. The Broncos won a shootout with Oregon State thanks to Madsen's 371 yards and four touchdowns on 23 of 32 passes.

If you long for the days when the Commanders had a big back that was tough to bring down, Micah Ford might interest you as a prospect. After watching a few plays where he bounced off defenders or ran through them, I had to look up his measurables. Ford is 6', 225 pounds. That's a large running back that can dole out damage, but he is also a big target. He could remedy that by running less upright. At that size, Ford has good burst. He is most effective when he can get downhill immediately as his lateral quickness appears to be average at best. While he's not a huge receiving threat, it is a part of his game as he has 27 receptions on the year. Ford helped Stanford beat Virginia Tech with 27 carries for 149 yards and three touchdowns.

It is possible that Aidan Mizell is the fastest wide receiver in college football. He's topped over 22 miles per hour this season according Reel Analytics. His only competition might be his younger brother, Arizona receiver Isaiah Mizell. Back to Aidan, he is slight at 6'1" but only weighs 177 pounds. You can't hit what you can't see, right? Mizell uses his speed to get open. He regularly beats defenders deep downfield and tracks the ball well. UCLA will also manufacture touches for him because once the ball is in Mizell's hands, he can take it the distance. He has to show that he's more than just a speed merchant by running a greater variety of routes and using his route running to get open. The Bruins beat Illinois, and Mizell contributed six catches for 176 yards and a touchdown.

After Ben Sinnott's performance against the New York Giants, I'm not sure we need another tight end. Heck, just trade John Bates and Chig Okonkwo in the offseason and use those roster spots for other positions. Okay, maybe that's going too far…maybe. Adding a player like Eli Finley to the tight end room would make it better. He's got good size at 6'4", 246 pounds. Finley is a good route runner and has good athleticism with that frame. That athleticism makes him effective on and off the line of scrimmage. Once he makes a catch, Finley is a good runner and can be hard to tackle. As a blocker, Finley latches onto defenders with strong hands and drives them off the ball. Louisiana Tech scored 63 points in a win over Southern Missouri, and Finley had six catches for 104 yards and a touchdown.

Arizona suffered a close loss to Utah despite putting up 39 points. Although there was a stretch of the game where Utah put a ton of pressure on Wildcats QB Noah Fifita, Arizona still had a ton of explosive plays. Zach Henning was a big reason Fifita had time to get the ball downfield. Henning is stout in the passing game at 6'4", 310 pounds. He plays with a strong base and strong core that allow him to anchor against power up the middle, something the Commanders need considering the defensive tackles in the NFC East. He's a little inconsistent in the run game. Arizona uses a lot of inside zone runs. What he does best is work to the second level to find blockers, but he can struggle to generate movement at the point of attack. Henning wears #58 in the clips below.

I wanted to highlight Myles Bradley earlier this season. In Week 4, he had a game with 4.5 sacks. It just so happens that Colin Simmons from Texas had five sacks that week. Well in Week 11, Bradley had five sacks, bringing his NCAA-leading total to 14.5. One thing that we have to get out of the way. Bradley is a small edge rusher at only 6', 230 pounds. That's so small that he may only be a pass-rush specialist in the NFL. The other thing is Bradley plays defensive end in a 4-3. Can he drop to play in space as required by a 3-4 OLB? What he definitely can do is use his speed to beat tackles around the corner and bring down the quarterback. He uses that speed to set up an effective spin move. While his hand usage is good, you won't see many power rushes. To go along with his five sacks, Bradley had nine total tackles, a forced fumble, and two deflections in Bowling Green's win over Kent State.

It's been a tough year for Virginia, but they were able to get a comfortable win against California. The Cavaliers' defense only allowed seven points with Kam Robinson leading the way. For the second year in a row, Robinson got a pick-six against the Golden Bears. It's because Robinson is a smart player that can quickly react to an offense. He uses that skill in coverage. In zone, Robinson drops to the right depth quickly and closes space on receivers to knock the ball away or make a pick. His quickness helps him beat blockers to make plays in the backfield. At 6'2", 239 pounds, it'd be nice to see Robinson play to his size by taking on blockers instead of running around them. To go with that pick-six, the fourth of his career which ties a school record, Robinson had 12 total tackles.

The matchup between Utah and Arizona was an exciting affair that went down to the wire. With the game in the balance on a key fourth down, it was Jackson Bennee who made the deciding play. Although Utah gave up their fair number of passes to Arizona and their QB Noah Fifita, Jackson Bennee stood out in a major way. Utah plays Bennee in the slot and he's superb playing man coverage from this position. That's because Bennee is incredibly quick and changes directions with ease. It is surprising to see him do this at 6'2", 190 pounds. The blend of quickness, agility, and length allows him to stay attached to receivers throughout their routes. Utah lists Bennee as a safety, but he looks like a slot cornerback at the next level. That's largely because Bennee lacks stopping power as a hitter and tackler. The effort and willingness is there, but he'll have to be stronger in this area to play the slot in the NFL. Bennee had three tackles and two PBUs in the Utes big win over the Wildcats.

Texas needed all 60 minutes to beat LSU, but there's no way they would've been in a position to win without Jelani McDonald. Last week, mistamoe2 commented that the Commanders could use a top-notch free safety. McDonald might be that player. He stands 6'2" and weighs 201 pounds. The Longhorns trust McDonald to play numerous alignments from single-high safety to split-safety looks, to man coverage. In zone coverage, McDonald is incredibly aware. He trusts his eyes to take him to where the ball is going and is rarely wrong. In man coverage, he moves fluidly to stay with receivers throughout their routes and will look back for the ball to avoid penalties. His run support and tackling is another attribute of his. Although he's not the biggest safety, he is physical. McDonald had 11 total tackles, a PBU, and three interceptions to help the Longhorns take down the Tigers.

Which college players caught your eye in Week 11 and could help the Washington Commanders? Let us know in the comments below:

#2874. Sixers Bell Ringer: Sixers back in East’s top spot on back of big performances from Embiid, Brown

Sixers Bell Ringer Season Standings:Jaylen Brown – 3.5Tyrese Maxey – 3.5VJ Edgecombe – 3Joel Embiid – 1LeBron James – 1Anfernee Simons – 1Dean Wade – 1

The Sixers traveled to Brooklyn on Monday night for their second night of their back-to-back after defeating the Indiana Pacers Sunday. LeBron James sat out this one for rest, but the Sixers had the services of Joel Embiid. Embiid wasted no time making his presence felt, pouring in 13 first-quarter points to lead the Sixers, who led 33-26 after one.

The Sixers exploded in quarter No. 2 for 45 points to stretch their lead after two to 78-59. Embiid led all scorers with 24 points at the halftime break. Jaylen Brown posted a dominant half of his own with 19 points and 10 rebounds.

The Nets countered with a big quarter of their own cutting the lead to 105-95 after three quarters. A big three at the buzzer from Jabari Walker helped the Sixers stall the Nets' run.

Brown left the game early in the fourth quarter after sustaining a blow to the head from Julius Randle but would later return. The Nets got the Sixers' lead down to just four points with under a minute to go when Embiid knocked down a pull-up corner three with 52 seconds to go to give the Sixers the needed cushion. The Nets fought hard and made the Sixers play all 48 minutes to close out this game. The Sixers, who led by as many as 24 points, would go onto win by a final score of 135-123. The Sixers moved back to first in the Eastern Conference.

Joel Embiid: 37 points, 5 rebounds, 3 blocks, 13-for-22 from the field, 9-for-10 from the line

Joel Embiid put together his best scoring game of the young season thus far. He looks to have battled through the foot injury that has limited him over the last few weeks and is back moving as good as he has all season. Embiid found his touch in the midrange where he dominated the game, catching a rhythm in an area of the floor where he has made a living throughout his career. Embiid, who has really struggled from three this season, also knocked down a pair from beyond the arc, including a pull-up dagger with under a minute to go. A healthy Joel Embiid is a dominant Joel Embiid and he appears to be trending in the right direction physically — and the touch has returned as well.

Jaylen Brown: 31 points, 13 rebounds, 4 assists, 9-for-19 from the field, 10-for-13 from the line

Jaylen Brown put up a 19-point and 10-rebound double-double in the first half alone in this one. He continues to stack good performances and show why he is the All-NBA and All-Star performer that he has been for so many years. Brown poured it in from all three levels as he often does putting on a clinical scoring performance. It was nice to see him return late in the game after suffering the blow to the head earlier in the fourth quarter. Brown's 13 rebounds are a season high for him. Defensive rebounding is another area himself and the Sixers continue to excel, which was a large concern for the team overall coming into the season. The Sixers' unselfish brand of basketball continues to lead to wins and standout performances for all different cast members.

#2872. Investors look to Nvidia's earnings and Anthropic's IPO to refresh the tech rally

I've spent the past 20 years pushing back against the periodically popular notion that markets are replaying the late-1990s and early-2000s tech bubble and bust.

We've simply never matched the levels of gross overvaluation, pervasive public greed, fevered IPO issuance, corporate malinvestment and Wall Street malfeasance that set up a generational market peak, a halving of the S&P 500 over two-and-a-half years and a lost decade for stocks.

We still haven't. Even if June was the peak in AI speculative fervor, as seems possible, and if Oct. 6 proves to have been a consequential broad-market top, the major indexes simply never stretched far enough into the thin air to make a potential retrenchment nearly as damaging as the early 2000s were.

Still, in a more muted fashion, markets have been behaving in ways that generate uncomfortable comparisons to past buying climaxes accompanying a technology revolution.

There is still room and time for the market to prove that the messy sell-down in AI-reliant growth stocks is simply a painful adjustment phase and the S&P 500's six-month purgatorial range a mid-cycle refresh. But it will need to start getting on with it.

Everyone wants to celebrate the way the market has absorbed the damage to semiconductors and other AI-buildout plays, rendering it a controlled demolition rather than indiscriminate destruction.

The Financial Times Unhedged newsletter on Monday called it "The perfect sell-off," the same day the Wall Street Journal cheered, "When Nvidia Stumbled, Equal-Weight Funds Delivered."

This is true, as far as it goes. A rush into defensive and "quality" stocks has largely offset the drag from Nvidia's $2 trillion market-cap loss from its October peak.

But recent defensive leadership, especially from low-growth consumer staples and Big Pharma, sends a less-reassuring macro message, and those groups lack the earnings momentum, narrative appeal or market-cap heft to carry a bull market to new heights.

BTIG technical strategist Jonathan Krinsky has noted echoes of the 2000 period when "boring" defensive stocks ripped as tech rolled over, supporting the tape for a while. Barclays equity desk noted Monday that the "quality factor" basket of stocks just had one of the best 100-day runs ever of the past 20 years.

And JP Morgan here plots the stampede into "low-volatility" stocks, mirroring the hasty retreat from "high-momentum" areas. There's room here for defensives to get even more popular before tripping multi-year extremes, but is that what an index investor should root for?

As for the ascendance of everyone's favorite, the equal-weighted S&P 500, Ned Davis Research analyst London Stockton this month noted that just over 50% of S&P 500 stocks were outperforming the index itself in 2026. Sounds healthy, right? Only to a point.

He explains: "Levels [of stocks outperforming the index] above 58% have most often occurred in and around years where there has been a large bear market." The current phase follows a period when leadership was extremely narrow, producing a "resemblance of the prior three years (2023-2025) to the two years prior to the last Tech bubble (1998-1999) with a very small percentage of companies outperforming the overall S&P 500.

"It is not likely that we are in a situation like in 2000," Stockton concludes, "but this highlights the importance that these large tech stocks at the very least do not decline substantially for the S&P 500 to continue rallying higher."

There are other straws in the wind that harken to prior tech busts:  The Federal Reserve is trying to restrain the corporate sector from overheating in the latter part of a cycle, same as in 1999. Retail traders have maniacally bought the cascading decline in Nvidia shares the past week or so, invoking the voracious dip-buying made famous in the late-'90s which worked until it failed spectacularly.  The market's refusal to pay up for sizzling earnings growth raises alarms about whether they are pulled forward, unsustainable or overstated; profits from 2000 were later revised down radically.

And Nvidia's alleged breach of chip-export rules and the forced departure of CEO Jensen Huang are a reminder that major corporate scandals were a key feature of the Tech Bubble's prolonged reckoning, though nothing resembling the multiple accounting frauds of that era are implied here.

Again, this is the kind of litany of worry and woe that gets recited each time a tech-propelled bull market breaks stride for a bit; more often than not a recovery follows to clear the gloom.

Perhaps Nvidia's results on Tuesday clearing the way for the company to restart stock buybacks and the final allocation of Anthropic's IPO shares will refresh the tech bid. With luck, the huge "hyperscaler" tech-platform stocks will move back to the fore as well-funded stewards of a more sober AI expansion.

Maybe, after weeks of Wall Street insisting that hedge-fund tech-stock positioning is scraping multi-year lows, this will finally presage an upside pain trade.

The vaunted seasonal, post-election tailwinds that the consensus was expecting to fill market's sails and lift the indexes from the floor of an enervating six-month trading range are running late. They need to arrive quite soon. Otherwise, the soggy tape - along with signs of ebbing liquidity reflected in Bitcoin weakness a strong dollar and jumpier equity volatility - will make those tech-bust echoes louder.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Despite pervasive bearish sentiment and the average stock trading near new highs, our historical studies suggest the market remains vulnerable to disappointment. With defensive leadership still dominating, risk appetite weak, and financial conditions still tight, maintaining a short-term cautious/bearish stance still seems warranted."

-Twenty years ago this week, I wrote a cover story for Barron's that antagonized plenty of friends in the stock-trading profession by foretelling a future of fast, cheap electronic markets that has largely come to pass.

Called "Death of the Floor," the piece detailed the regulatory, technological and behavioral shifts that were ushering in high-frequency, low-touch trading and pressuring the old exchange model.

Reading it now, the conclusions seem utterly obvious and inevitable, though at the time there were still 2,500 NYSE floor brokers and specialists trying to remain in the middle of most trades.

"For the specialists who manage trading in individual stocks and for the floor brokers who depend on the in-person auction model, a tough challenge is becoming even tougher. To stay in the game, they must master cybertrading. For the small investor, this is almost entirely good news. Trading costs should continue to fall, while the likelihood of getting the best price should improve."

Happily, exchanges have adapted and evolved – to zero commissions, thousands of ETFs and microsecond executions - and we have a market that can handle multiples of the capital flow that was routine back then.

If I have one regret about the piece, it's that my good friend and former colleague Bob Pisani thought the CNBC reporter skeleton illustrating the article was meant to depict him.

I assured him, many times, that it was not.

-Almost every business relies heavily on its most loyal repeat customers. But this skew seems extreme for online-betting companies.

A Bloomberg piece picked up here by Yahoo Finance reports that one-third of all sports bettors produces some 97% of revenue at digital sportsbooks. It says the 1% most active gamblers on average lost 78 cents of each dollar bet, twice the loss rate of the norm among all customers.

One can view this as simply paying for entertainment (I "lose" 100 cents of every dollar I place on a movie ticket), but the social disapproval toward the industry seems to have grown.

Add it to the swirl of issues surrounding the legal and de-facto decriminalized wagering industry.

Perceived hostility of Congressional Democrats to prediction-markets venues taking sports bets in states that haven't explicitly approved sports gambling is seen as a plus for DraftKings and Flutter.

Meantime, Robinhood has blurred the lines between investing and gambling by aggressively expanding into prediction markets. And on Monday it was reported that Kalshi has looked to combine investing and betting from the opposite direction, acquiring a brokerage firm in order to offer stock trading to its prediction-markets clientele.

Adults should be free to behave unwisely with their money in search of a windfall. But brokers promoting the negative-sum game of wagering while professing to help the little guy accumulate long-term wealth sends a dissonant message.

The ducks that were quacking loudly ahead of SpaceX's June IPO are much quieter in advance of Anthropic's planned debut this week.

Here is the year-to-date performance of two ETFs that hold shares of recent new listings, Renaissance IPO and First Trust US Equity Opportunities, relative to the S&P 500.

As of June 12, when SpaceX priced, these funds had gone vertical as the public worked itself into an anticipatory lather, the Renaissance fund running ahead of the S&P by some 20 percentage points in the prior six months.

Since then, the IPO funds have deflated (both now have large positions in SpaceX, of course) and there's a bit more focus on whether Anthropic's target valuation is justified given the vast rethink of the AI-proliferation thesis.

All else being equal, a more favorable setup now versus then for a market being asked to swallow a new $1 trillion-plus ticker.

#2871. Is there concern about Nick Bosa upon his return from injury?

The San Francisco 49ers defense struggled on Sunday against one of the best offenses in the league, as they gave up 40 points to the Dallas Cowboys.

Following the game, there were two main talking points about the defense: the cornerback struggles and the up-and-down pass rush. A lot of the pass rush focus centered on Nick Bosa, who has been a bit quieter statistically since returning from injury.

Bosa has yet to record a sack this season in four appearances, while having eight tackles. Is there any concern with the star pass rusher coming back from the nagging injury that kept him out for nearly two months?

"I see Nick working his way back," head coach Kyle Shanahan said on Monday. "I think that he has a number of plays that look just like Nick, and I think he's had a few plays that doesn't. I think Nick's ready to go, and I think he's definitely affecting the quarterback and doing some good things out there. But Nick's going to get better the more he gets back with us. He's taken a lot of time off with his injury.

"He had a lot of time off before that, going into Australia with his injury over the summer and everything like that, or from last year, so he hasn't gotten to play a lot of football. But I still see a guy out there who's a top pass rusher, who does a lot of good things. He's got to handle the majority of the chips, which doesn't give him as many opportunities on the edge. But I'm not worried about Nick. Nick's going to get better each week, and he'll eventually get there."

As a whole, the 49ers sacked Dak Prescott just once on Sunday after the Indianapolis Colts and Arizona Cardinals sacked him three and four times, respectively, over the two weeks prior. How did Shanahan see his unit as a whole?

"I thought we definitely rushed the passer better earlier on," Shanahan said. "Obviously, we didn't get any sacks, but I thought we affected the quarterback. I thought we hit him. I thought we wore down as the game went. I wouldn't have said it was a bad pass rush game by any means if I never watched the fourth quarter. But, by the time we got to the fourth quarter, I thought we had a chance, it was 38-19 with like nine-and-a-half minutes to go, and I thought we had a number of chances to get them off the field. And they stayed on for whatever the reason.

"Everyone had their moments in that, but then I thought we really died out and the pass rush disappeared. I thought all 11 did, but mainly our D-Line. A lot of them hold on it way too long. That was too much time with those wideouts running around. We've got to be able to finish better. We've got to clean some stuff up. I thought there were some times that we did rush them real well and Dak [Prescott] did a good job getting rid of it. Sometimes they had some chippers that we need to rush better on because we didn't get there at all. But for the whole part of the game, we've definitely got to do better."

When you look back on the film with Bosa's pass-rushing snaps, it's evident he's constantly getting double-teamed and chipped. That's more an indictment of the rest of the room than Bosa himself, as teams aren't fearing the pass rush elsewhere and instead are focusing game plans on San Francisco's star.

There have been a couple of seasons where Bosa's sack production hasn't told the full picture. Bosa had 69 pressures in 2024 with an elite 20 percent pass-rush win rate, but had only (I know, only!) nine sacks that season while playing 14 games.

In 2023, playing a full 17-game season, Bosa had 10.5 sacks, but an incredible 122 pressures with a 21.8 percent pass-rush win rate. Now, this season, in four games, Bosa has 13 pressures with a 16.7 percent pass-rush win rate despite the zero sacks. That's still a top-15 rate among edge rushers this year.

Bosa is still coming back from his injury, and the 49ers pass rush is still middling as a whole. Not one of their rotational defensive linemen has a pass-rush win rate over 12 percent outside of Bosa, which has allowed teams to chip him quite a bit.

Even with the doubles and the chips, Bosa still had five pressures on Sunday while getting his biggest workload of the year. Additionally, as I said in the three takeaways, yesterday's troubles felt more against the secondary than the pass rush as well.

There should be brighter days ahead for Bosa's sack production, but the underlying metrics and film are painting a more positive picture in his return from injury, at least from Sunday's game.

#2867. Game Recap: Seven Suns score in double figures in balanced victory over Denver, 117-105

The Phoenix Suns, on the second night of a back -to-back, made the journey to the Mile High City to open up their five-game road trip. The result? A 117-105 win.

It was a solid team victory for the Phoenix Suns, who once again watched their starting unit struggle early before the bench provided a highly effective and efficient first-half performance. In the second half, however, we saw a much more engaged version of the Suns' starting five, helping Phoenix build a lead that grew as large as 19 points.

The Suns shot 44.4% from beyond the arc, knocking down 16 three-pointers while winning the rebounding battle by a convincing 51-35 margin. And while Denver owned the interior early in the game, scoring 38 points in the paint in the first half, Phoenix surrendered only 12 in the second half. The Suns also converted 18 Denver turnovers into 31 points, reminding us how effective this basketball team can be when it's playing with defensive aggression and purpose.

Nikola Jokic and Jamal Murray each finished with 28 points, but Phoenix countered with a balanced offensive attack throughout the evening. Devin Booker led all Suns scorers with 20 points despite shooting 5-of-14 from the field, and seven Phoenix players finished in double figures.

On the second night of a back-to-back, on the road, against a quality opponent, the Suns received meaningful contributions throughout their rotation. They defended, they rebounded, they knocked down their shots, and they looked increasingly connected as the game progressed. That's the version of Phoenix Suns basketball we've been waiting to see with greater consistency.

The win is the Suns' first on the road and moves the team to 6-7 on the year.

If you were hoping to see some adjustments to the starting five, prepare to be disappointed. The Phoenix Suns rolled out the same starting lineup they've deployed for the majority of the season.

The starting unit played Denver even through the first 5:37 of the game. That said, the Suns were uncharacteristically careless with the ball, committing four early turnovers that led to four easy points for the Nuggets.

Denver was making its living in the paint, scoring 22 of its 24 first-quarter points there. Neither team could find much success from beyond the arc, combining to go 1-of-11 from deep, with the lone triple coming courtesy of Jordan Goodwin.

Nikola Jokic and Cam Johnson each scored six points to lead the Nuggets, while Goodwin paced the Suns with five. It was a relatively uninspiring quarter of offensive basketball, with Phoenix trailing 24-20 heading into the second period.

The second quarter began with an interesting adjustment: Dillon Brooks shared the floor with Luke Kennard, Khaman Maluach, Jordan Goodwin, and Collin Gillespie. You could sense that Brooks felt more empowered to be the alpha offensively within that unit, taking the majority of the shots and asserting himself as the primary scoring option. The problem? He wasn't hitting them, going 1-of-4 from the field through the first three minutes of the quarter.

Khaman Maluach checked out, and in came Koa Peat. You could immediately feel his impact on the group as a whole. There was more physicality, more activity on the glass, and a greater sense of balance.

With Collin Gillespie, Luke Kennard, Dillon Brooks, Miles Bridges, and Peat on the floor, the Suns deployed a small ball lineup despite Denver having Marvin Bagley III out there. It was an effective blend of physicality, rebounding, defense, and perimeter shooting, and Phoenix put together a 13-3 run as a result. Peat was only out there for 1:16, but he was a +5.

Nikola Jokic returned, as did Oso Ighodaro, and the Joker went right back to abusing Phoenix down low. The starting unit eventually returned, with Luke Kennard in place of Dillon Brooks. And with them came the turnovers.

Jordan Ott opted to utilize both of his challenges within a three-second span. He was successful with the first, as the basketball went out of bounds off Nikola Jokic, but unsuccessful with the second, as the foul called on Collin Gillespie was upheld. So with 2:03 remaining in the second quarter and the score tied at 51–51, the Suns were already out of challenges.

It was a miserable shooting half for Devin Booker, who made only one field goal and finished with five points, courtesy of going 3-of-4 from the free-throw line. He did distribute the basketball effectively, recording seven assists, although he also committed two turnovers. But hey, he collected his 3,000th career rebound. There's something to celebrate.

Nikola Jokić led all scorers with 16 points at the half, shooting 7-of-14 from the field. Denver continued to make its living down low, with 38 of its 53 points coming in the paint. The Suns, however, held a 12-6 advantage in second-chance points and a commanding 28-9 advantage in bench scoring.

Perhaps the most encouraging statistic of the first half? The Suns were winning the battle on the glass, outrebounding the Nuggets 27-19. Given what we've witnessed through the first 12 games of the season, that's a refreshing development. Like a Cherry 7UP.

The Suns' bench matched the production of the starting five, with both units scoring 28 points in the first half. The difference was in how efficiently they got there. The starters shot 37% from the field and 14.3% from beyond the arc while committing eight turnovers. Meanwhile, the second unit shot a blistering 73.3% from the field, going 11-of-15, while connecting on 60% of its three-point attempts and committing only two turnovers.

Once again, the bench was responsible for keeping Phoenix afloat. And despite Devin Booker's rough shooting performance in the first half, the Suns entered halftime with a 56-53 advantage.

The Denver Nuggets, a team not particularly known for its perimeter shooting, came out of halftime and promptly knocked down three triples. Take Jamal Murray out of the equation, and this is a team that shoots 30% from beyond the arc.

Denver lost its challenge with 9:12 remaining in the third quarter after a foul called on Murray against Jalen Green was upheld. What made the decision more significant was that it was Murray's fourth personal foul, presumably why Nuggets head coach David Adelman elected to challenge the play.

Green followed it up with an impressive transition dunk that tied the game at 62. On Denver's ensuing possession, Green came away with another steal and drew a foul while aggressively attacking the basket.

And wouldn't you know it? Dillon Brooks picked up a technical foul after knocking down a three-pointer. His crime? Yelling at the opposing bench, something we've obviously never seen another NBA player do after hitting a three-pointer. But his reputation precedes him, and the officials assessed him his third technical foul of the season.

The starting five looked considerably scrappier to open the third quarter than we've grown accustomed to seeing this season. Through the first six minutes of the period, the unit was a +3, forcing five Denver turnovers and converting them into 11 points. There was a welcomed sense of defensive aggression from a group that has struggled to consistently establish it.

The Suns extended their lead to 10 points following Devin Booker's first three-pointer of the evening. Phoenix allowed Denver to get within five points before putting together a 9-0 run that helped extend its lead to 13. The Nuggets responded with a 5-0 run to close the quarter, trimming the deficit to single digits.

Overall, the Suns outscored the Nuggets 34-29 in the third quarter, including a 31-5 run, surrendering only six points in the paint after allowing 38 in the first half. Their defensive aggression continued to pay dividends as well, converting six Denver turnovers into 14 points.

The Suns carried a 90-82 advantage into the fourth quarter.

Phoenix opened the fourth quarter with a 7-0 run, prompting a David Adelman timeout as Phoenix extended its lead to a game-high 15 points.

With Nikola Jokic on the bench, you knew Jamal Murray was going to take it upon himself to get the Nuggets going offensively, and he scored all six of Denver's points through the first five minutes of the quarter. But the Suns' effort on the glass was magnificent early in the fourth.

Meanwhile, the Suns were playing collective basketball and relentlessly attacking the offensive glass. They secured six offensive rebounds through those first five minutes, generating six second-chance points. Those extra possessions were vital in preventing Murray from generating any significant momentum for Denver and allowing Phoenix to maintain control of the game.

The starting unit returned, and Denver promptly knocked down a couple of three-pointers. It also felt as though the whistle tightened up, as Jalen Green continued to aggressively attack the basket without being rewarded with trips to the free-throw line.

With four minutes remaining, Denver deployed the Hack-an-Oso strategy, and Jordan Ott was having none of it. He immediately pulled Oso Ighodaro from the game, opting to deploy a small-ball lineup with Miles Bridges at center and the Suns protecting a 14-point advantage.

Jamal Murray was feeling it, knocking down a three-pointer and following it up with a jumper. He then drew a foul on a three-point attempt, helping the Nuggets pull within eight points with 1:46 remaining. Ighodaro subsequently returned to the floor.

Devin Booker responded by burying a big 17-footer to push the lead back to 10, and that one felt like the dagger. Murray scored again, bringing his fourth-quarter scoring total to 18 points, but with 34 seconds remaining, it essentially became a free-throw shooting contest the rest of the way.

It's off to Sac Town for the Suns' first game against the Kings this season.

#2863. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2862. Steve Kerr: ‘Lineup combinations are going to change’

It was somewhat understandable — albeit, still a bitter pill to swallow — that the Golden State Warriors were shellacked by the league's best team in the San Antonio Spurs without Steph Curry being there to save the day. Scoring 14 points in the first quarter against a defense anchored by perhaps the best defender in the world in Victor Wembanyama doesn't need much explanation, especially when Curry — a proven hard counter to Wembanyama and his defensive abilities — is back in the Bay Area rehabbing a torn right oblique muscle.

However, scoring fewer than 14 points in the first quarter against the New Orleans Pelicans — heading into tonight, the fourth-worst defense in terms of points allowed per 100 possessions — is great cause for concern. The Warriors managed a paltry 11 points against the Pelicans in the first quarter, while giving up 35 in turn. It was a hole they never managed to climb out of, too insurmountable for even one of their patented fake comebacks to occur.

It's clear that without Curry — and to a certain extent, backup guard Brandon Williams — lineups have become wonky. Combinations that normally wouldn't see the light of day are being deployed. Such is the nature of a team that is without their best player, their second-best player, and a couple of key role players that connect everything together (not to mention, a center that virtually just up and left the team) — all combining for hundreds of millions of dollars in salary missing.

But that is the reality right now for the Warriors, who will have to find ways to tread water. One of those ways is finding combinations that do work — a task for Steve Kerr and his coaching staff to figure out.

The Warriors had perhaps their most winnable game of the road trip tonight — and did things that were far from winning basketball. The schedule isn't going to get any easier, with Dallas up next on Thursday, followed by Memphis the following night to complete a tough back-to-back slate. Three days after, they will thankfully return home — but against the Eastern Conference's best team in the Boston Celtics.

The NBA is a league without mercy — and the Warriors will just have to find ways to cope with that fact.

#2861. Which USMNT players should Mauricio Pochettino bring with him to Manchester United?

Mauricio Pochettino hasn't even taken his first training session as Manchester United's new manager, but transfer targets are already being lined up for January and beyond.

MORE — What/who next for USMNT? | USA 6-0 Haiti | Poch's tough task at Man United

And some of them are players he's worked with while in charge of the USMNT over the last two years.

Weston McKennie is one name already being mentioned as a possible buy for Man United, and the Juventus midfielder didn't exactly bat away the speculation.

MORE — Was Pochettino's time in charge of the USMNT a success?

Speaking to the media about the possibility of himself, and other USMNT players, being linked with a move to Old Trafford, here's what McKennie had to say.

"I think that, obviously, the time Pochettino spent here allowed him to appreciate this squad and see the quality it has to offer," McKennie said. "And yes, I think it's a possibility. We have a lot of guys on this team who will probably make a move soon or within the next year. So I think the links are somewhat inevitable. At the end of the day, though, you never know what's going through a manager's head or what kind of players he's looking for. Still, I think you could see a few of us linked with United, and maybe it will actually happen."

Given that most of the USMNT squad Pochettino built is young, and American players are generally commanding lower transfer fees compared to others, there are certainly some deals out there for United to make and it would make sense for them to make use of Pochettino's connection to the USMNT.

And contrary to previous years (and perhaps due to certain financial limitations) United's current hierarchy seem to be open to signing players who have a higher ceiling after some time developing rather than spending big money on the finished products.

There are four USMNT players who make sense right away for Man United to buy, but financially all of these players would be more difficult to sign:

Folarin Balogun - So close to signing for Everton in the summer, Monaco know Balogun will leave soon and his deal is up in 2028 which could see United get a bit of a deal. United really need a poacher up top and Balogun would bring something totally different to Sesko. He dazzled with his finishing and making runs in-behind for Poch.

Chris Richards - This is the next logical step for Richards. He's excelled at Crystal Palace and is an aggressive center back who loves to step out of the back on the ball. Still just 26 years old, Richards would start right away for United.

Antonee Robinson - Left back is obviously a huge problem area for Man United and Robinson, 29, has been one of the most consistent left backs for many years in the Premier League. He's out of contract at Fulham in 2028 so could be available for a much lower fee this summer.

Tyler Adams - United have struggled to replace Casemiro and even after signing Carlos Baleba and Andrey Santos, it's pretty clear they need more help in defensive midfield. Adams was recently named Pochettino's USMNT captain and his intensity is perfect for the way the new United boss wants to play. Adams is another who sees his current contract run out in 2028.

Then there are a couple of USMNT players Pochettino could probably persuade United to buy in the next few transfer windows without as big of a financial outlay:

Alex Freeman - After an incredible summer at the World Cup, his meteoric rise continues at Villarreal. Still just 22, Freeman is versatile at right back or center back and is exactly the kind of defender Pochettino loves. Reliable, strong and intense.

Sebastian Berhalter - He's now a nailed on starter for the USMNT and has been wonderful in his first few months at England in Middlesbrough. Wouldn't break the bank and would be a solid squad player at United and can fill a variety of roles in midfield and attack. His arrival would raise eyebrows, but it shouldn't.

Sergino Dest - Full backs are a problem for United and Dest became a favorite of Pochettino's at right wing-back. From an attacking sense he is superb and his deal at PSV is up soon. Once again, a bit of a bargain and a great squad player.

And then there are a trio of players who would be great long-term signings for United, and we know Pochettino is great at developing youngsters:

Cavan Sullivan - Obviously there is a deal lined up for him to head to Manchester City soon. But what about a late switch to the other side of Manchester? That would make him an instant United hero... The 17-year-old has been a sensation in recent months and Pochettino loves a young playmaker to build his projects around.

Julian Hall - The teenage forward has grabbed the opportunity Poch gave him with both hands and at 18 years of age he looks set to battle it out with Balogun to be the USMNT's starting center forward. May stay in the Red Bull system, but looks certain to be a star.

Justin Ellis - Another teenage forward, Ellis also grabbed his opportunity in recent months and the 19-year-old is destined for a move to Europe. A proper center forward who is raw and full of potential. Like Hall and Sullivan, all three have the potential to be the face of the USMNT together for the next 10-15 years.

#2860. Miami Dolphins’ Most Valuable Player | Week Ten | Youth

The Miami Dolphins have been going through a bit of a youth-movement this season, in case you haven't heard. They're the youngest team in the National Football League by a wide margin — with an average age of just over 25 years at the start of the season. They've also had the most snaps taken by rookie players out of any NFL team this season — leading the Cleveland Browns by a comfortable amount.

That emphasis on developing young players is paying off for the Dolphins in a major way. While the team is currently struggling in the standings — winning just one game on the year so far — their rookies are taking their lumps and learning the ropes at an expedited pace. That should help the team progress much quicker than most anticipate, while keeping their future cap situation in the good (and cheap) place.

One young player who has been excelling in Jeff Halfey's defensive scheme is rookie first-rounder Chris Johnson. Miami's young cornerback has two interceptions on the season after nabbing one against Daniel Jones of the Colts on Sunday. He returned that interception 59-yards for a touchdown — Miami's first pick-6 on the year. In addition to his two interceptions, Johnson also has a forced fumble during his rookie campaign.

Following the game, Johnson spoke about how coaching helped him make his stellar play on Sunday.

"Definitely felt good. Really just reading my keys. Once the quarterback scrambles, he's looking to get the ball out of his hands. That's kind of something we talked about throughout the week. When we get him off-schedule, it's an opportunity. Really just making the most of my opportunity in that situation."

#2858. Warriors had no ‘juice’ as pathetic Pelicans slaughter Golden State for second straight embarrassment

The absence of Stephen Curry is being felt across the Warriors roster.

On Monday night, the Warriors suffered their second consecutive blowout loss, dropping 115-94 to the lowly Pelicans, and Steve Kerr was well aware that his "starting lineup didn't seem to have a lot of juice."

"We're gonna have to change some combinations and find some things that work for us," Kerr said. "We got some good looks early and it was one of those nights where shots didn't go, and it felt like that really deflated us."

Steve Kerr says Warriors had no juice in Monday's loss to Pelicans. AP Photo/Darren Abate

The Warriors already found themselves staring at a gaping hole from the moment the game started, being outscored 35-11 by the end of the first quarter. New Orleans nearly doubled their lead by halftime as Golden State trailed 61-36 by the end of the first half.

Brandin Podziemski and rookie Yaxel Lendeborg were the only players in the Warriors' starting five who scored double-digit points, as LJ Cryer finished with seven while Draymond Green had two and Al Horford didn't log a single point.

Yaxel Lendeborg showing his frustrations during Monday's loss to the Pelicans. Getty Images

Near the end of the game, Lendeborg was seen sitting on Golden State's bench, looking dejected after putting together a performance where he did everything he could to help the team win, finishing with 28 points, eight rebounds and three assists.

"He fought his way back. The second half, he did a much better job attacking," Kerr said. "I thought the game, at the start of the third quarter, it felt like how it should've started."

Golden State scored 29 points in the third and fourth quarters, and Kerr said he felt like he saw the energy that the team was missing when the game started.

"The ball was moving. We were diving on the pick and roll, pulling the defense in," Kerr said. "First half, we were just on the perimeter, launching."

Kerr went on to say that as the team adjusts to playing without Curry, they will need to find some lineup creations that will work.

"I'm looking for a force of cutting. Playing with a sense of urgency. The connection that starts when the ball starts to move off of those cuts," Kerr said.

#2857. Lions' Aidan Hutchinson confirmed to have torn ACL, ending his 2026 season

Fears that Detroit Lions edge rusher Aidan Hutchinson suffered a torn ACL in his right knee were confirmed after additional exams, according to The Ringer's Tom Pelissero. Though tests reportedly revealed no other significant damage to Hutchinson's knee, he is expected to miss the remainder of the 2026 season with the injury.

On Tuesday, Lions head coach Dan Campbell confirmed that injury, saying "it stinks" and that Hutchinson will come out of it better on the other side.

Hutchinson injured his knee on the Lions' first defensive series versus the New England Patriots on Sunday in Munich. After sacking New England's Drake Maye, Detroit's fifth-year star jumped in celebration and landed awkwardly on his right leg. Hutchinson immediately went to the turf. As teammates and players approached, he gestured to the front of his right knee.

Campbell added Tuesday that he will not speak with Hutchinson about celebrating plays. Campbell said he didn't want to take emotion out of the game.

Hutchinson reportedly underwent an MRI after Detroit's 35-10 win that showed a torn ACL. Lions head coach Dan Campbell said the defensive end would undergo further testing when the team returned to the U.S. before making an official announcement.

"We'll know more when we get back in the States," head coach Dan Campbell said postgame, via the Detroit News' Nolan Bianchi. "Anytime you lose a player of his caliber, the type of teammate he is, he's a captain of ours, that doesn't make things easy."

Meanwhile, a fan captured video of Hutchinson in a wheelchair at the airport prior to the Lions traveling back to Michigan on Sunday.

Maye sustained an injury to his ankle on the same play and the Patriots fear the QB may have suffered a fracture that would end his season. The third-year star stayed in Germany for additional testing that could determine if he needs surgery, Patriots head coach Mike Vrabel confirmed during a radio interview Monday.

In nine games this season, Hutchinson recorded 6 sacks and 24 total tackles in addition to 1 pass defended and 1 forced fumble.

Hutchinson's torn ACL is the second serious, season-ending injury that he has sustained during his NFL career. During the 2024 season, he suffered a broken fibula and tibia in his left leg during a Week 6 win over the Dallas Cowboys. Hutchinson recovered to play in all 17 games last year, compiling 14.5 sacks.

The Lions' 2022 first-round draft pick (No. 2 overall) out of Michigan, Hutchinson has been named to two Pro Bowls and earned second-team All-Pro honors during his career. He's compiled 49 sacks in 65 games with 200 tackles and 5 interceptions.

Last year, Hutchinson signed a four-year, $180 million contract extension (including $141 million in guaranteed money). That extension kicks in next season following the fifth-year option the Lions picked up this year.

#2856. Patriots takeaways: Latest on Drake Maye, possibly adding another QB, team not responding and more

FOXBOROUGH - Various reports have suggested the Patriots fear Drake Maye may have suffered a fractured ankle, and could be lost for the season.

When asked to verify those reports, Patriots coach Mike Vrabel wouldn't make that leap during his noon time press conference with the media.

He reiterated that Maye stayed in Germany, was headed back, and there would be more testing done to determine the severity of the injury.

"I'm not going to make any comments about his status until he gets back here," Vrabel said. "He stayed with a few of our people, and they're en route back here. Once he gets back here and our people are able to visit with him again, and talk with him again, I think we'll have more to offer. But I wouldn't imagine him being available when we come out of the bye."

RELATED: Patriots top futures markets heading into bye week: Does the loss of Drake Maye tank the season?

Maye was injured during the first series of the 35-10 loss to the Detroit Lions in Germany.

He suffered an ankle injury after being taken down by Aidan Hutchinson. Maye limped to the sideline, eventually went to the locker room, and didn't return to the game.

When asked, Vrabel indicated the team wasn't in the process of exploring other quarterback options in wake of Maye's injury.

NFL insider Jordan Schultz tweeted that the Patriots were expected to look at free agents such as Jimmy Garoppolo, who has previously played for offensive coordinator Josh McDaniels, or potentially add a quarterback from another team's practice squad.

Vrabel indicated Tommy DeVito would be the starter following the bye, and rookie Behren Morton the back up, but didn't acknowledge kicking the tires at this stage to add another quarterback to the mix.

"I don't see that being the case right now," Vrabel said when asked if he anticipated looking for quarterback help in wake of Maye missing time. "Well, you know, I mean, we'll have conversations and we'll discuss it and we'll look and see, but I mean, Tommy and Behren have been in our system, have been working. Behren's been improving. He's been working each week and, and I know that if he has to play for us, that he'll be ready to go.

"And I'm, I'm excited for, for his challenge and I hope that everybody else is because, because that's really what we're at. We're 5 and 5 in the middle of the season, just past the middle of the season."

Following Sunday's loss, Tommy DeVito, who took over for Maye and performed poorly, was asked his confidence level if he needed to step in and be the starter going forward.

"I'm going to continue the process I have been doing up to this point. Something that as a two, as cliche as it sounds, you have to be prepared to be a starter," DeVito said. "So just keep doing what I'm doing, keep my head down, and then if something like that were to happen, you would have the reps and practice and the walkthroughs and everything going forward, so just keep on preparing and on to the next."

DeVito, who finished 12 of 24 for 81 yards, with one TD and one pick, said it felt like like the Lions "bullied" the offense.

"That's just kind of just how I felt. We never got to establish who we were. Kind of felt like we were playing on their terms, and I think that's just not our style of football. That's not our offense. That's not how we want to play. That's not what we preach," DeVito said. "So it starts with the quarterback, so it starts with me. I just have to play better and sustain drives and get the thing going and stop feeling so lax."

While Vrabel said DeVito's performance against the Lions wasn't "good enough" the Patriots coach expressed confidence in his backup.

"I think there were some things that he missed, but also, you know, not having a ton of live action since training camp, and really, I think he'll be ready to go coming out of the bye," Vrabel said. "I think that he'll be able to look at some things and ... get us in the right play or get the ball out quicker or look at protections or rotation, whatever that may be, I think that'll all improve. I have a lot of confidence in Tommy.

"I know, his toughness is going to be through the charts and his competitiveness will be exactly what we need."

Whatever messages Vrabel has been sending, the players haven't responded, especially during the past two games, which were blowouts.

He acknowledged the Lions "outplayed and out-coached" us and "outplayed" the Patriots during Sunday's loss.

Vrabel was asked how difficult it's been to draw consistent effort out of his team, and how frustrated he might be that problems persist.

"A lot of things lead to the performance ... the turnovers, the split-second decisions, the critical mistakes, the missed tackles, the drops," Vrabel said Monday. "I mean, there's all a bunch of stuff, it all leads to being inconsistent and so we need to have consistent actions.

"Again, we talked about preparation, how we prepare is great, and I thought we had a good week, but the only thing that matters is how we perform and how we play on Sunday.

"So we'll still have to continue to prepare and we'll just have to find ways to play better."

Vrabel pretty much admitted the team hasn't responded well to adversity during games.

"We have to mentally be strong enough to withstand them making a play," Vrabel said. "(The Lions) are talented. They're going to make some plays and so when they make a play, it's our response that's critical. We can't let one play affect the next one."

Bottom line: Vrabel said he needs to coach better, and the team needs to play better.

"Hopefully we have some sort of a re-set here with the bye," Vrabel said. "Being 5-and-5, there's a lot of teams that go in different directions at this point in the season."

#2855. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2854. South Korea will suspend all aircraft operation for 35 minutes and markets will open late. Here’s why

South Korea will suspend all aircraft takeoffs and landings nationwide for 35 minutes on Thursday to minimize noise during the English listening portion of the country's highly competitive college entrance exam, the government said Tuesday.

The restrictions will be in place from 1:05 p.m. to 1:40 p.m. local time, affecting 130 flights, including 81 international and 49 domestic services, according to the Ministry of Land, Infrastructure and Transport.

Planes already in the air will be required to remain at least 3 kilometers above the ground during the exam, while emergency flights will be exempt from the restrictions.

The measures will also apply to helicopters, light aircraft and drones, with all drone flights prohibited nationwide during the 35 minutes. Violators could face criminal penalties, the ministry said.

The government will adjust the departure and arrival times of affected flights, while airlines will notify passengers of the revised schedules in advance.

The Korea Transportation Safety Authority will also send text messages to licensed drone operators, including those who have already received flight approvals, warning that drone flights will be prohibited during period regardless of prior authorization.

"We will thoroughly implement the necessary measures, including restrictions on aircraft operations, to ensure students can concentrate ton the English listening test in a table environment," said Jeong Chae-gyo, director general of aviation policy at the ministry.

Jeong also urged airlines and relevant authorities to cooperate in adjusting flight schedules and notifying passengers in advance, and to advise travelers to check their revised departure and arrival times with their airlines.

South Korea's college entrance exam, known as Suneung, is a high stakes annual test that plays a major role in determining students' university admissions.

The country's stock market will also open an hour later than usual on Thursday, at 10 a.m. local time, and close at 4:30 pm, as announced by the Korea Exchange.
