#2853. Rockets flounder in Miami 128-110

Sometimes a team has the energy to take a (private, comfortable) flight after a game, and play another game the next day, and play very well. After a big win in Washington, where pretty much everything from the early third quarter onward was garbage time, one might think the team would be more rested ready to compete in its next game. That's a great plan, and morally sound, but probably easier said than done if that post game flight is to Miami. The Rockets announcers called it a long flight, but it really isn't that long, a bit over two hours. Still, given all the gear, and to-ing and fro-ing you'd expect the Rockets to hit their likely downtown, luxury hotel, after midnight. You may be old, and I may be old, but the Rockets? They're not old. They might want to enjoy some nightlife in Miami.

Could they? Yes, absolutely. Why? Because although last call is typically 2AM in the Miami area, venues in the "Entertainment District" can serve until 5am. Bars and restaurants with a seating capacity under 100 people? They can stay open until 5am. And in certain areas liquor sales go on 24/7. Add in the fact that while you and I might not have access to posh, or wild after parties in Miami, (I can't speak for you) very wealthy young basketball stars probably do. The Rockets might discover a lot more entertainment, amusement and diversion on offer late night in Miami than you or I might find. Again, I can't speak for you.

This is possibly why the Rockets were flat, like a fish, a flat fish, a flounder. And flounder they did. At no point did this game look like a win for the Rockets. It started badly, with Miami shooting 3s like the peak Golden State Warrios, and that great shooting did not not only include a less-washed looking Klay Thompson (Heat Culture Injection!) and Andrew Wiggins, but everyone else, too. It didn't help that Miami came out playing like the U of Miami Hurricanes football team, and this was fine, apparently. They love the Hurricanes down there. Of the football variety, anyway.

The Rockets looked slower, less forceful, and generally not ready to do much. The, unique in the NBA Noah Laroche "No Screen" system, seemed to baffle the Rockets, and their switching and produced about 900 open corner 3pt shots for Miami. The novelty of a system they're not likely to see more than once was too much for the Rockets and they were breaded and fried like fish for 128 points tonight. Whereas Miami's coach, Erik Spoelstra just played the hits from last season, and packed the paint, and dared the Rockets to score inside while being, ah, "vigorously defended and possibly knifed" or make outside shots.

Make outside shots, they did not. The Rockets shot 28% from three, on fairly low volume as well. Miami put up 50 3pt shots, and made 18. That's a stark difference from the Rockets 36 3pt attempts. Why would you want to put up more 3s if you're not making them at a good rate? Because the 2pt shots weren't falling either. At 41% on twos, it's better to shoot more threes.

Would more energy have won this one? I'm not sure. They Rockets typically lose at least one game a season to Miami. The Heat bet on tired legs shooting from range, and swarming defense in close. It wasn't some sort of utter shut down, the Rockets scored about NBA average at 110, but the flat, floundering, start for 15pts in the first quarter couldn't be overcome. A better shooting Rockets team might have won this, as they had a 21 to 13 offensive rebound advantage, but if you don't cash those boards with makes it can look like this game. Maybe, though, more energy would have helped. They deep bench, particularly Isaiah Crawford looked decent in their minutes.

Another point to consider is that the Rockets truly do give up a fair number of games available by their better players by not playing various players on a back to back. Adams might have helped control the paint. It turned out he wasn't really needed against the Tall But Bad Wizards. It wasn't a bad decision to play him in DC, but when you're guessing about FVV and Adams (if available) on every back to back, you're going to roll snakeyes sometimes.

Of concern also, Amen Thompson sat out a game in a return to his native land of South Florida with left knee tendonitis. He seemed to land badly on a play in DC, and the seems to be result of that, despite him playing on. Hopefully this is nothing long lasting, or serious, and more of a precaution.

This might have been a better outcome if the Rockets started in Miami and went to The District, but we'll never know. Best to forget it and move on.

#2852. Florida to host 5-star OL Maxwell Hiller ahead of early signing period

With Florida football set to host its final home game of the 2026 season on Saturday, the Gators will have one last opportunity to welcome members of their 2027 recruiting class to Gainesville before the early signing period begins in December.

The matchup also presents a crucial opportunity for Florida's coaching staff to strengthen relationships with its commitments and make a final impression on top recruiting targets before the signing window opens.

Among the recruits expected to return to Gainesville is five-star interior offensive lineman Maxwell Hiller, who will visit Florida again ahead of the early signing period. Despite the upcoming trip, Hiller has indicated that he has already seen everything he needs from the Gators and remains confident in his decision as signing day approaches.

"It's so close to the point like I just I'm not looking to see anything else," Hiller told Swamp247. "I really just want to be around the team."

The 6-foot-5-inch, 300-pound offensive lineman out of Coatesville High School in Pennsylvania has not visited Gainesville since Florida's season opener against Florida Atlantic in September.

Hiller initially planned to return for the Gators' matchup against Oklahoma but had to cancel because of SAT testing obligations. Now, the five-star prospect is set to make his first trip back to the Swamp in more than two months, accompanied by his high school offensive line coach for what could be a significant final visit before officially signing with Florida.

"Ever since my freshman year, he's trusted me and stuff like that with playing for him and his offensive line, which is flattering, him being such a good coach. He's taught me so much stuff from on the field to off the field, life lessons, stuff like that," Hiller stated.

Although Hiller has followed much of Florida's 2026 campaign from a distance, the five-star prospect has kept a close eye on the Gators throughout the season, most recently being their upset victory over the Kentucky Wildcats.

"I mean huge win upset, on the road versus Kentucky. I mean, I saw it was alright game. Definitely some things that can be cleaned up," Hiller said. "But yeah, overall, just a good game, good win on the road."

Despite the encouraging moments, Hiller has also recognized Florida's struggles throughout the season, particularly along the offensive line, which faced its share of challenges during the Gators' victory over Kentucky. However, those inconsistencies haven't discouraged the five-star prospect, who intends to arrive in Gainesville ready to compete for immediate playing time.

"I'm gunning for a spot right as I get there. That's what I want to do. I want to play as early as possible and prove myself to myself, I don't even want to prove to anybody else, I want to prove myself that I can compete on that level and dominate on that level at such a young age," Hiller said. "When I come in I'm not, I'm not holding back at all. I'm just, I'm gunning for every spot I can take."

Hiller is ranked No. 3 overall and No. 1 at his position nationally according to the 247Sports composite, while the Rivals industry ranking has him at Nos. 4 and 1, respectively; ESPN ranks Hiller Nos. 3 and 1 as well.

He committed to Florida in April of 2026, and has stated he does not plan to reopen his recruitment; however, Penn State, Maryland, and Tennessee, among others, remain in pursuit of the blue-chip prospect.

Will that be enough to keep him in New York? Maybe. If the Giants can find the money.

"It's really hard to pay three different dudes [at] essentially the same position the same type of money," Schultz said. "The Giants are not going to be able to pay three dudes $25-30 million dollars [at] essentially the same spot."

#2850. St. Louis Fed's Musalem says four more rate hikes may be needed in coming months

St. Louis Federal Reserve President Alberto Musalem indicated Tuesday that the central bank may need as many as four rate hikes in coming months, offering one of the most hawkish views yet by a Fed policymaker about the steps needed to quell inflation.

During a speech delivered in Argentina, Musalem made the case that current policy is not significantly restricting economic activity.

"The economy will likely require several interest rate increases before the spring of 2027 to achieve 2% inflation in a timely manner," he said.

Musalem said the real policy rate, which subtracts the inflation rate from the Fed's target rate, needs to be between 2%-2.5% by next spring, and estimated that the current rate is about a full point below that range. That position suggests the Fed may need to hike at each of the upcoming Fed meetings in December, January, March and April.

Musalem does not get a vote on the rate-setting Federal Open Market Committee over the period where he says that the rate increases will be needed, though he does get to present views and provide input on the "dot plot" of individual members' expectations. He will next vote in 2028.

To be sure, Musalem noted "an unlikely but still plausible scenario" in which no further hikes would be needed.

"Falling energy prices and lower shipping costs, or an increase in potential output growth driven by higher productivity growth, might ease inflation pressures such that inflation moves toward target in 18 months," he said. Another possibility is that demand might decelerate as spending on artificial intelligence slows.

However, he said the more likely scenario is that "persistent strong demand and ongoing supply pressures" combine to keep inflation above the Fed's 2% target, thus requiring further increases in the fed funds rate from its current range of 3.75%-4%.

"It would be preferable for a higher real policy rate to happen in a balanced way, driven both by lower inflation and a higher nominal policy rate," he said. "However, the nominal policy rate may need to do most of the work."

Jan 28, 2026; Salt Lake City, Utah, USA; Utah Jazz forward Lauri Markkanen (23) reacts after a play against the Golden State Warriors during the second half at Delta Center. Mandatory Credit: Rob Gray-Imagn Images

Utah has had a rough stretch after starting the season 1-1, losing 12 straight games. Injuries haven't helped, with Lauri Markkanen, Jaren Jackson Jr. and Jusuf Nurkic all sidelined. Those are three key contributors, and without them, it's hard to expect much winning from this group. Rookie Darryn Peterson and Keyonte George have looked good, though, providing a few bright spots.

Milwaukee continues to struggle, losing 10 straight games and falling toward the bottom of the standings. Jaime Jaquez Jr. and Tyler Herro have been two of the team's few bright spots, providing some much-needed scoring. The bigger concern is the development of its rookies. Nate Ament and Brayden Burries have both been inconsistent and haven't provided reliable minutes, even with plenty of opportunities on a struggling roster.

Sacramento is pretty much where most expected it to be, but its lack of offensive options has become a concern. Darius Acuff has been forced to take more than 20 shots per game, which could lead to some poor shooting habits as he continues to develop. The Kings have also lost six straight, and without more scoring help around Acuff, it's difficult to see this team turning things around anytime soon.

It's the same conversation in Brooklyn as in previous weeks. Julius Randle and Michael Porter Jr. have put up impressive individual numbers, but the rest of the roster hasn't contributed much. Mikel Brown Jr. has provided some additional scoring, although he's been inconsistent. The Nets have now lost two straight and still look like a team without enough reliable pieces to compete.

Trae Young has had a shaky start to the season, looking like an All-Star in some games while disappearing in others. That inconsistency has made it difficult for Washington to find any offensive rhythm, contributing to its eight-game losing streak. There have been some positives, though. Rookie AJ Dybantsa has looked every bit like a first overall pick, while Anthony Davis has been a reliable presence.

Chicago is finally showing some signs of life after a miserable start, going 2-1 over its last three games following a 1-9 opening stretch. Norman Powell has led the way offensively, but the biggest loss has been Caleb Wilson, who is sidelined with a fractured hand. Wilson had been the team's biggest bright spot, and getting him back will be important if Chicago wants to continue building momentum.

Charlotte hasn't been able to find much consistency, going just 2-8 over its last 10 games. Coby White hasn't come anywhere close to living up to his contract, leaving the Hornets without the production they expected from him. Rookie Kon Knueppel has also yet to play meaningful minutes this season and is now sidelined with a hamstring strain. It's been a disappointing start for a team that needs its young players to step up.

Toronto Raptors forward Kawhi Leonard (2) Credit: Brad Mills-Imagn Images

The absence of Kawhi Leonard has really hurt Toronto, which has now lost four straight games. Immanuel Quickley has struggled toward the end of games, particularly with his playmaking and decision-making when the Raptors need him most. R.J. Barrett and Scottie Barnes have been two of the team's brighter spots, but Toronto needs more consistent production from its backcourt to get back on track.

Indiana is still waiting for Tyrese Haliburton to return, as he is expected to remain sidelined through Nov. 21. Pascal Siakam has done a good job leading the offense, while Andrew Nembhard has stepped up and taken on a larger role in Haliburton's absence. The Pacers are 3-7 over their last 10 games, with one of those wins coming against the Knicks in the NBA Cup. They've managed to stay competitive at times, but getting their All-Star point guard back should make a major difference.

New Orleans has looked downright terrible at times, despite having a roster with plenty of talent. Dejounte Murray has also requested a trade, adding more drama to an already disappointing season. The Pelicans have actually been worse with him on the floor, making his situation an interesting one to monitor. There are some positives from the younger players, though. Second-year player Jeremiah Fears recently recorded his first triple-double, while he and Derik Queen have looked like great draft picks. Zion Williamson has also been good.

The Clippers are starting to show some signs of life after taking down Oklahoma City in an NBA Cup game and winning two straight. Darius Garland, Brook Lopez and Rui Hachimura have led the way, providing reliable and efficient scoring. Garland has been particularly impressive, showcasing his playmaking ability in addition to his scoring. Los Angeles still has plenty of ground to make up, but its recent performances have been encouraging.

Cooper Flagg and Kyrie Irving have continued to look good individually, but figuring out how to play together remains important for Dallas. The two are still developing their chemistry, and the Mavericks have dropped three straight games. There's plenty of talent on this roster, but it needs to translate into more consistent results.

Phoenix has really turned its season around after a poor start, climbing back to 6-7. Devin Booker, Dillon Brooks and Jalen Green have all stepped up, giving the Suns a much more reliable offensive attack. Miles Bridges has also been a key contributor and is looking like a great offseason addition. This team is starting to find its identity and could become a much bigger threat as the season progresses.

Franz Wagner has looked like the star of Orlando's roster, helping lead the team through a difficult start. After opening the season 2-7, the Magic have won four straight games and are finally starting to find their rhythm. Paolo Banchero remains sidelined with an injury, making this turnaround even more impressive. Once Banchero returns, Orlando should have an even better chance to climb the standings.

Nov 9, 2026; San Francisco, California, USA; Golden State Warriors guard Stephen Curry (30) reacts after scoring a three point basket against the New Orleans Pelicans during the third quarter at Chase Center. Mandatory Credit: Kelley L Cox-Imagn Images Credit: IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect (Reuters)

Stephen Curry's absence has exposed just how much Golden State relies on its superstar. Curry is expected to miss at least a month with an oblique injury, and the Warriors have lost three straight games since he's been out. Yaxel Lendeborg has been carrying much of the load, but replacing Curry's production is nearly impossible. Golden State just needs to stay afloat until he returns.

Donovan Mitchell has been vital to Cleveland's success, playing excellent basketball throughout the season. James Harden has also gotten better as the season has progressed, gradually shaking off the offseason rust. Evan Mobley has continued to look like the All-Star center he's proven himself to be, while second-round pick Meleek Thomas has looked like the steal many expected him to be. Cleveland has the talent to compete, but it needs to put together more consistent performances.

Nikola Jokic and Jamal Murray have been forced to do just about everything for Denver, combining for 50 points per game. Jokic also leads the team in both rebounds and assists, showing just how much of the offense runs through him. The problem is that nobody else has emerged as a notable scoring option. The Nuggets didn't add much during the offseason and that lack of reinforcements is starting to show.

Jalen Johnson has arguably been Atlanta's best player, leading the team in rebounds and assists while averaging close to 20 points per game. Nickeil Alexander-Walker has also been impressive, leading the Hawks in scoring with 21.4 points per game. Despite their individual performances, I'm not completely sold on this team. Their most notable win has been a three-point victory against Cleveland at home, while most of their other wins have come against bottom-feeding teams. Atlanta could be a potential fraud if it can't start beating better competition.

The Knicks haven't looked like the championship team many expected them to be entering the season. Losing an NBA Cup game to an Indiana team without Tyrese Haliburton certainly doesn't help their case. Mikal Bridges has looked like a shell of himself, while Jalen Brunson, Karl-Anthony Towns, OG Anunoby and Josh Hart have all played well. There's plenty of talent in New York, but the team hasn't put everything together yet.

Deni Avdija and Damian Lillard have Portland looking like one of the top teams in the league, giving the Trail Blazers a strong offensive foundation. Ja Morant has also looked like a great pickup, especially considering how little Portland gave up to acquire him. He's been a key contributor and has added another dimension to the offense. The biggest problem for Portland is that it's competing in a loaded Western Conference.

Mar 1, 2026; Dallas, Texas, USA; Oklahoma City Thunder guard Shai Gilgeous-Alexander (2) looks to move the ball past Dallas Mavericks guard AJ Johnson (8) during the game between the Mavericks and the Thunder at American Airlines Center. Mandatory Credit: Jerome Miron-Imagn Images

The injuries to Jalen Williams and Chet Holmgren are really starting to hurt Oklahoma City. Shai Gilgeous-Alexander looks drained, carrying most of the scoring and playmaking responsibilities. Jared McCain and Ajay Mitchell have both been great, but their contributions haven't been enough to make up for the missing production. I still expect the Thunder to bounce back, but they need reinforcements in a bad way.

Miami has been one of the hottest teams in the league, winning eight of its last 10 games and putting together a five-game winning streak. Davion Mitchell has provided reliable playmaking for Bam Adebayo and Giannis Antetokounmpo, while the team's chemistry is finally starting to click. After a disappointing start, the Heat are beginning to look like the team everyone expected them to be.

Cade Cunningham has continued to look like one of the best point guards in the NBA, leading Detroit's offense and creating opportunities for his teammates. The Pistons have looked like one of the top teams in the league at times, although they've also had some shaky performances. Jalen Duren is getting closer to returning after resuming on-court activities, and his presence should give Detroit a significant boost. I expect this team to climb once he's back.

Just when you think Memphis is coming back down to earth, the Grizzlies respond with another winning streak. They've won three straight and continue to look like a legitimate playoff team. Cameron Boozer has been making a strong case for Rookie of the Year, while Ty Jerome has arguably been the biggest surprise of the NBA season. He's looked like a legitimate top scorer in the league, giving Memphis an offensive weapon few expected. I'm still not sure how sustainable this is, but the Grizzlies have certainly earned their spot.

With Anthony Edwards sidelined, there were plenty of questions about whether Minnesota could continue winning games. LaMelo Ball has answered those questions, leading the way in scoring and setting up his teammates. He's been excellent, doing a lot to quiet the narrative that he isn't a winning player. Minnesota's role players have also stepped up, giving this team a chance to remain competitive until Edwards returns. The sky's the limit for this group.

Houston Rockets forward Kevin Durant Mandatory Credit: Jerome Miron-Imagn Images

The return of Fred VanVleet has made a massive difference for Houston, providing the team with a reliable ball handler and changing the way its offense operates. Jabari Smith Jr., Alperen Sengun and Amen Thompson have all taken huge leaps, while Kevin Durant continues to be the efficient scorer he's always been, simply taking fewer shots. Houston looks like a completely different team and has established itself as a legitimate contender.

Boston has looked impressive despite still having a significant hole at center. Jayson Tatum has returned to his superstar form, while Paul George has experienced something of a career revival. Payton Pritchard and Derrick White also remain two of the best supporting pieces in the NBA, providing reliable production and depth. The Celtics have the talent to compete with anyone, but their lack of a reliable center could become an issue.

Philadelphia has looked like the best team in the Eastern Conference, winning three straight games and climbing to 12-3. LeBron James has embraced whatever role the team needs, focusing on playmaking and rebounding while still contributing offensively. Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown have carried much of the scoring load and have been impressive all season. This looks like the most complete team in the East.

Luka Doncic has been absolutely unreal, putting together an MVP-caliber start and looking like the early favorite for the award. The Lakers have won eight straight games and have established themselves as one of the league's top teams. Austin Reaves and Walker Kessler have also been two of the team's biggest contributors outside of Doncic, making their recent injuries something to monitor. Both missed the last game, and their availability will be important to Los Angeles' continued success.

San Antonio has been nearly unstoppable, winning 12 straight games after dropping its first two contests. Victor Wembanyama has catapulted himself into the early MVP conversation, while Dylan Harper, Stephon Castle, Devin Vassell, and De'Aaron Fox have all been reliable scorers. What's most impressive is how comfortable the younger players look. Despite being early in their NBA careers, they play with the confidence and composure of players who've been in the league for a decade. The Spurs have earned the top spot.

#2847. Detroit Lions plan to activate, play Kerby Joseph this week vs. Buccaneers

Detroit Lions safety Kerby Joseph has not played in a football game in 401 days, but it appears that counter will drop to zero this upcoming weekend.

On his weekly radio appearance on 97.1 The Ticket, Lions coach Dan Campbell said the expectations—barring any setback—is for Joseph to be activated from the Physically Unable to Perform list this week and play in Sunday's game against the Tampa Bay Buccaneers.

"Barring anything else here negatively—which there hasn't been, he's progressed nicely here—the plan would be to get him going this week. Get him going and get him into Tampa Bay and let him play," Campbell said.

Joseph has been dealing with a knee injury that limited his play early in 2025 and was eventually painful enough to cause him to miss the final 11 games of the season. Since then, Joseph has been working tirelessly to build enough strength and stability in his knee for the Lions to be comfortable enough to put him back on the field. The Lions returned him to practice three weeks ago, and they've been encouraged by his progress.

"He looked a lot better than what I anticipated him looking (like)," Lions safety coach Jim O'Neil said a couple weeks ago. "So it was very encouraging. So now he's gotta stack multiple days. He's got to stack them, so today will be a big day for him, but he looked awesome yesterday at practice."

It's unclear how much of a role Joseph will have upon his return, as the Lions have a pretty deep bench at safety. Not only did Brian Branch return to the lineup a few weeks ago, but veteran Chuck Clark has started to play better, and Detroit did just trade for former Jets safety Andre Cisco—who saw the field on defense last week in Germany. Regardless, it sounds like Detroit will have their All-Pro safety back in some capacity.

On top of providing an update on Joseph, Campbell noted that center Cade Mays (ankle) is unlikely to play this week, but he's still very encouraged by how backup Juice Scruggs is playing.

"I don't see Cade being ready this week. There's a chance, but I'm not putting all my eggs in that basket," Campbell said. "And Juice has been doing a nice job for us. He's playing really good football. That whole o-line played dang good football the other day."

#2846. U.S. agencies say they initiated investigation into Nvidia partner Megaspeed in 2024

The U.S. Commerce and Justice Department on Monday stateside said they had initiated an investigation into Singapore-based company Megaspeed in 2024 to "address inaccurate information circulating in public reporting."

Megaspeed International, a cloud computing firm that operates data centers across Southeast Asia, has been drawn into the fallout surrounding the leadership shake-up at chip giant Nvidia.

In a joint release, the departments outlined a timeline of their investigations into Megaspeed, saying the probes began in 2024 over potential export-control violations.

In June 2025, Nvidia placed Megaspeed on its "no sell" list, after Commerce Department investigators requested records from Nvidia concerning direct or indirect sales to the company.

In the same month, U.S. officials and Singapore police conducted an in-person inspection of Megaspeed and interviewed Alice Huang, who was allegedly involved in a relationship with Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang.

"Following this enforcement action, the Department of Justice took the lead role in the criminal investigation of Megaspeed," the statement said.

On Wednesday, Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick also reposted the statement, adding, "The Department of Commerce works to protect the U.S. economy and our critical industries every day. We are very proud of the work of the Bureau of Industry and Security."

In response to the statement, Chris McGuire, senior fellow for China and emerging technologies at the Council of Foreign Relations wrote on X, "Very, very strange statement tonight... about Megaspeed. The facts here just don't add up, and this does nothing to explain why Commerce did not cut Megaspeed off from chips."McGuire said that Megaspeed kept buying chips after Nvidia put them on their no-trade list, and asked if Nvidia also blacklisted Megaspeed's subsidaries Speedmatrix and PT Megaspeed International. "I would bet they did not! If they kept shipping to them after blacklisting Megaspeed, that would be very concerning," McGuire added.

The statement comes after Singapore's Ministry of Home Affairs said the first time authorities there learned of U.S. interest in Megaspeed and Alice Huang was in June. Singapore had already been conducting its own independent investigation and said she was not investigated for espionage.

The city-state barred Alice Huang from re-entering the country in February 2026 after authorities found that she had knowingly provided inaccurate information about her role at Megaspeed.

Alice Huang was reportedly recruited by China's Ministry of State Security to gather information on Jensen Huang. The Justice Department has not reached a conclusion on whether she was a Chinese spy.

Jensen Huang resigned as CEO last week after revelations about multiple extramarital relationships, including with two Nvidia employees. The tech giant has appointed Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress as interim CEO.

The agencies' statement also comes as Nvidia prepares to report its third-quarter results Tuesday afternoon U.S. time, with Kress set to lead her first earnings call.

Earlier on Monday in the U.S., the Commerce Department moved to close a loophole that has allowed Chinese companies to access Nvidia's advanced computing power through data centers in third countries.

The interim final rule from the department's Bureau of Industry and Security is aimed at restricting remote access to advanced U.S. computing technology.

It also comes amid growing scrutiny of Lutnick for not having done enough to prevent restricted Nvidia chips and computing capacity from reaching Chinese companies.

— CNBC's Anniek Bao and Dan Mangan contributed to this report.

#2845. Warriors vs. Pelicans player grades: Yaxel Lendeborg leads the way in a rough game

Well, that was a dud. The Golden State Warriors have looked so impressive when Steph Curry is off the court this year … but only in the games where he starts on the court. In their second game since Curry's abdominal injury, the Warriors once again looked completely lost … and once again needed more than six minutes to score their first field goal.

The end result was a 115-94 loss to the New Orleans Pelicans but, if we're being honest, the game wasn't nearly that close. So let's run through the grades, because nobody wants to spend much time on this one. As always, grades are based on my expectations for each player, with a "B" grade representing the average performance for that player.

Note: True-shooting percentage (TS) is a scoring efficiency metric that accounts for threes and free throws. Entering Monday's games, league-average TS was 57.4%.

33 minutes, 28 points, 8 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 steal, 2 blocks, 4 turnovers, 2 fouls, 9-for-23 shooting, 2-for-9 threes, 8-for-8 free throws, 52.8% TS, -30

Lendeborg had the worst game of his young career on Friday, in his first taste of being The Man at the NBA level. He responded by calling himself out and cutting his hair.

For a while it seemed like this game might be a similar outcome, but as time wore on, he really started to figure things out. Lendeborg, like all of his teammates, couldn't find the bottom of the hoop in the first half, but he poured in 20 second-half points, including 10 in the first few minutes of the third quarter as he almost single-handedly tried (albeit unsuccessfully) to get the Dubs back in it.

The resilience was noteworthy. As was the defense which, while far from perfect, featured some really impressive hands. Lendeborg has a bit of Andre Iguodala's ability to swipe at the ball while moving backwards and still avoid fouling, and it's very impressive. It disrupted Zion Williamson on a few occasions.

There's still a lot of work to do if he wants to lead the team to victory in Curry's absence, but he showed some signs in this one.

Grade: B–Post-game bonus: Led the team in points; tied for the team lead in rebounds; worst plus/minus on the team.

25 minutes, 2 points, 8 rebounds, 5 assists, 1 steal, 1 block, 4 fouls, 1-for-3 shooting, 0-for-1 threes, 33.3% TS, -13

Just kind of a whatever game for Green. He didn't really do much on either side of the ball to put the Warriors in decent position, but also didn't do anything to hurt them. His defense was okay, but not as good as it often is. He made some nice passes and didn't turn the ball over, but wasn't really able to create any offense of his own. Not bad, all things considered; just not noteworthy, either.

Grade: B-Post-game bonus: Led the team in assists; tied for the team lead in rebounds.

14 minutes, 0 points, 5 rebounds, 3 blocks, 0-for-4 shooting, 0-for-2 threes, 0.0% TS, -17

Not a great matchup for Horford, and it didn't help that his shot wasn't falling, either. But he did have some tremendous plays on defense, which included a highlight-reel block of Williamson in the opening minutes. Three blocks in 14 minutes is pretty noteworthy!

Still, while he was patrolling the paint on some possessions, it did feel like New Orleans was just too fast and athletic for him in this game, which is why he was replaced by Charles Bassey in the starting lineup for the second half.

30 minutes, 15 points, 6 rebounds, 3 assists, 1 steal, 1 block, 5 turnovers, 2 fouls, 3-for-8 shooting, 3-for-7 threes, 6-for-7 free throws, 67.7% TS, +1

Podziemski had some struggles in this game, though it's worth highlighting just how strong his offensive efficiency was. Still, against the length, speed, and athleticism of the Pelicans, Podz had a difficult time penetrating the defense, and took just one shot inside the arc all night long (though he did draw some fouls). Podziemski has a nice jumper, and his three ball has been good this year, but his game works best when it's working inside out, rather than outside in. The lefty is at his best when he's penetrating the defense, getting looks in the lane, and then parlaying that threat into outside looks, and that wasn't the case today.

The bigger issue, though, is that with Curry out, the Warriors need Podziemski to function as a facilitator of sorts, and it's hard to do that when you turn the ball over five times. He's usually very steady with the rock, but was a bit of a liability in this game when dribbling and playmaking. And it was not his best defensive effort either, I thought.

But you do have to love 15 points on 67.7% true shooting…

24 minutes, 7 points, 1 rebound, 3 assists, 1 turnover, 1 foul, 2-for-10 shooting, 1-for-8 threes, 2-for-2 free throws, 32.2% TS, -10

You can certainly understand the idea behind putting Cryer in the starting lineup, so the Warriors could have an extra dose of shooting in Curry's absence, especially with how well Cryer played last week. But it's hard to have an impact when the primary thing you bring to the table is shooting threes, and you go 1-for-8 from deep.

Cryer showed some of the things he can do to help open up this offense with Curry sidelined, but ultimately it was a very tough game for him.

14 minutes, 0 points, 4 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 turnover, 4 fouls, 0-for-5 shooting, 0-for-4 threes, 0.0% TS, -16

I've said it before and I'll say it again: Niang is the type of player who can look really good when the team is playing well, but is unlikely to look good when they're playing poorly. He can elevate a team from good to great much more easily than from bad to good.

16 minutes, 4 points, 1 block, 2 fouls, 1-for-2 shooting, 2-for-2 free throws, 69.4% TS, -5

This was Bassey's worst game in a while, though even at that, he replaced Horford in the starting lineup for the second half as the team sought more athleticism in the paint. He had some nice plays, but it's hard to impact the game as a center when you play 16 minutes and don't get any rebounds, though he had some good defensive possessions still.

21 minutes, 8 points, 2 rebounds, 1 assist, 3 turnovers, 3 fouls, 3-for-7 shooting, 2-for-6 threes, 57.1% TS, -20

An okay scoring effort for Melton, but, like Podziemski, he really struggled to do anything against New Orleans' defense. He couldn't penetrate to get shots at the rim or create … one shot inside the arc and one assist, against three turnovers, just won't cut it for Melton, especially when Curry is out. Also one of his more poor defensive games of the year, I thought.

19 minutes, 12 points, 7 rebounds, 2 assists, 1 turnover, 1 foul, 2-for-6 shooting, 1-for-3 threes, 7-for-8 free throws, 63.0% TS, -7

It was a really tough game for the bench, but Santos did some very nice things. He looks much healthier than he had in recent weeks, and this was his first double-digit scoring output since the fourth game of the season. It's funny: sometimes Santos seems to be bothered more than most of the team by length and athleticism, and sometimes he seems to handle it better than the rest of the team. This game was squarely the latter, as he combatted New Orleans' size and speed by being extra aggressive, and couldn't be kept off the glass.

23 minutes, 11 points, 3 rebounds, 1 assist, 2 steals, 1 turnover, 4 fouls, 4-for-11 shooting, 2-for-6 threes, 1-for-1 free throws, 48.1% TS, -8

First and foremost, Richard's toughness needs constant praising. It seems quite possible that the plan for Richard is to play through the pain until the team gets healthier, and then forgo the second half of the season to have surgery for the torn UCL in his thumb. It could end up costing Richard the chance to play during a stretch run, and he deserves to be commended for toughing out the pain, and for putting the team first in just his second NBA season.

He's on the court for defense primarily, but he was one of the team's most aggressive scorers in this game. It didn't always work, as the shooting efficiency points to, but it was very notable, and it helped keep the Pelicans' defense honest. I liked seeing him in attack mode, and hopefully we get more of that going forward.

8 minutes, 2 points, 1-for-1 shooting, 100.0% TS, +6

Conventional wisdom might make it seem like Payton should be getting more minutes with Curry and Brandon Williams sidelined, but in reality, it seems to push GPII further down the depth chart, since the Dubs really need some offense to replace that high-octane duo, and Payton is a defense first (and second) guard. Still, he did some good things in his limited time in this game.

8 minutes, 3 points, 5 rebounds, 1 assist, 1 foul, 1-for-1 shooting, 1-for-2 free throws, 79.8% TS, +6

I don't like that it usually comes under such unfortunate circumstances, but I really am enjoying every chance we get to watch Ike. The undrafted rookie, who is on a two-way contract, really filled out the box score in his limited time in this game.

6 minutes, 2 points, 1 rebound, 1-for-3 shooting, 33.3% TS, +8

Even though he was on the team last year, Leons is still a bit more raw than the other two-way contracts on the team, Ike and Cryer. But he's still good for a few eye-opening plays when he gets on the court. He definitely has a lot of talent.

Grade: BPost-game bonus: Best plus/minus on the team.

Monday's inactives: Jimmy Butler III, Steph Curry, Moses Moody, Kristaps Porziņģis, Brandon Williams

#2844. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

Holdings are as of June 30, 2026, as reported in Berkshire Hathaway's 13F filing on August 14, 2026, except for:

The full list of holdings and current market values is available from CNBC.com's Berkshire Hathaway Portfolio Tracker.

#2842. Lisa Cook lawyer signals Fed governor won't attend Trump committee's hearing

Federal Reserve Governor Lisa Cook's attorney on Tuesday gave the strongest signal yet that his client will not attend a hearing at the White House with a "committee of inquiry" investigating mortgage fraud allegations against Cook.

The committee established last month by President Donald Trump, who has previously tried to remove Cook from the central bank's board, is pretextual and poses a threat to the Fed, attorney Abbe Lowell wrote in a letter to presidential counsel Will Scharf.

"Governor Cook has no legal obligation to participate in an unfair hearing — and indeed, has a duty to the Federal Reserve to decline to participate in any proceeding that would undermine the institution's unique status and independence," Lowell wrote in the letter, which is dated Monday and was made public Tuesday.

The latest correspondence came nearly two weeks after the White House committee delayed its proposed hearing with Cook to Dec. 14 from Nov. 5. Lowell said that any hearing with Cook could not take place until at least December because of conflicts with her Fed duties.

While Lowell had raised strong concerns from the outset about the legitimacy of the committee, he initially said Cook "welcomes the opportunity to present the facts so she can clear her name."

But he has continued to cast doubts on both the committee's purpose and its composition of three executive branch officials, including National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett.

Lowell's tone sounded more hostile than ever in this week's letter, the first known to be sent by either party in the matter since the Republican Party's devastating losses in the midterm elections on Nov. 3.

He accused Scharf and the committee of seeking "only to manufacture 'cause' for a predetermined removal outcome."

Lowell highlighted two top concerns. First, he asserted that Hassett "is not competent to serve on any factfinding committee" because his prior comments about Cook "demonstrate that he has prejudged the issue."

Second, Lowell called it "fundamentally unfair" that the committee is demanding Cook hand over documents "immediately," while pledging to provide the governor with its own records just seven days before the hearing.

"Your and the Committee's failure to address these legitimate issues, over multiple weeks of back-and-forth correspondence, reinforces my prior observation: President Trump's decision to fire Governor Cook has already been made," Lowell wrote.

"Consequently, this Committee process appears to be a façade designed to obscure a predetermined decision to fire Governor Cook or to unreasonably manufacture a supposed 'adverse inference' if she does not participate in a process that does not comport with the requirements of a fair hearing," the lawyer wrote.

Lowell told Scharf that Cook would still be prepared to appear for a hearing if the White House "implements fair procedures." But the "ultimatums" Scharf gave in his last reply in early November "do not provide a productive path forward to enabling that participation," Lowell wrote.

The White House did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment on Lowell's letter.

Trump, who frequently urges the Fed to lower interest rates, has long sought to fire Cook, an appointee of former President Joe Biden.

Cook, the first Black woman to serve on the Fed's Board of Governors, last voted to cut rates in December 2025, siding with the majority. The Fed unanimously voted to raise rates after its meeting in September.

Trump officials sent a criminal referral last year alleging Cook may have committed mortgage fraud by falsely claiming different properties as her primary residence in order to get more favorable terms. Cook adamantly denied wrongdoing, and, after Trump tried to fire her in August 2025, filed a lawsuit to block her removal.

The Supreme Court in June prevented Trump from firing Cook, but gave him a path to try again if additional legal steps were taken. In August, the White House told Cook it was reviving its efforts to fire her on mortgage-related grounds. In October, Trump signed a presidential memo establishing the committee of inquiry to investigate allegations that Cook lied on records related to "one or more mortgage instruments."

#2841. How the Bears’ defense grades after their bye week

The Chicago Bears had their bye week this week, using the weekend to recover and regroup heading into their final eight games of the 2026 season. All told, their 7-2 record is one to be proud of, and though their offense gets a lot of the national praise, their defense has certainly surpassed expectations.

In particular, the way the Bears' defense has played in the two games after they traded for Jeffery Simmons has been remarkable. Even before then, though, their defense was better than many anticipated, thanks largely in part to creative pass-rush scheming by defensive coordinator Dennis Allen and strong play from this offseason's additions. This defensive improvement has seen the Bears project as a likely playoff team by Cloudbet review and a potential winner of the NFC North for the second year in a row.

All year, I've been recapping how the Bears have done analytically based on PFN Impact grading. Coming out of the bye week, I wanted to recap how each of their defensive and special teams players have ranked at their respective positions throughout the course of the season.

Neville Gallimore: 68.8 (108th of 120 DTs)

Tyrique Stevenson: 63.3 (96th of 102 CBs)

As a team, the Bears rank No. 11 in the NFL with a 76.8 PFN Defense Impact grade after Week 10. They rank in the top ten in the league in percentage of drives allowing a score, lowest EPA allowed per play, and lowest success rate allowed.

The obvious standout here is Jeffery Simmons, though only two of his nine games have been played with the Bears this season. He only comes in second narrowly behind Quinnen Williams not just among NFL defensive tackles, but among all NFL defensive players, regardless of position. He has established himself as a genuine contender for Defensive Player of the Year between his short time with Chicago and his previous seven games with the Titans.

None of the Bears' current top three performers by PFN Impact grading were on the team last year. Dillon Thieneman has been fantastic as a rookie, allowing a targeted passer rating of just 67.3 this season with a well-rounded analytical profile that grades above average among safeties in coverage, run support, and overall efficiency. Cam Lewis had a shaky start to the year but has improved his standing in recent weeks.

In addition to Simmons and Thieneman, the third of those top three defensive players this year has been Devin Bush. His 84.3 run defense grade ranks seventh among all NFL linebackers going into Week 11, and he's tied for fifth at his position with seven tackles for loss. Neither of the Bears' other two primary linebackers have been fantastic in run support or in rushing the passer, but D'Marco Jackson ranks third among all linebackers with an 88.6 coverage grade, giving him the slight edge over T.J. Edwards in the grading scale.

Along the defensive line, Austin Booker has grown into a quality starting edge rusher with 5.0 sacks through his first nine games. He and Simmons are the only defensive linemen with an above-average grade at either defensive end or defensive tackle, though it's worth noting that Montez Sweat has been on an upward trajectory since the Bears acquired Simmons from Tennessee.

In the secondary, fourth-round rookie Malik Muhammad has surprised as an average starter, which is a very impressive start to an NFL career for a Day 3 draft pick. Kyler Gordon hasn't played in enough games to qualify for a PFN CB Impact grade, though his presence in the nickel has been a welcome one. Chicago remains shaky at cornerback along the perimeter, though Jaylon Johnson boosted his stock with a 94.9 grade in Week 9 against the Buccaneers amidst an otherwise disappointing season.

Cairo Santos ranks No. 21 in the NFL with a completed field goal percentage of 83.3% going into Week 11. He's gone 3-for-4 from beyond 50 yards, which is a better conversion rate than many kickers, but he doesn't have as large of a sample size kicking from deep, and the blocked field goal he had from 23 yards out in Week 2 against the Vikings looms large. From a punting standpoint, Tory Taylor doesn't grade as elite in a particular aspect of his game, but he ranks No. 13 in the NFL in both net yards per punt and gross punting average, making him slightly above average for his position.

#2840. Investor Steve Eisman says the AI neocloud stocks are in 'big trouble' from Nvidia fallout

Investor Steve Eisman said the so-called AI neocloud stocks will be the biggest losers from the unfolding Nvidia scandal.

Nvidia was essentially a "virtual vertical holding company and it's unwinding," said Eisman of "Big Short" fame on CNBC's "Squawk Box." "I think neoclouds are all in big trouble though because they are part of that vertical holding company and I think they're about to get ejected."

Neoclouds, which used borrowed funds to build data centers, have been among the hardest-hit stocks during the recent pullback in the artificial intelligence trade. CoreWeave and Nebius are each down more than 50% the last three months.

All eyes on Wall Street are on chipmaker Nvidia as it reports earnings on Tuesday after the bell while grappling with a potential Chinese espionage scandal. Founder and CEO Jensen Huang resigned on Thursday under a cloud of controversary revolving around his affair with a suspected Chinese spy. The Commerce Department has subsequently moved to close a loophole on China's remote use of data centers.

Investors are worried as scrutiny increases on Nvidia's China revenue, it may pull back on its extensive financing that supported the AI industry.

Frontier algorithm company Anthropic is expected to make its public debut on Thursday as concerns about the company's profitability potential linger amid the rapid and ongoing commodification of AI.

Eisman said he doesn't think the much-ballyhooed offering is very important anymore to the AI trade because expectations have come down.

"I don't think anybody really cares," the investor said.

Instead, Eisman is focusing on how much Democrats will restrict data center building when they take control of Congress and various governorships in January.

"I think the biggest issues by far are how much are, how much are local and state governments going to slow AI data center construction? Is it going to be 100% moratorium? That would obviously be — that would put the country into recession overnight. Or is it something like 10% to 30%?" he said.

In the event of a 10% to 30% slowdown in data center construction, Eisman said the group of companies within the AI trade that he favors the most are the hyperscalers.

"The group that makes out the best are the hyperscalers because they go cash flow positive again," he said.

Correction: Nvidia founder and CEO Jensen Huang resigned on Thursday. An earlier version misstated the day.

#2838. Browns offense effective at the goal line and perimeter against Texans

Down 14-0, down 17-0, it doesn't seem to phase this Cleveland Browns team in games against the Titans and Texans. Maybe it's just something with bad AFC South teams, who knows. But Cleveland only had one cold quarter of offense this week, and then they put it together for the remaining three quarters, showing a great deal of consistency by stretching the field with the long ball, working the perimeter, and having success at the goal line.

Below, we analyze the snap counts and stats on offense for the Cleveland Browns' Week 10 game against the Houston Texans.

25-of-40 (62.5%) for 312 yards, 1 INT. 8 carries, 49 yards (6.1 YPC), 1 TD.

There were a couple miscues by Deshaun Watson, with the biggest being a missed touchdown pass to Jerry Jeudy at the end of the first half that saw the Texans playing a bizarre defense. It's unclear if that was a mix-up between the two or an inaccurate throw. Watson also had a little trouble hitting some receivers in the right spot then they were crossing the field, as evidenced by passes behind KC Concepcion and ahead of Blake Whiteheart, leading to an interception.

Besides that, though, it was a solid game for Watson. The biggest key was his ability to push the ball downfield. With the Browns down big, he got the long ball to Jeudy. On the drive before that, he had a perfect one dropped into Concepcion too, but he couldn't hang on. Watson also got away from defenders against the Texans to gain 49 yards on the ground, including a touchdown run. He graded out to a 77.7 by PFF on offense, fourth highest on the team.

4 carries, 6 yards (1.5 YPC). 4 catches, 31 yards (6 targets).

10 carries, 38 yards (3.8 YPC), 1 TD. 3 catches, 33 yards (5 targets).

There still wasn't a ton of production in the ground game, although Quinshon Judkins did get the all-important go-ahead touchdown in the final minute. Both running backs were assets as receiving targets, though, taking advantage of the Texans' defense along the perimeter.

3 catches, 21 yards (8 targets). 1 carry, 6 yards (6.0 YPC), 1 TD.

Finally, the Browns actually got a completion to Malachi Corley. And then they did it a couple more times. It's about time they used him, because if he's playing snaps as a receiver, he can't just block 100% of the time. Some may say that you do that to catch a team by surprise like they did this week, but they should've done that sooner. Now, that play will be an option moving forward, and if teams collapse on it, you might have someone leaking out behind Corley. He was the team's highest-graded player on offense by PFF (89.8).

This was a little bit of a rougher day for KC Concepcion. I wouldn't say he had point blank dropped passes, but there were about three contested balls that I think he'd tell you that he wishes he could've held on to. Nonetheless, he still had a touchdown run as part of the Browns' goal line success.

Jerry Jeudy is getting open against these down-the-depth-chart defenders. As mentioned earlier, he also got open before the half, but Watson missed him.

Only one catch for Denzel Boston, who had a toe injury that bothered him again late. Thankfully, the bye is coming up. Funny enough, they used him heavily on punts this week.

Harold Fannin is just a beast, he refuses to go down. He now leads the team in catches (44) and yards (544) heading into the bye.

Blake Whiteheart was the team's lowest-graded offensive player. I'm not saying he should be replaced this year, but during the bye, seeing if the team can find a better contributing second tight end might be on their radar.

The offensive line was quite good in pass protection this week, as the sacks were minimal. Penalties weren't a thing either!

Tytus Howard was the team's second-highest graded player on offense (82.1), per PFF. Zion Johnson, Spencer Fano, and Austin Barber also did a solid job as far as grading goes. Elgton Jenkins is the only player who had a rough grade.

#2837. Nvidia warns chip buyers of legal risks tied to China AI access, sources tell CNBC

Nvidia warned its customers that new legislation on Chinese companies remotely accessing its artificial-intelligence chips could expose it and them to U.S. "legal action," sources told CNBC.

The chip giant is facing increasing scrutiny from U.S. lawmakers over Chinese companies' access to its advanced chips, which U.S. export controls restrict from going to China.

Nvidia messaged chip buyers in recent months to caution that the proposed Remote Access Security Act, or RASA, would give the U.S. government broader authority to control access to cloud services in the same way as physical hardware, potentially exposing both parties to legal risk, according to two people with direct knowledge of the matter. The people asked not to be named while discussing private communications.

It also said that, for now, it would not sell its chips to customers who rent more than 50% of the graphics processing units, or GPUs, to Chinese companies, the people said.

Separately, the Financial Times reported earlier this month that Nvidia verbally told data-center clients to direct at least 51% of their services to non-Chinese customers under future contracts.

Regulators have had limited powers to control companies' access to those chips through cloud services hosted overseas.

RASA passed the House of Representatives in January but has not yet passed the Senate. Remote access to the chips has provoked the ire of President Donald Trump's administration, which has sought to keep the chips out of China to maintain the U.S. lead in the AI race.

CNBC reported on Nov. 5 that Chinese lab Moonshot AI sought access to Nvidia chips through data centers in Europe. The Trump administration previously singled the company out, accusing it in July of accessing the chips through centers in East Asia.

The U.S. Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security, or BIS, announced on Monday an interim final rule designed to prevent Chinese companies' remote access to AI chips.

Nvidia and BIS did not respond to a request for comment.

On Sunday, U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent called for a "dramatic shakeup" at Nvidia after Jensen Huang's shock departure as CEO, amid reports of an extramarital affair with a woman U.S. authorities reportedly suspect of spying for China.

Bessent suggested other Nvidia executives should face scrutiny over the chip giant's compliance with export controls.

"I want to keep coming back to what was the culture at Nvidia, and were they pressing these seemingly illicit sales to goose their revenues and their earnings?" Bessent told Fox News.

In an August securities filing, Nvidia said it sought to "strictly comply with all applicable export control regulators" but did not have "physical control of our products after sale" and relied on its customers' and partners' compliance programs.

Huang has been asked to testify before the Senate Banking Committee on Dec. 3.

#2836. Warriors get blown out faster than the British in 1815 in disaster trip to New Orleans

In the Battle of New Orleans, Sir Edward Pakenham took the British army on a road trip to Louisiana to face Andrew Jackson's American troops. In less than 30 minutes, the British army had suffered over 2,000 casualties and Pakenham was dead. However, that's longer than the Golden State Warriors lasted Monday night in a 115-94 loss to the New Orleans Pelicans.

One game after the San Antonio Spurs raced out to an 18-0 lead on the Warriors, the Pelicans started the game on a 23-2 run. Coach Steve Kerr used two timeouts in the first six minutes of the game, a reaction to the team starting 0-for-11 from the field and 0-for-9 from three-point range. By the time De'Anthony Melton made the Warriors' first shot from the floor with 4:08 remaining in the first quarter, Saddiq Bey already had 13 points.

The first quarter ended with the Pelicans up 35-11. The Warriors shot 2-for-18 for the quarter, committed six turnovers, and allowed the Pelicans to grab four offensive rebounds. By the time the second quarter began, Joe Lacob was desperately trying to negotiate terms of surrender with Pelicans owner Gayle Benson, through delegates in the Netherlands.

LJ Cryer was particularly rough in this one, bricking a number of wide-open looks from behind the arc and causing our site's commenters to scour the ranks of G League players eligible for two-way contracts. The outside shooting was so bad that Bob Fitzgerald wasn't able to bust out a call of, "From Lake Pontchartrain!" on a make, dragging Kelenna Azubuike into extended discussions of biegnets instead.

The deficit got as bad as 45-18 by four minutes into the second quarter, while the Pelicans' lead would max out at 31 points late in the third, right around when Jeremiah Fears locked up his first career triple-double. The second-year guard finished with 19 points, 10 rebounds, and 10 assists, plus his first career flagrant foul for slamming into Charles Bassey. Fortunately for Bassey, Fears weighs about 185 pounds.

Bassey continued to be a magnet for both offensive rebounds and flagrant fouls in this one. Five days after Marcus Smart drew an ejection and a three-game suspension for punching Bassey, he drew a flagrant foul from the pint-sized Fears. It sparked a 10-2 run where the Warriors continued to miss shots, but also crash the boards and draw fouls, getting into the bonus early. They were 17-for-18 from the line in the first half, while going 8-for-39 overall.

In one of the game's lowest lowlights, newly-shorn Yaxel Lendeborg tried to hit Bassey under the basket, but hit the rim with his pass. Bassey managed to grab the loose ball, which was passed back to Lendeborg, who proceeded to fire a pass three feet over Georges Niang's head for a turnover. Look, the Minivan doesn't have hydraulics.

On another possession, the Pelicans grabbed two offensive rebounds, missed three shots, then stole the ball and got a layup when the Warriors finally did corral the ball.

The NBC Sports Bay Area telecast cut out in the middle of the second quarter, perhaps because the production truck itself was sick of watching the game. However, Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy announced at a press conference that the delay was actually former President Joe Biden's fault.

How bad was the first half? When Zion Williamson scored through contact and missed the subsequent free throw, the Warriors committed a lane violation that gave him a second chance at the and-one and a 29-point Pelicans lead.

As a (semi)final indignity, they fouled Fears with 0.5 seconds to play to make the halftime score 61-36.

Lendeborg cut off most of his hair over the weekend, perhaps trying to get sleeker after the blowout loss to the Spurs. He did come through with a game-high 28 points, doing most of his damage in the second half by drawing a lot of fouls. Lendeborg also logged a -30 plus/minus, which could make the other Warriors hesitant to bring out the hair clippers on the remainder of the road trip.

The final box score doesn't reveal the extent of the Pelicans' dominance Monday, mainly because the Warriors' two-way players dominated the Pelicans' two-way players in garbage time. Yves Missi may have owned the glass and grabbed 14 rebounds in 23 minutes, but Julian Reese and Malik Dia were no match for Graham Ike and Malevy Leons, baby! Cryer scored five of his seven points and all three of his assists in the final 4:13 of the game.

What were the positives? Will Richard gutted out what looked like a painful re-injury to his wounded thumb, finishing with 11 points and two steals, along with a couple of deflections. Gui Santos had 12 points and seven rebounds in 19 minutes and consistently attacked the hoop, going 7-for-8 from the foul line. Draymond Green was mostly invisible, but finished with eight rebounds and five assists and a Dennis Rodman-like two points. He seemed checked out near the end of the game, presumably thinking about the best places to buy beard dye on the road.

Also, as per the terms of the peace treaty, the Pelicans and Warriors will return all prisoners of war and Steph Curry retains his popcorn rights at the Smoothie King Arena, though Mike Dunleavy, Jr. did have to recognize the sovereignty of the King Cake Baby.

The Pelicans had lost six straight but seems to have righted the ship with two big moves: Sending Dejounte Murray home, and playing the Warriors without Steph Curry. The Warriors have now lost three straight following their eight-game win streak, with this game serving as a poor omen for the upcoming road games in Dallas and Memphis.

Maybe it's time to get more aggressive with Kristaps Porzingis trade talks? After all, Podz already cut his hair, Al Horford is already bald, and it would be a shame for Santos to lose his beautiful locks. It's too bad Curry is going to be ineligible for postseason awards, because the last two games are a compelling argument that he's the most valuable player in the NBA.

#2835. Aschenbrenner’s Situational Awareness made AI bets again before October rout

Leopold Aschenbrenner's artificial intelligence-themed hedge fund Situational Awareness, which nearly collapsed in July, loaded back up on AI stocks as it returned to the financial markets at the end of the third quarter. But all of its holdings are down big the last month and a half.

According to a new regulatory filing, as the fund got back on its feet Aschenbrenner took an equity position in Australian neocloud company SharonAI valued at more than $440 million, along with equity stakes in AI-centric plays Cleanspark, IREN, Bitdeer Technologies and Fermi. The Cleanspark stake is worth almost $39 million, while Iren and Bitdeer stakes are more than $21 million and more than $20 million, respectively. Fermi, meanwhile, has a value of more than $11 million.

The fund also owned call options at the end of September in neocloud Nebius, memory chip name Sandisk, the Roundhill Memory ETF (DRAM) and data center power play Bloom Energy. Sandisk and Nebius were key holdings that almost took out his fund over the summer.

All of these Situational Awareness holdings have been routed since Sept. 30. SharonAI is at the top of that list, seeing a 58% loss in October. It's also fallen 11% so far this month. IREN was down 41% in October, while Nebius declined 35%. Last week, Nebius plummeted more than 16% in a single day after the company issued a weak full-year forecast.

It's not known whether the hedge fund manager got out of those positions during the October AI selloff. The 13F filing is just a snapshot of what the hedge fund held at the end of the quarter.

Aschenbrenner — who launched the fund in 2024 after rising to fame that same year with his AI manifesto also entitled Situational Awareness — re-entered financial markets a bit in September, sources told CNBC at the time.

The Monday filing shows he picked up right where he left off.

The near-collapse of his hedge fund over the summer forced him to sell all of its public equity positions to Ken Griffin's Citadel. The fund had $45 billion in assets at its peak, but that tumbled to around $10 billion, consisting mostly of private stakes.

Stocked tethered to the AI trade took a hit in the most recent quarter as investors grew concerned about a slowdown in development of the technology. Those fears were spurred by comments from industry leaders such as Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei and OpenAI CEO Sam Altman back in September, with the latter saying that the company going public this year would be "ill-advised."

Since then, pressure on the trade has only continued.

In recent weeks, investors have worried about how Democratic wins in the midterm elections could hinder data center development, whether some technology companies are using too much debt to finance the AI buildout and what role Nvidia will play in that financing with Jensen Huang now out as CEO at the company.

Losses in Nvidia as well as other AI-related stocks dragged down the three leading U.S. indexes during Monday's session as investors continued to assess the fallout of Huang's departure.
