#2776. Fan Notes from the Patriots’ loss to the Lions

I wouldn't say that Sunday's game was the worst thing to ever happen to Patriots in Germany… but for a certain subset of the United States, it's certainly up there.

The Patriots got destroyed, Drake Maye is possibly out for the season, the defense has given up 80 points over a two-week span, and for all intents and purposes, the 2026 season is over. Maybe we'll get some good news over the bye week, but most of the reliable reporters are saying that Maye's ankle injury is fairly significant and we're looking at multiple weeks.

But Drake Maye getting hurt is just one of many things wrong with this team. Let's talk about them.

I'm going to start off with the posistives, because believe it or not, there are a few. None of them involve the product on the field, but in light of a game like this one some pretty great truths came to light.

The first truth is that any lingering doubts about Drake Maye being a superstar should fully be gone. The Patriots offense looked strong on their opening drive and then didn't do a damn thing for the rest of the game. The offense that Maye had moving was only able to generate two first downs until the fourth quarter. They had seven three and outs. Tommy Cutlets finished with 81 passing yards. Cory Kiner led the team in rushing with 25 yards. Jahmyr Gibbs single-handedly gained more yards than the entire Patriots team. This is an absolutely putrid unit that Drake Maye was able to run significantly well. I'm taking a lot of solace in that.

The second truth is that if your QB is going to suffer a "significant lower leg injury" a broken ankle is the one I want to hear about. Torn ligaments and ruined knees and shattered femurs take forever to heal and there's always a risk of reinjury. But if it's a broken bone, then Maye can just stay off it, have his beautiful wife ply him with her delicious baked goods, and he'll be just fine.

The third truth is that maybe this injury was kind of a blessing. I don't think a single game has gone by in 2026 where the Patriots didn't have at least one starter go down, and it was pretty obvious to me that this wasn't their year. I saw this team making the playoffs for sure, but I didn't have a ton of confidence that they'd be making it back to the Super Bowl. And even if they did, the NFC is light years stronger than the AFC this year, so I wasn't overly enthusiastic about having to go up against whatever wagon that conference sends to the championship game. So if the rumors are true and Maye is going to be out for a while, then you can just shut him down, shut A.J. Brown down, shut everybody down, evaluate the players that will be a big part of the team next year, and focus on a high pick in what promises to be a very strong 2027 NFL Draft class. That's not a horrible way to close out the season.

It especially sucks that the injury came on what I thought was a pretty clear hip-drop tackle, despite what the league says. Double sucks that the Lions lost one of their all-stars on the same play when Aiden Hutchinson Bill Grammatica'd himself celebrating. I don' think that Hutchinson is a dirty player and he was trying to hurt Maye in any way, so I don't see his injury as any kind of karma or anything like that. Tough day for the league losing two great players on one play.

But I did just get to use "Bill Grammatica" as a verb, which I never thought I'd do. So, that's something, I guess.

And you have to love the way the NFL stands behind the decisions the on-field refs make no matter what and only seem to apply the exact letter of the law when it doesn't make them look incompetent. There could be a scenario where a lineman pulled out a samurai sword and straight-up decapitated a player with no flag thrown, then when the commentators go to Gene Steratore for a reaction he'd say "if you take a look, guys, you'll see that there's still some tendons just barely keeping the head on the neck there. You'll also notice that the blood is gushing straight up instead of pouring out and over. That doesn't make it a clean decapitation, so great no-call by the refs."

There's no point discussing the offense. The offense is dead in the water. The only thing I wish I'd seen yesterday was Tommy DeVito paying homage to his home country's soccer team and taking a flop at some point, rolling around holding his ankle like someone just took a sledgehammer to it, Annie Wilkes style. I would have liked to see Behren Morton get activated yesterday. Because why the hell not.

Fun, random fact: though Annie Wilkes (played by the immortal Kathy Bates) shatters Paul Sheldon's legs with a hammer in the movie version of MISERY, in the novel she cuts off his foot with an axe. She also cuts off his thumb and puts it on top of a cake. #TheMoreYouKnow.

What makes absolutely no sense to me is how bad this defense has gotten. It's more or less the same unit from last year. They're all mostly healthy. And they can't rush the passer, cover, or tackle—three things I'd argue comprise an important part of the game. If the Lions had beaten the Patriots something like 16-6 yesterday, I would have been much more OK with this loss. But when Amon-Ra St. Brown, who weighs maybe 200 pounds at six feet tall, is carrying six Patriots defenders into the end zone with him, it speaks to a much larger issue.

And the larger issue parallels almost exactly the things that I have grown very, tired of.

I'm very tired of watching Christian Gonzalez get absolutely smoked at least once per game.

I'm very tired of the defense surrendering multiple 90-plus yard scoring drives.

I'm very tired of A.J. Brown dropping two out of every five passes thrown his way.

And I'm very tired of this pervasive feeling that, for some reason, this Patriots team doesn't really give a crap about this season.

And it's that last one that's most concerning. A team can be banged up and not all that good, but if I got the sense that they genuinely cared about the season, I could live with it. I just can't shake the feeling that the team has kind of punted on the year when they were all in and one of the most likable units ever in 2025.

Why? Is it really the Mike Vrabel offseason stuff? Is that really not sitting well with the team? Maybe. I find that a little hard to believe personally, but who knows. Whatever it is, I feel like the players who are still healthy enough to stand are on their tip toes glancing down south towards Cancun, where they have already booked a flight scheduled to depart on January 11th.

I used to live in Los Angeles. I spent three years there post-college working in all facets of the production industry, not because I had any grad dream of making it big as an actor or director or anything like that, but because I was young, single, and very stupid, and L.A. seems like a great place to be if you check all of those particular boxes. Sadly, I'm now only one of those three things, so my West Coast days are long over. I'm an East Coaster at heart, and I don't really miss LA. all that much, with two notable exceptions. The first is Southern California in February. The second is 10 a.m. football on Sundays. Waking up hungover as hell, stumbling to the kitchen for a Gatorade and a coffee, then crawling back into bed for a few hours to watch football while I assessed the previous night's poor decisions. I don't really miss the hangover parts, but it was nice to wake up to a football game.

So yesterday was a nice little throwback to that. I woke up, let the dog out, got some coffee, turned on the TV, and there were the Patriots, getting ready to dominate. Even better, I wasn't even hungover!

And by "getting ready to dominate," I mean "getting ready to get dominated." I think it's time we all sit down and have a serious talk.

Given the fact that Drake Maye left after the first drive and didn't return, there isn't really much to say about the game itself. So instead I'm going to treat today like Festivus come early and air a few grievances I currently have with the 2026 Patriots.

I feel like the respectful thing for both teams to do yesterday was just run it three times and punt it for the rest of the game to speed it up and get it over with. Not because the Lions owe any team any mercy or anything like that, but when a game is over by the first quarter, you just kind of want to get on with your day.

To that end, another nice thing about yesterday's game is it reminded me of the few years I spent living in Los Angeles post-college. Waking up on Sunday to a 10 a.m. football game was awesome, and then you were free from 1 p.m. on. Pats at 10, then head down to Hermosa Beach to watch the later slate of games. It's nice to have the Patriots game over by 12:30.

Especially when it's a game that represents the end of your season. The sooner you can attack the grieving process, the better.

They're 5-5 coming into the bye. Technically, the season isn't over. But with the Chargers, Bills, Vikings, Chiefs, Jets, Broncos, and Dolphins left to play, how many of those games do youy think they even have a chance of winning without Drake Maye? I think absolute best-case scenario there is three more wins to finish 8-9.

Which is also the absolute worst-case scenario. 8-9 is a great way to end up picking 16th or 17th overall as opposed to the Top 10 pick you'd get with a 5-12 season. So who knows. I'm kind of just talking out of my ass at this point. I guess all we can really do now is wait until we get the official word on Maye from the team and forge on as best we can.

#2775. NFL Honors moves from Thursday back to Saturday

The NFL Honors ceremony is on the move, again.

The made-for-TV awards show, which shifts this year from broadcast to streaming, will also be sliding back to its original day.

John Ourand of Puck reports that NFL Honors will be held not on the Thursday of Super Bowl week but on the Saturday of Super Bowl weekend. The show slid to Thursday in 2022.

Netflix has acquired the rights to the show, which attracted 3.7 million viewers in February 2026.

During the event, the various Associated Press awards are announced. Also, the new class of Hall of Famers is unveiled.

As NFL programming goes, the numbers aren’t great. As live TV goes, three million people at one time is nothing to sneeze at. Whether Netflix will realize that kind of audience remains to be seen — if the official figure is even disclosed.

Regardless, the show will quickly be forgotten as the NFL pivots the next day to the biggest event of the year.

#2774. Rex Ryan politicks for job with Cowboys

Rex Ryan is plenty of things. Bashful is not one of them.

Days after being arrested for allegedly running over the feet of two people in Nashville with his pickup truck and fleeing the scene, Ryan is on the air — and he’s using the bully pulpit to politick for a job with the Cowboys.

“I could go there today and say, ‘Alright, we can have an average ass defense,’” Ryan said on ESPN New York, via Brandon Contes of Awful Announcing. “I’ll promise you they’d be average, I’d probably make them top-10. But I’ll take that group right now, in one week, I’d go in and turn this thing out.”

Ryan’s pitch came after he ripped the current defensive coaching staff in Dallas.

“It’s the worst coached defense I’ve ever seen,” Ryan said. “It really is. And I’ve seen some horrible defenses, this one’s awful, and it starts with coaching. . . . They make more mental mistakes than any defense I’ve seen, as well as the physical, they’re not a tough physical defense.”

As noted separately by Michael McCarthy of Front Office Sports, Ryan also criticized the Dallas defense on ESPN’s Get Up.

“It starts with the young guy they hired, Christian Parker,” Ryan said. “What on his resume told you he was going to be a good defensive coordinator candidate for a team like the Dallas Cowboys with Super Bowl aspirations? By the way, what told you anything about him that would make you think he could be a leader in this? Because you better hire a leader for that spot.”

(It would have been great if someone else on the show would have said, “Yeah, the Cowboys defense is extremely pedestrian.”)

“To me, absolute garbage,” Ryan said. “You can’t fire them all yet, but you damn sure better. To me, it’s the whole defensive staff. It’s not just Christian Parker. It’s not. It’s every one of those coaches on defense.”

Prior to the 2024 season, the Cowboys were considering hiring Ryan to be the defensive coordinator. Eventually, Dallas brought back former Cowboys defensive coordinator (and former Vikings coach) Mike Zimmer. In September 2024, Ryan said he would have had the job if owner and G.M. Jerry Jones had been willing to pay.

“I could’ve fixed [the Cowboys’ defense] in a New York minute,” Ryan said at the time. “But, that being said, they couldn’t pony up the money, or I would’ve been there.”

Jones obviously could have come up with the money. But he wouldn’t. Because he doesn’t. And it’s hard to imagine Jones cleaning house on the defensive side of the ball and giving the keys to Ryan, who hasn’t had a coaching job in more than a decade.

Especially if Jones would owe any of the defensive coaches a buyout.

Then again, if Ryan is willing to work for whatever Jerry will pay, that could be the key to getting Ryan a chance to put his feet where his mouth is.

#2772. Cowboys vs 49ers: Dallas defense reaches a new low

We have officially reached the nadir of Christian Parker's and the front offices' machinations on the defensive side of the ball. The Cowboys lost 46-40 in a game that should have been an offensive showcase. Dak Prescott threw five touchdown passes, George Pickens caught three of them, and Dallas scored 40 points against one of the better teams in the NFL. It still wasn't enough.

That tells you everything you need to know about this defense. We've all seen plenty of bad Cowboys defenses over the years, but this group is making a compelling case for being the worst. The tackling is atrocious, the communication is a mess, and the decisions being made by defensive coordinator Christian Parker have people screaming "what are you doing?" at their TVs.

Sunday's loss was not simply a case of San Francisco having too many weapons. The 49ers have a talented offense, certainly, but Dallas made it far too easy for them. The Cowboys surrendered 584 total yards, the most they have ever allowed at home in franchise history. San Francisco averaged 9.4 yards per play and scored 46 points, including 29 in the second half, no wonder 40 points wasn't good enough.

Let's start with the numbers because they are almost difficult to believe. San Francisco recorded eight offensive plays of at least 20 yards against Dallas. Three of those went for more than 60 yards, and all three ended in touchdowns.

Brock Purdy connected with Christian McCaffrey for a 74-yard touchdown in the second quarter. Deebo Samuel took a reverse 70 yards to the house in the third quarter. Then, with Dallas having clawed its way back into the game, McCaffrey caught a short pass and turned it into a 66-yard touchdown with less than four minutes remaining.

Three plays. Two McCaffrey receiving touchdowns. One Samuel rushing touchdown. A combined 210 yards and three scores. 210 yards! On three plays! That's not just a defense giving up a few chunk plays. That's a defense repeatedly losing control of the game.

The other five gains of 20 yards or more came from Jauan Watkins, George Kittle and Keenan Allen, among others. The 49ers were consistently finding space, getting defenders out of position and making Dallas pay. Even when San Francisco wasn't scoring immediately, it was moving the chains in chunks.

The 49ers finished with 389 passing yards and 214 rushing yards. They averaged 7.4 yards per carry, and McCaffrey alone accounted for 223 yards from scrimmage and three touchdowns. Dallas had no answer for the versatility of Kyle Shanahan's offense.

The worst part is that this isn't an isolated issue. Explosive plays have been a recurring problem for this defense all season. At this point, it's difficult to call it a bad afternoon or a matchup problem. It's a fundamental failure of the unit. This is what this defense is.

The tackling has been an issue for weeks, and Sunday's game offered another painful reminder. Defenders consistently failed to finish plays, took poor angles and allowed ball carriers to turn routine gains into substantial ones. The 49ers didn't have to manufacture every yard. Too often, Dallas gave them extra opportunities through poor tackling and a lack of urgency.

When your defense is surrendering nearly 600 yards and 46 points, every player has to be flying around. There is no room for passengers. The Cowboys needed urgency, physicality and accountability. Instead, too many plays ended with San Francisco players running freely while Dallas defenders struggled to get them on the ground. Deebo Samuel walked for almost twenty yards on his long-score with a convoy of Niners blockers around him.

Head coach Brian Schottenheimer didn't hide his frustration afterward, calling the second-half defensive performance unacceptable and specifically describing the tackling as atrocious. He's right, but haven't we talked about accountability enough?

And then there was the play that should follow Christian Parker around for the rest of what should be his short tenure as Cowboys DC. Dallas had just scored to cut San Francisco's lead to 38-32. The offense had done its job, and the defense needed one stop to give Prescott another opportunity to take the lead. The 49ers faced second-and-9 from their own 34-yard line with just over three minutes remaining. What did Parker dial up? A zero-blitz that left 37-year-old Von Miller matched up against Christian McCaffrey.

McCaffrey is one of the most dangerous receiving backs in football. Miller is a veteran edge rusher whose value comes from getting after the quarterback, not trying to cover one of the league's most dynamic offensive players in space. Purdy found McCaffrey on a short pass over the middle, and the running back took off for a 66-yard touchdown. Miller had no realistic chance of keeping up with him, and McCaffrey ran untouched the rest of the way.

What exactly was Parker expecting to happen?

This is where criticism of the defensive coordinator goes beyond the results. Parker continues to show a lack of feel for his personnel, his matchups and the situation in front of him. You cannot put a 37-year-old pass rusher in that position against a player like McCaffrey, especially when the game is on the line and your defense desperately needs a stop.

It is one thing to get beat by a great player making a great play. It is another to manufacture a matchup that gives that player every possible advantage. Parker has struggled to get this defense playing consistently, and Sunday's decision was another example of questionable game management.

The Cowboys have invested significant resources in this unit, brought in veteran players and tried to build a defense capable of keeping them competitive. The results have been nowhere near good enough.

Perhaps the most frustrating part of Sunday's loss is that Dallas actually had made a huge comeback to be in position to win. Prescott finished 30-of-51 for 343 yards, five touchdowns and one interception. Pickens had a career day with 11 catches for 145 yards and three touchdowns. Ryan Flournoy added six catches for 71 yards and a score, while CeeDee Lamb contributed seven receptions for 72 yards and a touchdown.

The Cowboys scored 40 points and gained 439 total yards. They were competitive against an 8-1 team, and they still had a chance to win despite falling behind by as many as 19 points.

But the defense couldn't hold up its end of the bargain. Consider how absurd that is in the context of the NFL. Over the last five years in the regular season, teams scoring at least 40 points are 116-6-2. That's a 94.4% winning percentage. Dallas just became one of the exceptions.

The offense scored 40. The defense surrendered 46. There isn't much more to say.

Christian Parker has not shown that he has a handle on this defense. The personnel has its limitations, but the coaching decisions, execution and effort have all been unacceptable. The Cowboys don't need another postgame promise to study the film. They need answers, and they need them quickly. Because if this is what the defense looks like with the season on the line, there isn't much reason to believe.

#2767. Arch Manning will participate in Senior Day, but won't comment on his future

Texas quarterback Arch Manning concludes his fourth slate of home games with the Longhorns on Saturday. Whether he’ll have another year there, or somewhere else, remains to be seen.

The only clue, if it even is one, comes from his decision to participate in the Texas Senior Day.

“I’m a senior, so I’m doing it,” Manning said, via the Associated Press.

So what’s next after he participates in the traditional farewell home game?

“I’m not really thinking about the future right now,” Manning said.

The future entails three primary options: (1) enter the draft; (2) return to Texas; or (3) transfer.

Manning started the year as the favorite to be the first overall pick in the 2027 NFL draft. That has changed.

Last year, he had 26 touchdown passes and seven interceptions for the Longhorns, who finished 10-3 for a team that did not qualify for the College Football Playoff. This year, Manning has 19 touchdown passes and 10 interceptions through 10 games. Texas currently is No. 6 in the CFP rankings.

After two straight losses (to Oklahoma and Florida), coach Steve Sarkisian didn’t rule out benching Manning.

Texas has since won four in a row, capped by a come-from-behind victory over LSU in which Manning threw three interceptions but also engineered the game-winning drive.

His uncles, Peyton and Eli, exhausted their full college eligibility before entering the NFL. Both were the first overall picks.

For Arch, the final decision may come down to whether he believes he’ll be taken No. 1 overall — and whether he can land with his preferred NFL team.

He could get creative, declaring for the draft and going through the process and returning to college if he decides he won’t be the first pick and/or can’t get to the team he wants. While it may require the hiring of a lawyer and/or the filing of a lawsuit to perform an about-face, the NCAA’s rules have been exposed as a house of well-worn cards in a wind tunnel.

Regardless, Manning has a business decision to make. And there will be many factors to consider, from when he’d be drafted to where he’d be drafted to how much money he could make by returning to Texas to how much money he could make by transferring to another school — like Ole Miss, where Uncle Eli and his grandfather, Archie, played.

#2765. Always one of a kind, Oubre reflects on Sixers stint, why he chose Pacers

Always one of a kind, Oubre reflects on Sixers stint, why he chose Pacers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Kelly Oubre Jr. experience switched sides Sunday afternoon at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Back in Philadelphia with the visiting Pacers, Oubre was his usual self. There's never any risk of mistaking Oubre for somebody else.

"Love him," Pacers head coach Rick Carlisle said pregame. "Such an upbeat guy. He's such a unique person. Love the wardrobe, all the different stuff. He's a very authentically one-of-a-kind type guy."

Carlise then detailed Oubre's various basketball qualities, some of which he showed in Indiana's 131-124 loss. The 30-year-old lefty wing scored 16 points and grabbed four rebounds. His day also included a theatrical imitation of Jaylen Brown's off-arm push after the Sixers' star was whistled for an offensive foul.

Sixers fans grew familiiar with Oubre's strengths, weaknesses and eccentricities during his three seasons as a Sixer. He wound up becoming the team's third-longest tenured player behind only Joel Embiid and Tyrese Maxey. Oubre played 178 regular-season games and averaged 14.9 points, 5.4 rebounds, 1.6 assists and 1.3 steals.

After the Sixers replaced president of basketball operations Daryl Morey with Mike Gansey, Oubre signed a two-year contract with the Pacers in free agency.

"Mike came over from Cleveland and I heard whispers that he wanted some of his Cleveland guys," Oubre said before the game in the visitors' locker room. "And I was like, 'OK, cool. I'm a Philly guy. So I guess when he comes over, I will no longer be a Philly guy.' That was kind of the situation at hand.

"No bad blood because business is business at the end of the day. They have a great team over there, and they put together a team that they think is the best for them. I'll be all right elsewhere. I figured it out."

Oubre thought Indiana made sense as his next stop.

"It was the pace, the culture, the attitude that this team has," he said. "It's a fast-paced style of basketball. It's a bunch of guys who work collectively as a unit and kind of figure it out from there.

"I thought that would be a great opportunity for me to come and and kind of free-flow play, not have to think. I was wrong. Been thinking a lot, but I'm starting to figure it out and starting to get my rhythm here. … It's a great culture. Coach Rick Carlisle is a legend, Hall of Fame coach. And they wanted me. I love people who want me."

As Oubre settled into a new city, the Sixers had a stunning, transformational summer.

Gansey traded for Jaylen Brown, signed LeBron James, and added veteran role players like (former Cavalier) Dean Wade, Anfernee Simons and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope. Oubre followed along.

"It was must-see TV, like (for) everybody else," he said. "As a basketball fan, it was pretty cool to see where I was kind of reinvent and get new life and new energy. So I was happy with that. Would I have loved to be a part of it? Of course.

"But at the end of the day, I'm a grown man and I've got to keep it moving and figure it out for myself. It was entertaining. … I'm kind of happy for all the people that have sacrificed over these years. … I'm trying to put a road block in that road for y'all."

Oubre joined the Sixers on a minimum-salary deal in September of 2023 after averaging 20.3 points the past season with the Hornets. Head coach Nick Nurse pitched Oubre on his potential to be a valuable, multi-dimensional player for good teams.

"I was literally about to be done with basketball," he said. "Coach Nurse called me multiple times and said, 'I want you to play for me.' And I was like, 'You know what? This guy really seems to care. He really wants me.' So thank you for just pulling me out of that pit and that hole that I was in four years ago. I got some new life in my basketball career.

"I'm always thankful for that and I love Nurse for that. Just appreciative for my time here. The battle wounds and scars that I've been able to build up, it made me a better man."

Throughout his Sixers stint, Oubre often spoke of growth, evolution, adaptation and self-discovery.

He talked again Sunday about his desire to "Be water" and how life as a Sixer changed him.

"I grew a lot, man," Oubre said. "Just the mental fortitude of being able to withstand adversity. Being able to wake up and not necessarily know which soliders are going to battle, just having to put on different hats on any given night. At first it was challenging, because I'm thinking about all the logistics and things and like, 'This is not what they asked me to do.' But at the end of the day, I just bought into being somebody that can be like water and grow with the team.

"It's been a blessing to be able to play with (VJ Edgecombe), be able to watch Tyrese (Maxey) ascend. Those are the guys where we were front line a lot. Just going through war with those guys was amazing. It just taught me mental fortitude. If I can make it through this, I can make it through anything."

Oubre felt he developed a meaningful connection with the Philadelphia fan base over his three years.

"When they talk about the City of Brotherly Love, I think they're talking about your soul, not necessarily about what you can offer them and what you do," he said. "Everybody in this city works hard. They wake up, go to work, keep the city going. And us as the prima donna basketball players, the least we can do is go hard and sacrifice for them so that they have something to cheer about when they come home from work.

"I think that was the relationship that we had. I'm forever indebted and forever grateful to the people in the city of Philadelphia."

#2764. 2027 NFL Draft order: Titans move into top spot

With the Los Angeles Chargers' 23-20 win over the Baltimore Ravens last night, the Tennessee Titans now sit atop the 2027 NFL Draft order. As you can see, the Titans don't lose the strength-of-schedule tiebreaker until you get down to the Texans, who have three wins and will likely win a couple more games.

#2761. Observations after Sixers shake off slow start, Embiid injury scare to win

Observations after Sixers shake off slow start, Embiid injury scare to win originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers brushed off their blowout loss to the Celtics and got back in the win column Thursday night.

They overcame a first-quarter deficit as high as 17 points and earned a 123-112 home win over the Raptors. The Sixers moved to 10-3 and Toronto fell to 5-7.

Tyrese Maxey had 27 points and eight assists. Joel Embiid added 20 points.

Jaylen Brown tallied 18 points and six rebounds. VJ Edgecombe posted 15 points, eight rebounds and a season-high eight assists. Anfernee Simons scored 17 points and shot 5 for 6 from three-point range.

RJ Barrett recorded 31 points and 14 rebounds. Immanuel Quickley had 26 points and six assists.

The Sixers were without LeBron James (low back/sciatica management), Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain), Dominick Barlow (lower back stress reaction) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear).

The team told reporters Thursday morning that Bona will be re-evaluated in approximately one week and that Barlow participated in shootaround. The 23-year-old forward also went through a pregame workout on the Xfinity Mobile Arena floor. There's no formal timetable for his return.

Toronto's sidelined players included Kawhi Leonard and Scottie Barnes.

The Sixers will face the Pacers on Sunday afternoon in Philadelphia. Here are observations on their victory over the Raptors:

Dean Wade began the game in James' normal spot. The Sixers started slowly, missing their first foul field goals and opening 1 for 8 from three-point range.

He stayed down after setting a ball screen and having an apparent knee-to-knee collision with Ja'Kobe Walter. The Sixers called timeout with 8:30 left in the first quarter and Embiid grabbed at his right knee. He eventually rose with assistance from teammates and Sixers head athletic trainer Kevin Johnson, then limped back to the locker room. Jabari Walker entered.

At the 5:26 mark of the first quarter, Embiid checked back in to cheers from the home fans.

"I knew that he didn't twist it or anything," Maxey said. "I knew it was a knee-to-knee, so I wasn't too worried. He kept saying, 'I just got kneed.' That still hurts, though."

Embiid ended up logging 28 minutes, though he certainly wanted to play more.

"He was very, very motivated to play," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "It was hard to get him out for his scheduled rests tonight, which is a good problem sometimes."

While Embiid's return was a major positive development, the Sixers fell further behind. The Raptors' lead swelled to 32-16 with a Jamison Battle three-pointer and Nurse burned a timeout.

Simons sunk a buzzer-beating three to conclude the first quarter, cutting the Sixers' deficit to nine points.

"Those are big momentum plays that usually have some weight to them and carry you into the next moment," Nurse said.

The Sixers kept rolling against a shorthanded Toronto team playing on the second night of a back-to-back. Simons nailed a triple to kick off the scoring in the second quarter. Brown buried a mid-range jumper and Edgecombe jammed in a crowd-pleasing dunk to give the Sixers a 35-34 edge.

Brown and Edgecombe shined in the second quarter both as individuals and as a duo. The Sixers need Edgecombe to attack a bit more offensively when he shares the floor with Brown and bench players. It doesn't hurt for the second-year guard to have that clarity about his role in those units.

"He's one of our better playmakers," Maxey said of Edgecombe. "He's really good at getting downhill and creating for himself and others."

The Sixers' only three bench rotation players were Walker, Simons and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope (11 points, five assists). In addition to Walker, Wade got center minutes behind Embiid.

Embiid picked up his third foul with 2:23 left in the second quarter and had to take a seat. The teams were tied at 60 apiece through two periods.

The game remained even for much of the third, but Maxey fueled an 11-0 Sixers run late in the quarter.

His speed and the Sixers' collective focus on pushing the pace whenever possible allowed the team to get regular points before Toronto could set its defense. Maxey zoomed in for a layup, then tossed the ball ahead to Brown for an easy bucket. Brown assisted a Simons three. Walker converted a fast-break lay-in and canned a three on the first possession of the fourth quarter. All of a sudden, the Sixers held a 101-87 lead.

Brown, Edgecombe and the bench gave the Sixers more good minutes early in the fourth quarter. They sealed the deal without any late drama. And the fans chanting for a Tacko Fall appearance got their wish with a final-minute entrance by the 7-foot-6 center.

#2759. Jaguars’ Trevor Lawrence is about to give Giants their biggest test

Are the Giants ready for Trevor Lawrence?

Sunday's nationally televised Jaguars-Giants showdown at MetLife Stadium presents a huge challenge for coordinator Dennard Wilson's Giants' defense: Lawrence, one of the NFL's best quarterbacks.

He is third in the league in passer rating, one spot behind the 49ers' Brock Purdy and two spots ahead of the Lions' Jared Goff.

After this Week 11 matchup with Jacksonville, the Giants — who are trying to retain first place in the NFC East — host the 49ers in Week 13 and travel to Detroit in Week 16.

These are enormous tests for Wilson's group, which ranks 11th in Pro Football Focus' ratings.

Six quarterbacks currently have a plus-100 passer rating — Joe Burrow, Purdy, Lawrence, Patrick Mahomes, Goff and C.J. Stroud.

Wilson and Co. did a fairly solid job against Stroud during the Giants' win at Houston in late October.

The Giants opened the season against the Cowboys' Dak Prescott and Rams' Matthew Stafford — both high-level quarterbacks. Then things got easier in four of their next seven games — against the Titans' Cam Ward, Cardinals' Jacoby Brissett, Saints' Tyler Shough and Eagles' Joe Milton.

Yes, the Giants also had to face Washington's Jayden Daniels (twice) and Stroud in that stretch. But going up against Lawrence and then Purdy in two of the next three games is a different sort of issue.

At 7-2, Jacksonville is a well-rounded beast of a team — fifth in PFF's offensive ratings and sixth in defense.

As for Lawrence, he has 21 touchdowns and just five interceptions, to go with his 112.8 passer rating. He also leads the NFL in touchdown rate (8.1%), which means a higher percentage of his passes result in touchdowns than any other quarterback in the league.

Lawrence is a big-play threat, too, with a league-high 13.4 yards per completion. That will pose a significant threat to the Giants' secondary, which is currently eighth in PFF's coverage ratings.

Overall, the Giants have done a nice job of limiting long pass plays. That isn't surprising, since Wilson is a former secondary coach. They have surrendered 20 completions of 20-plus yards, fourth-fewest in the NFL.

But the Giants will have their hands full with the Jaguars' top two wide receivers, Parker Washington and Jakobi Meyers, who average 17.9 and 14.1 yards per catch. Washington is second in the league in that stat.

The Giants' top cornerback, Greg Newsome, is quite familiar with both receivers. He played with them in Jacksonville last year after an early October trade from Cleveland. Will that play to Newsome's advantage?

No matter how you slice it, Lawrence looms as the most effective quarterback (at least in terms of 2026 production) the Giants have faced this year. This is a major step up from Ward, Brissett, Shough and Milton.

NFL playoff picture: Where Giants stand in NFC East race after Cowboys defense flops Nov. 16, 2026, 3:54 p.m.

Giants' Malik Nabers critiques J.J. McCarthy's late-game decisions in loss to Commanders Nov. 13, 2026, 3:29 p.m.

And Lawrence has been playing out of his mind since Week 3, coming off a loss in Denver. In the seven games since then, the Jaguars are 6-1. Lawrence has 17 touchdowns, four picks and a 113.4 rating in that run.

The past three weeks, his rating is 129.0 (with seven touchdowns and one pick), as the Jaguars have crushed the Colts, Ravens and Titans — 42-7, 33-10 and 40-23.

Of course, all three of those teams have significant flaws, especially on defense. Wilson won't claim his Giants group is flawless. But will it have enough answers for Lawrence?

#2758. PFT's Week 11 2026 NFL power rankings

1. Bills (last week No. 1; 8-1): For three straight weeks, a sluggish start turns into an easy win.

2. Jaguars (No. 2; 7-2): Parker Washington is playing his way into the franchise tag.

3. 49ers (No. 3; 8-1): The defense is going to need to perform better against the Vikings.

4. Broncos (No. 4; 7-2): The next time the Chargers play the Broncos, Sean Payton will tell Jim Harbaugh to stick his "loophole" up his poophole.

5. Bears (No. 5; 7-2): They can't afford to overlook the Saints, four days before a showdown with the Lions.

6. Chiefs (No. 6; 7-2): There's nothing wrong with Patrick Mahomes's elbow.

7. Bengals (No. 8; 7-2): Joe Burrow was right about feeling 2019 LSU vibes.

8. Seahawks (No. 9; 6-4): "Take your time, Sam."

9. Lions (No. 10; 7-2): Dealing with significant defensive injuries has become part of their normal.

10. Vikings (No. 12; 7-2): The "story being written" could lead to a Pulitzer Prize shaped like a Super Bowl trophy.

11. Steelers (No. 7; 6-3): They're not ready to compete with the best teams in the conference.

12. Rams (No. 15; 6-4): Can they keep winning without Puka Nacua and Davante Adams?

13. Packers (No. 11; 6-4): They desperately need a running game.

14. Browns (No. 16; 6-4): The playoffs are anything but a pipe dream.

15. Giants (No. 13; 5-3-1): They'll need the extra days to get ready for the Jaguars.

16. Patriots (No. 14; 5-5): It feels like the wheels are coming off.

18. Raiders (No. 17; 4-6): Fernando Mendoza looks the part, but they possibly could have beaten the Seahawks with Kirk Cousins.

19. Buccaneers (No. 18; 3-6): They could catch the Lions napping on Sunday.

20. Ravens (No. 19; 4-6): They won't have to worry about Lamar Jackson making it back for a playoff push.

21. Commanders (No. 26; 3-5-1): When the dust settles on the season, they could be the NFC East champions.

22. Texans (No. 20; 3-6): Last year, the defense was good enough to win games on its own. This year, the defense is bad enough to lose games on its own.

#2756. Irish HC Marcus Freeman provides update on lineman Charles Jagusah

Notre Dame senior offensive lineman Charles Jagusah has been on a long and challenging journey dealing with injuries and multiple surgeries.

Jagusah has not played since the US LBM Coaches Poll No. 3 Fighting Irish faced Ohio State in the 2025 College Football Playoff National Championship game.

The former 4-star recruit and High School All-American missed all of the 2025 season and the 2026 season so far after he fractured his left humerus bones in a UTV accident following the 2024 season. Jagusah has also missed time due to a tear in his right pectoral muscle during a preseason practice.

After a number of surgeries to fix his arm, coach Marcus Freeman announced at his weekly press conference on Monday that Jagusah is no longer on the team's injured list. He said he's unsure if Jagusah will play in Saturday's game against SMU, but the senior's next phase will be "getting him to practice to see what he can do."

"It's amazing for that young man, who has been through so much and to be at a point where he can return to practice," Freeman said.

Jagusah has appeared in only five games since 2023 for the Irish. Freeman revealed "something crazy" about the senior lineman as he makes his valiant return to the team.

"He's never played a game at Notre Dame Stadium," Freeman said. "He's played in a couple of games, but he's never stepped on the field in a game at Notre Dame Stadium."

Jagusah appeared in a road game against Stanford, and the Sun Bowl, the Sugar Bowl and the Orange Bowl for the Irish.

Freeman said the team is planning to find a role for Jagusah to assist Notre Dame during its playoff push.

"How cool would that be?," Freeman said. "His journey has been amazing in terms of things he has overcome. I'm so excited to see that guy out in practice this week."

#2755. Observations after Embiid posts season-high 37 points in Sixers' win over Nets

Observations after Embiid posts season-high 37 points in Sixers' win over Nets originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Joel Embiid's season-best 37 points led the Sixers to victory on Monday night in Brooklyn.

The Sixers notched a 135-123 win over the Nets to move to 12-3 on the season. Brooklyn fell to 3-10.

Jaylen Brown tied his season high with 31 points and set a new season high with 13 rebounds. Tyrese Maxey had 24 points and nine assists.

Julius Randle's 33 points topped the Nets.

On the second night of a back-to-back, the Sixers were without LeBron James (lower back/sciatica management), Dominick Barlow (lower back stress reaction), Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear). The Sixers are now 4-0 without James.

They'll visit the Magic on Wednesday night. Here are observations on the Sixers' win against the Nets:

Embiid returned after missing Sunday's victory against the Pacers with the "right foot plantar fascia injury management" injury designation that's become the norm for him early this season.

He had an especially aggressive start in New York and set plenty of middle ball screens for Maxey. When the Nets blitzed or hard hedged Maxey, Embiid found a nice soft spot around the foul line and wasn't hesitant to fire open jumpers. The 32-year-old big man posted 13 points in the first quarter on 6-for-11 shooting (1 for 2 from three-point range).

Beyond the stats, it must be encouraging for the Sixers to watch Embiid trust his instincts and jumper. We'll see whether he can sustain a good run and fully snap out of his shooting slump.

Maxey broke the scoring ice when he swiped a steal and then coasted in for a dunk. Dean Wade's put-back dunk gave the Sixers a 4-0 lead. Wade started in James' place for the fourth time and had eight points and 11 rebounds over 32 minutes.

With James out and Embiid back, Jabari Walker played at both power forward and center. He logged 16 minutes, a number that would've been higher without his foul trouble. Walker scored 12 points and grabbed five rebounds, the latest in a string of strong performances.

The Sixers extended their lead at the start of the second quarter.

Anfernee Simons drilled a corner three. The Sixers' three-man bench shot 6 for 9 from the floor in the first half.

Everyone who played for the Sixers chipped in something positive. Wade leapt up to snag a Walker miss and threw down an and-one dunk. VJ Edgecombe hit a floater to grow the Sixers' advantage to 50-30. Embiid had a well-worn path to the foul line in the second quarter and made 9 of his 10 free throws.

Sixers head coach Nick Nurse kept an eight-man rotation, only playing Simons, Walker and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope off the bench until the final 18.5 seconds.

The Sixers' collective effort on the glass was a major strength in the first half.

All of the starters besides Maxey recorded at least one offensive rebound. Brown had a double-double before halftime and poured in 19 points over the first two quarters.

The Sixers' focus and intensity slipped in the third quarter.

Nolan Traore's layup cut Brooklyn's deficit to single digits. Michael Porter Jr. hit a long two over Caldwell-Pope to trim the Sixers' lead to six points.

The Sixers concluded the third period on a high note. Walker sunk a buzzer-beating three to lift his team to a 105-95 edge. Again, the 24-year-old provided much more than intangible energy and made meaningful, momentum-shifting plays.

The action stopped with 9:02 left in the fourth quarter after Brown was fouled by Randle. The Sixers' star forward appeared to have his forehead collide with Randle's face. He was down for several minutes before going to the bench.

Randle needed stitches in his mouth before re-entering with 5:01 to go, Amy Fadool reported on the NBC Sports Philadelphia broadcast. Brown subbed back in with 6:02 remaining and a bandage on the cut above his left eye. However, he exited at the 1:44 mark and did not return.

Nurse told reporters in Brooklyn that Brown was out at the end of the game because of "tightness," according to PhillyVoice's Adam Aaronson.

After their shaky third quarter, the Sixers were ultimately fine in the fourth and reached a dozen wins.

Egor Demin's jumper pulled the Nets to within 125-121, but Embiid then iced the game by swishing a corner three.

#2754. Dan Campbell: I'm not telling players to stop celebrating because of Aidan Hutchinson's injury

Lions head coach Dan Campbell isn’t going to tell his players to stop celebrating in the wake of defensive end Aidan Hutchinson’s torn ACL.

Hutchinson will miss the rest of the season after injuring his right knee while celebrating a sack of Drake Maye in Sunday’s game. Hutchinson jumped in the air and immediately grabbed his right knee after suffering the injury when he landed back on the turf in Munich.

During an appearance on 97.1 The Ticket on Tuesday, Campbell called the injury “one of those things that happened” and that “this game is built off of emotion” that he doesn’t want to take away from his players.

“I hate it, I know he hates it. But I mean, the number of moves that I’ve seen Hutch make and the number of plyometrics, explosive movements, the spins, the one leg lateral movements, lateral balances, I’m just like, it was one of those things, man,” Campbell said. “I hate it. I think we all understand what it is, and let’s be smart, but no, I’m not gonna say, ‘Nobody celebrates anymore.’”

Campbell recounted a story from his own playing days when he tore his biceps while celebrating a play and that didn’t lead him to make celebration avoidance a rule for the Lions when he got the job. That doesn’t make Hutchinson’s injury any easier for the Lions to deal with, but it makes his response to questions about the advice he’ll give in the future easier to understand.

#2753. Warriors’ Stephen Curry not ‘afraid’ to admit his $116 million contract extension could be it for career

Warriors' Stephen Curry not 'afraid' to admit his $116 million contract extension could be it for career appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

At 38, Stephen Curry finds himself in the middle of a remarkable season during which he has till date averaged 27.8 points, 3.9 assists and 3.7 rebounds per game. Still, every basketball fan is well aware that he is winding down arguably the greatest point guard career of all time, and well, the Golden State Warriors legend has himself admitted that he may be close to hanging up his boots.

Curry recently claimed that the two-year, $116 million extension that he recently signed is likely to be the final contract of his career, per ESPN.

"All of it," he said. "I assumed that it would probably be one of, if not the last, contract I signed. I'm not afraid to say it. But maybe I'll surprise myself and want to keep going."

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

The two-time MVP and four-time champion made it clear that he wants to finish his career at Golden State, although he claimed that he may as well end up surprising himself and sign another deal. Curry also indicated that he would accept a reduced role later in his career if that gave him a better chance of winning, saying that prioritizing championships over his individual status would not be an issue.

"It would not kill my ego," he said. "I want to win. Whatever that means, you make decisions based on what's in front of you."

His extension involved a slight pay cut that returned $10 million in cap flexibility to the Warriors for the following summer, while including a player option for the final season. Curry believes he can still be the best player on a championship-contending team, and his recent performances support that confidence.

Still, there is little doubt that age is finally catching up with Steph. Curry's right knee problems last season resulted in a 27-game absence spanning 65 days, with scans revealing no structural damage but identifying patellofemoral pain syndrome, or 'runner's knee.'

That however, does not seem to have resulted in any major impact in his ability to play, and fans will be best served cherishing whatever remaining time he has left in his career.

#2752. Dan Campbell won’t cut out celebrations despite Aidan Hutchinson injury

After Detroit Lions defensive end Aidan Hutchinson tore his ACL during a sack celebration, a huge debate ensued about whether Hutchinson was being reckless and selfish by putting himself in danger with the excessive celebration. A ton of Lions fans grew up in the era of Barry Sanders modestly handing off the football after touchdowns, so they've been accustomed to seeing players "act like they've been there before" and have a relative disdain for showboating. And given that this fanbase also witnessed a very similar injury to linebacker Stephen Tulloch in 2014, it's not surprising that some are mad at Hutchinson.

But don't count Lions coach Dan Campbell among them. During his radio appearance on 97.1 The Ticket this week, Campbell was asked if he believed he needed to talk to the team about celebrations, given both Hutchinson's injury and the amount of unsportsmanlike penalties the team has taken this year. He gave a pretty emphatic answer.

"No, I don't believe that. I think what happened speaks for itself. We're not talking to eight-year-olds here," Campbell said.

Campbell then proceeded to tell a story about how he thinks he tore his bicep celebrating a play with just an emphatic arm-pump celebration, and that didn't encourage him to tone down the emotions after a play.

"So if you say, 'Hey, don't pump your arm next time, make sure you stay stoic,' this game is built off of emotion," Campbell said. "If you have to contain yourself after a play is made—I'm not talking about in the moment, in the game, because you've got to keep your head—then you're really becoming something you're not."

Ultimately, Campbell seemed to imply that this was just a freak accident. He's seen Hutchinson do a ton of other incredibly athletic things that test the knee more than this celebration. He just got unlucky in the moment.

"The number of moves that I've seen Hutch make and the number of plyometrics, explosive movements, the spins, the one-leg—the lateral movements, lateral bounces," Campbell said. "I'm just like—it was one of those things, man. I hate it.

"But, no, I think we all understand what it is. Let's be smart, but, no, I'm not going to say, 'No one celebrates anymore.'"

As for how the team will respond with Hutchinson now out for the year, Campbell said they'll always be on the lookout for more talent to add to the roster—including claiming Tyrus West off waivers—but they are going to need several players to step up in his absence. But he's confident.

"We're prepared to handle this storm. We are suited to handle this storm actually I think better tahn we were in '24. That's the good news," Campbell said. "Now, what has to happen, what can happen, and what will happen is that somebody's got to pick up the slack. Somebody has to fill that void, and it's not going to be one person, by the way. One person's not going to fill the void of Aidan Hutchinson.

"So it's going to require (Alim McNeill) Mac to be at his very best. It's going to require (DJ) Wonnum to be at his very best. It means Derrick Moore and Sky(ler) Gill-Howard are going to have to freakin' step up and elevate their game. And Tyleik (Williams), we're going to ask Tyleik to do more, too. So that's really what you're looking at, man. So, we've got resources here to handle this, but guys are going to have to fill that void. We've got to do it collectively.

"The good news is we're playing as one on defense. That really is our strength right now, we're playing as one unit."

#2749. Jim Harbaugh on finally getting a win: "Let's do it every week"

Before the season, many expected the Chargers not only to get back to the playoffs for the third straight year but to finally make some noise.

Instead, it took them more than two months to make a peep.

But they roared to life on Monday night, with a win over the Ravens in Baltimore. The 23-20 victory instantly restored the boundless optimism of coach Jim Harbaugh.

“Thrilled, let’s do it every week,” Harbaugh told reporters after the game, via Jeff Legwold of ESPN. “Good to see the smiles back on the faces.”

Harbaugh should be smiling the most. His stubborn refusal to make major changes — starting with firing or reassigning offensive coordinator Mike McDaniel and continuing with a failure to trade players who won’t be there next year, like Khalil Mack — was putting Harbaugh in increasing danger of being fired. While the playoffs are a practical impossibility, Harbaugh’s obvious goal is survival for 2027.

That’s ultimately why there was no fire sale; moving good players would have made Harbaugh’s firing more likely. Surely, it’s one of the reasons why he resisted stockpiling future picks. If he’s not there to use them, who cares?

Could Harbaugh and the Chargers stack some wins? They’ve got the 2-8 Jets, the 5-5 Patriots (without quarterback Drake Maye), the 3-6 Buccaneers, and the 4-6 Raiders over the next four weeks.

It changes after that, with three of the last four games against the 8-1 49ers, the 7-2 Broncos, and the 7-2 Chiefs.

Will they run the table and sneak into the playoffs? Most likely not. Can they win enough to get Harbaugh a fourth season with the Chargers?

At this point, that’s the only realistic goal left.

#2748. Zeek Biggers suffers season-ending injury; Could Christian Wilkins be a consideration?

The Miami Dolphins defensive line suffered a major loss last week when Zach Sieler was placed on injured reserve following a biceps rupture sustained in the team's game against the Detroit Lions. A week later, they lost another key defensive tackle with Zeek Biggers sustaining a knee injury that will end his season. NFL Network's Ian Rapoport was the first to report the update.

Biggers will undergo surgery to repair the lateral meniscus in his right knee. He is expected to fully recover in time to start the 2027 season.

The Dolphins selected Biggers in the seventh round of the 2025 NFL Draft. He has appeared in 18 games, including all nine for Miami this season, with two starts and has recorded 38 tackles, 2.5 sacks, and a pass defensed in his career.

With Sieler on injured reserve and Biggers headed there, Miami is left with defensive tackles Jordan Phillips, Kenneth Grant, and Keith Cooper, Jr., on the 53-man roster. They also have Jordan Jefferson on the practice squad.

Phillips also left last Sunday's game against the Indianapolis Colts and had his arm in a sling after the game. He was expected to get an MRI on Monday to determine the severity of his injury.

The Dolphins could be looking to bring in a veteran defensive tackle to provide depth with he loss of Biggers, and could be desperate for additional linemen if Phillips is also unavailable for an extended period of time.

Miami could consider former Dolphins defensive tackle Christian Wilkins, if the veteran is healthy after a foot injury cost him most of 2024 and all of 2025. The former 13th overall pick spent five years in Miami before joining the Las Vegas Raiders in 2024. There was drama between Wilkins and the Raiders following his injury, with reported disagreements about his recovery plan as well as allegation that he tried to kiss a teammate on the head. Wilkins has not played since being released.

Bringing Wilkins back to Miami could give the Dolphins the veteran they need who can immediately slide into the starting lineup. How his foot healed after the injury and his playing shape would have to be determined before Miami would make any moves.

The Dolphins could also consider bringing back defensive tackles Matthew Butler or Fatorma Mulbah, both of whom were with the team in training camp before being cut ahead of the 53-man roster limit to start the regular season. Besides Wilkins, there also veterans like Quinton Jefferson and Maurice Hurst who could be considerations.

Miami face the Buffalo Bills in Week 11. Buffalo is the second-ranked rushing team in the league, averaging 152.7 yards per game this season, and will pressure the Dolphins interior throughout Sunday's game. The defensive tackles, including any additions this week, have to be ready for the onslaught coming their way.

#2747. ‘Hard to win': Simons, Walker carry Portland lessons into Sixers roles

'Hard to win': Simons, Walker carry Portland lessons into Sixers roles originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Anfernee Simons and Jabari Walker shared the floor for 128 games in Portland. The Trail Blazers won 43 of them.

They sat beside one another in the Sixers' interview room Thursday night after a victory and discussed what the other 85 taught them.

"Whether you've got a big role or a small role, it's hard to win in the NBA," Simons said. "We've seen it firsthand."

Their roles were considerably different then.

Simons was an established starter entrusted with creating and scoring. Walker, a 2022 second-round pick, was trying to establish himself in a rotation. During their three overlapping seasons, Portland went 33-49, 21-61 and 36-46. The Blazers missed the playoffs each year.

Both are now reserves on a Sixers team constructed to contend. In nine games they've both appeared in this season, Philadelphia is 7-2.

They provided 26 points, 11 rebounds and six three-pointers in the Sixers' 123-112 win over the Raptors at Xfinity Mobile Arena. Simons scored 17 points and shot 5 for 6 from three-point range. Walker recorded nine points and eight rebounds, including four on the offensive glass.

The Sixers outscored Toronto by 24 points during Simons' 23 minutes. Neither former Blazer committed a turnover.

Their production helped Philadelphia overcome a 17-point first-quarter deficit and improve to 10-3 without LeBron James.

"You learn once you come off the bench to kind of read the game before you get in there," Simons said. "Obviously, we started off kind of slow. You just want to go in, bring some energy, get some stops, get out and run. I think everybody who got off the bench brought energy."

Simons supplied an immediate lift with a deep three-pointer at the first-quarter buzzer. His shot ended an 8-0 Sixers run and trimmed Toronto's lead to nine.

He remained involved during an 11-0 burst late in the third quarter, knocking down a three and setting up Walker for a basket. Walker then made a three early in the fourth before Simons scored on the Sixers' next possession.

When Toronto pulled within six with less than five minutes remaining, Simons answered with his fifth three-pointer.

Making those shots is the most obvious way Simons can help a roster already featuring James, Joel Embiid, Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown. He said the Sixers have not made him feel as though every opportunity must go in.

"They encourage us to keep shooting the ball," Simons said. "Knowing that a team has that much confidence in us to keep shooting makes the game that much easier. Whether you miss or make, as long as we get the right shots in the right moments, I think everybody can live with the misses and makes."

Simons spent seven seasons in Portland before playing last year with Boston and Chicago. His move to Philadelphia reunited him with Walker and Rayan Rupert, another former Blazer who joined the Sixers on a two-way contract during the offseason.

Knowing familiar faces awaited him helped Simons enter another new locker room.

"It made the transition seamless," he said. "Just being able to have some comfortability with guys that I played with for multiple years. It's been fun, kind of feeling like a little bit of old times. Me, Bari and Rups have been through a lot."

Walker's path to Philadelphia began a year earlier and required an additional step.

After playing 188 games over three seasons with Portland, Walker signed a two-way contract with the Sixers in July 2025. He earned a standard contract in February and has since pushed his way into the backup-center conversation despite standing 6-foot-7.

Walker believes some of the work he did on losing teams was easily missed.

"When I first got to the league, I was part of a rebuilding process," he said. "I just felt like some of the little things I did didn't always get noticed when you're losing. I told myself, if I ever got to be part of a winning organization, then I'd go out and expand my abilities."

Rebounding has made Walker difficult for Nick Nurse to overlook.

Walker credited his timing and strong hands to lessons passed down by his father, former NBA center Samaki Walker. Those instincts have become especially useful while the Sixers piece together their frontcourt behind Embiid.

"Rebounding is a skill I kind of honed in on," Walker said. "My dad was a good rebounder. He was known for his rebounding and his timing. I think it's just a gift that he transferred to me."

Walker has made it clear that he expected to earn a role this season.

"I don't know if I was delusional, but I always felt like I was going to play and I was going to be a part," he said. "I read something where they said it's a surprise, but if you were around me this summer, it wouldn't be a surprise. This is expected, and I just want to continue to get better."

The freedom Simons once had in Portland is no longer necessary. Walker does not need a high volume of touches, either. Both understand that playing beside the Sixers' stars requires shooting when open, moving the ball and handling the less glamorous work.

"You've got to find ways to impact the game multiple ways, even if you're not scoring," Simons said. "Whether it's cutting, making the right read and pass to Joel, being the right swing pass to VJ, boxing out on defense or just being in the right position."

Their opportunities will fluctuate. Simons may take nine shots one night and only a few the next. Walker's minutes will depend on matchups and the health of Philadelphia's frontcourt.

Winning has changed what they are being asked to do. Their three years together in Portland taught them why the adjustment is worthwhile.

"You feel appreciative of the times where you go into a situation where your role might be a little bit less, but you're winning," Simons said. "That's something you can't take for granted."

The Washington Commanders run game against the Giants wasn't particularly noteworthy on the face of things. The Commanders totaled 105 rushing yards on 33 carries at 3.2 yards per carry. That number is boosted by quarterback Jayden Daniels running the ball too, both on designed runs and on scrambles. When you narrow it down to just the running backs, the Commanders backs totaled 77 yards on 22 carries at 3.6 yards per carry, which is still not particularly great.

However, there was a shift in the second half run game that saw the Commanders yards per carry jump pretty drastically. Under new offensive coordinator David Blough, the Commanders have transitioned away from former coordinator Kliff Kingsbury's heavy use of gap scheme runs into more of a zone run scheme. Kingsbury liked to use various gap scheme runs with different lineman pulling in different directions to confuse the defense, but Blough's foundation has been built on zone runs that have every lineman blocking in the same direction together.

The results have been hit and miss. Early in the year the offensive line looked good blocking zone and running back Jacory Croskey-Merritt hit some nice cutbacks. But in recent weeks, the zone run scheme has struggled to become a trustworthy scheme to keep the offense on or ahead of schedule, frequently leading to second and/or third and long situations. That was the case against the Giants too.

#2742. Mike Vrabel disagrees with Milton Williams's claim that field conditions caused injuries

On Sunday in Germany, both teams had key players go down with significant injuries. And both injuries could be season-ending.

After the game, Patriots defensive tackle Milton Williams blamed the injuries suffered by Lions defensive end Aidan Hutchinson (knee) and Patriots quarterback Drake Maye (ankle) on the playing surface in Munich.

“Two top players in this league, probably got season-ending injuries playing on that [field],” Williams said, via Andrew Callahan of the Boston Herald. “It was bad. But like I said, I ain’t making no excuses. [The Lions] executed. They played on the same field we did, and they executed and made more plays than we did. Give them credit.”

On Monday, Patriots coach Mike Vrabel disagreed with Williams’s opinion.

“I don’t think Drake Maye’s injury was caused by the grass,” Vrabel told reporters, “and certainly we’ve seen other players that have celebrated or have left their feet and come down and had issues at that. So the field was the same for both teams, and that’s kind of what the field was.”

Vrabel stopped short of commenting on whether the field was an issue independent of the injuries. But he’s right; it’s the same for both teams.

Still, we know by now that every field is different. And there’s surely a difference between American fields that host up to 10 games per year every year and the international one-off surfaces.

#2741. The 49ers are proving doubters wrong about their Kaelon Black draft selection

The San Francisco 49ers made a couple of bold swings in the 2026 NFL Draft, taking wide receiver De'Zhaun Stribling and running back Kaelon Black well above consensus this offseason.

While the Stribling pick caught a lot of initial flak, the Black pick drew the ire of national media as well, as he was the third running back taken in the draft, with Kyle Shanahan coming out and even saying that the Indiana product was the No. 2 back on their board.

The 49ers have had their fair share of misses in the draft at running back under Shanahan and John Lynch. Their first pick at the position back in 2017 was Joe Williams, who evidently didn't pan out. From there, they've taken Trey Sermon, Elijah Mitchell, Ty Davis-Price, Isaac Guerendo, and Jordan James before taking Black this offseason.

Williams, Sermon, Davis-Price, and Guerendo all failed to pan out and were third or fourth-round picks, which added a stain to the regime's draft history at the position. But Black has been an immediate success, surpassing my expectations from the draft, and the 49ers have shown a clear trust in the rookie from Day 1.

Black has gone for 356 yards and two touchdowns on 79 carries, providing a good complement to Christian McCaffrey as a hard-nosed, rugged runner. His 4.5 yards per carry mark ranks 12th in the NFL, while he's averaged nearly 10 carries per game.

Black has really come along late. He went for 63 yards and a touchdown on 5.3 yards per carry against Atlanta (although he did fumble in that game). He followed that with 70 yards on 11 carries last weekend against Las Vegas. And he just had 53 yards and a touchdown on 4.8 yards per carry against Dallas this weekend.

San Francisco came into the year saying it wanted to keep Christian McCaffrey fresh throughout the season. It's followed through with that sentiment, as McCaffrey is averaging 12.8 carries per game so far. A year ago, he averaged 18.6 carries per game through nine games. And that's not including the high receiving volume he's had in both seasons. Last season, the 49ers star paced the NFL in touches by over 40. This year, he's 17th, having nearly 90 touches less than the NFL's leader Jahmyr Gibbs.

The freshness has also led to a far more efficient backfield. The 49ers are fourth in EPA per rush this season and fourth in rushing success rate. A part of that is the offensive line play and a part of that is the ability to rotate backs and have that trust throughout games.

Speaking on the backfield split, head coach Kyle Shanahan pointed out how valuable McCaffrey has been as a mentor to Black this season, which has led to a successful pairing.

"I think Christian's the perfect mentor just because even if he doesn't say anything, just how Christian works every second, Christian's the most detailed, focused, obsessive player I've ever been around," Shanahan said on Monday. "Just watch how he blocks when [WR] Deebo's [Samuel Sr.] running back and he's playing receiver and just the technique and the effort he goes through on every single play. Just the way Christian plays football is the best example or mentor to anybody who's on our team or anybody who wants to watch how a football player goes about his business.

"I also think Christian really respects Kaelon. I remember Christian telling me right when he got here how much of a pro that guy seemed like and seemed like he was made of the right stuff. It's because K.B., similar to Christian, he works at everything. He's very hard on himself. He's very tough and approaches each day to get better. As talents and as people, I think they pair together very well."

The two styles are quite different. McCaffrey is the elusive back who obviously flourishes as a receiver, while Black has not been afraid to exert his physicality, which came out several times against Dallas.

"Oh, it was awesome," Shanahan said of Black's physicality. "The one I got to see live was running over the safety, and the safety brought it too. I thought that was a good play by the safety, but K.B. brought it more. Then to finish it on the corner was such an impressive run. I didn't get to see his chips on the D-Lineman live.

"I saw Quinnen Williams go out in the game and they told me that was because K.B. hit him and I was trying to figure out how he did because it was on a bootleg. Then he did it another time on another player going through the B-gap, so just the physicality he looks for and then the way his body's wired that when you have that mentality and you're wired that way, obviously when he hits them it feels a little bit different."

The 49ers have been willing to lean on McCaffrey when they need to, giving him 15+ carries four times this season. But he hasn't exceeded the 20-carry mark, which has allowed him to maintain a force as a receiver, where he leads all running backs in receiving yards. We saw that play out on Sunday, as McCaffrey had 13 carries to Black's 11, but added four catches for 161 yards and two touchdowns in the air.

San Francisco's offense has continued to find new heights, and a big part of that has been Black's readiness and ability to complement McCaffrey in the backfield. Thus far, they've proved the doubters wrong with a bold draft pick who has made a huge impact as a rookie so far.

#2740. Kyle Hamilton: Ravens are "disgracing" their "standards"

The Ravens wore all black on Monday night. It looked like they were going to a funeral.

The Ravens’ season feels over after losing their fifth in a row, following a 4-1 start. And while it’s easy to blame the free fall on the multiple injuries suffered by quarterback Lamar Jackson, new quarterback Jacoby Brissett managed to beat the Chargers with the Cardinals in Week 1. On Monday night, the Ravens were flat, lifeless, and uninspired in prime time against the previously 0-8 Chargers.

The end result was a second straight home loss in a standalone, nationally televised game.

“Right now, we are disgracing those standards, as leaders, as players and as a team,” safety Kyle Hamilton said after the game, via Jamison Hensley of ESPN. “Whatever flak we get is warranted. . . . All the talking that’s been going on is all just talk. If you don’t go out there and do what you’re supposed to do, stop the run, nobody really cares, and stuff like this just stops mattering.”

Hamilton understands that performances like this can have consequences.

“At some point, you have to just decide about your manhood and what you want out of your job,” Hamilton said. “This is our job. We can all get fired; that’s the reality of it. If we don’t do better, then we get fired.”

Hamilton has enhanced job security from his contract. Hamilton’s $17 million salary for 2027 is already fully guaranteed. And his $17 million salary for 2028 becomes fully guaranteed in early 2027.

Still, others who lack that type of financial certainty could be facing a one-way ticket out of Baltimore.

Compounding the latest loss is the fact that it came against Jim Harbaugh, the brother of longtime Ravens coach John Harbaugh and the mentor of current Ravens coach Jesse Minter. One of the explanations for the Chargers starting 0-8 was the departure of Minter, the Chargers’ defensive coordinator during Jim Harbaugh’s first two years in L.A. and his final two years at Michigan.

The embarrassing loss to the NFL’s last winless team creates an unfortunate bookend to John Harbaugh’s 18-year tenure in Baltimore. The year before he arrived, the Ravens started 4-2 before going on a losing streak that included losing to the league’s last winless team at home — the 0-13 Dolphins.

That got Brian Billick fired. And while Minter is a long way from being one and done, the Ravens need to stop the bleeding. Quickly.

The schedule isn’t favorable. They have a short-week trip to Charlotte and a visit to Houston before a Week 13 bye. The stretch run includes a home game against the Buccaneers followed by a four-game tour of the AFC North: at Steelers, Browns, at Bengals on New Year’s Eve, Steelers.

In John Harbaugh’s 18 seasons, the Ravens had fewer than eight wins only once (they were 5-11 in 2015). It’s going to be very hard to get to eight wins in 2026.

They may not get past five. Hell, they may not get to five.

Things can change quickly in the NFL. But the more the losses pile up, the harder it gets to dig out of the ever-deepening hole. For now, they’re fading into the rear-view mirror in a division with three teams that have six or more wins.

If the Ravens lose on Sunday, good luck finding the focus and the discipline during Thanksgiving week to be fully prepared for a meeting with the Texans that could be a de facto elimination game.

Even if the Ravens’ all-black uniforms was a fitting demonstration of the fact that, for 2026, they’re already dead.
