#2710. Tiger Woods to make long-awaited golf return at Hero World Challenge

Tiger Woods is ready to return to professional golf, and he's now set a date for when his comeback officially begins.

After multiple injuries and setbacks, Woods has committed to play in next month's Hero World Challenge with some of the PGA Tour's top players. This will be the first time Woods competes in a professional golf tournament in more than two years.

Woods hosts the annual event in the Bahamas, which is slated for Dec. 3-6. He was added to the event through one of three remaining wild-card spots in the 20-golfer field, according to tournament organizers on Tuesday, Nov. 17. The 50-year-old has been away from competition since The Open Championship in July 2024.

His comeback attempt ahead of last year's Masters was derailed by a DUI arrest and rehab stint stemming from a car crash in March that led to a plea deal and the suspension of his driver's license.

Tiger Woods said in a statement Tuesday night he is "stepping away" to "seek treatment and focus on my health." Woods' social media message marked his first extensive public comments in the wake of his rollover car crash and subsequent arrest for suspicion of DUI. Woods' prior dominance on the links cannot be understated. Take a look back at the 10 best moments of his career. Keep in mind, this is a subjective list.

10. "Better than Most" 2001 Players Championship Ask any golfer and they can tell you the exact putt that the words "Better than Most" come from. The iconic putt was great in and of itself, but it was made legendary by analyst Gary Koch's commentary, repeating "Better than Most" over and over with increasing enthusiasm as it got closer and closer to the hole.

9. 2000 U.S. Open Woods' dominance is well-documented, but it wasn't always. The 2000 U.S. Open was one of the first tournaments where fans started to realize just how much damage Woods could do. After leading by just one shot after the first round, Woods then kicked his game to another gear, leading by six after 36, 10 after 54, and 15 when all was said and done. Woods was the only player in the entire tournament to finish under par. He finished at 12-under.

8. Woods aces the 16th at 1997 Phoenix Open Often referred to as the "loudest hole in golf," the 16th hole at TPC Scottsdale is known for producing legendary moments. Of course, Woods had to add himself to that mix. Woods' ace in 1997 sent the crowd into an absolute frenzy, the kind of eruption that would make Shooter McGavin lose his mind.

7. Just short? 2005 Masters This is arguably the shot that Woods is most known for. Any golf fan can close their eyes and see the ball stop at the lip of the cup, flashing that iconic Nike logo, and leaving the crowd disappointed just moments before it takes one final turn into the cup and sending everyone into hysterics. The fact it happened at Augusta just added to the legend.

6. 2022 Masters Woods did not win this tournament, but Woods' career isn't all about the wins, it's also about the resilience, the ability to get back up when you get knocked down. Woods was involved in a pretty serious car crash just 14 months prior to this tournament. Somehow though, Woods still managed to make the cut at the Masters, extending his streak of consecutive Masters cuts made to 22, tying him with Fred Couples and Gary Player for the longest such streaks in history.

5. "Hello, world" 1996 Fresh off winning the U.S. Amateur for the third time in his young career, the golfing world was waiting in anticipation wondering if Woods would remain an amateur. However, it was clear that there was something in the works. After all, Woods' dad, Earl, had secured a five-year, $40 million deal with Nike, which in all likelihood, wasn't a spur of the moment deal. Woods' iconic "I guess, hello world" marked the beginning of what would become the most iconic professional career in golf history.

4. The "Tiger Slam" 2000-2001 Not many golfers can say they've won a career Grand Slam. Tiger Woods won all four tournaments in a year. Though some skeptics will say that it doesn't count since they didn't all happen in the same calendar year, those people don't have friends, so who cares what they have to say. Woods won all four major tournaments in a row, making him the defending champ at each of the biggest events in golf. That's like being an undisputed heavyweight champion in boxing, but probably a thousand times less likely. It's very likely that we never see another golfer win all four majors in a row. That's how insane this feat truly was.

3. 2006 Open Championship Though this wasn't the first or even the second time Woods would win the Open Championship, Woods' victory in 2006 was iconic because of what Woods was dealing with at the time. Woods lost his father, Earl, earlier that year. You can see the emotion on Woods' face after the winning putt as he shares an embrace with his caddie.

2. 1997 Masters Woods was under a lot of pressure prior to this tournament. He was the young up-and-comer that would bring in a new era of golf. But he'd never won a major, he'd yet to make a major impact on the sport. Then the 1997 Masters happened. He won by 12 strokes, and set a then-record four-round score of 270 (-18). Even more iconic was Woods' resilience in the tournament. Through nine holes, Woods carded a 4-over and was struggling immensely. He then shot four birdies and an eagle on the back nine, finishing the first round at 2-under. That was just a taste of all that was to come.

1. 2019 Masters By 2019, Woods was well past his prime. He wasn't the same dominant force that shook the Earth when he walked by. Woods hadn't won a major in over a decade. Fans had written him off, but Woods wasn't done. It was a flash from the past that had fans giddy with enjoyment. He didn't dominate like fans were accustomed to years prior, but he managed the win anyway. The best part was definitely him embracing his children behind the 18th green, the same place he hugged his parents after his win in 1997. It was really a full circle moment and an exclamation point on one of the greatest careers ever.

Tiger Woods said in a statement Tuesday night he is "stepping away" to "seek treatment and focus on my health." Woods' social media message marked his first extensive public comments in the wake of his rollover car crash and subsequent arrest for suspicion of DUI. Woods is also expected to play in TGL's 2027 season as a member of Jupiter Links Golf Club beginning next month. The PNC Championship, slated for Dec. 19-20 in Orlando, Florida, is the PGA Tour's annual father-son event that Tiger Woods and Charlie Woods have also been regulars at in recent years. Golfweek previously reported that PNC Championship organizers are holding a spot in the 2026 field in case Woods is healthy enough to compete.

Woods announced in October 2025 that he underwent a disc replacement surgery on his lumbar spine. It was the seventh back surgery of his career, and the second in about a year's time after he underwent microdecompression surgery in September 2024. Woods also needed surgery when he ruptured his Achilles in March 2025 while training and practicing at home.

Before the March 2026 incident that led to his arrest and decision to go to rehab, Woods had been working his way back with an eye toward playing in the Masters in 2026. He played in the TGL Finals on March 24 earlier this year.

The Hero World Challenge is a 20-player, limited-field event with no cut. Scottie Scheffler, Justin Thomas and Cameron Young are among the top golfers scheduled to play. Rickie Fowler and Gary Woodland also received special tournament invites along with Woods on Tuesday.

#2709. Dan Campbell says he won't discourage Lions players from celebrating after Aidan Hutchinson injury

Detroit Lions head coach Dan Campbell won't let one freak injury change how he approaches the game. Following star pass rusher Aidan Hutchinson tearing his ACL during a celebration, Campbell said he will not discourage other Lions players from celebrating big plays moving forward.

Campbell, who made those comments during an appearance on Detroit's 97.1 The Ticket, explained that Hutchinson's injury was "just one of those things," and that's not enough for Campbell to establish a "nobody celebrates anymore" rule.

"I hate it, I know he hates it. But I mean, the number of moves that I've seen Hutch make and the number of plyometrics, explosive movements, the spins, the one-leg lateral movements, lateral balances, I'm just like, it was one of those things, man," Campbell said. "I hate it. I think we all understand what it is, and let's be smart, but no, I'm not gonna say, 'Nobody celebrates anymore.'"

When asked whether he felt he would need to address the issue with the team, Campbell shot down that idea. He then revealed that he once sustained an injury while celebrating a big play.

"I think what happened speaks for itself," Campbell said on 97.1 The Ticket. "We're not talking to 8-year-olds here. And I'll give you a story. I remember one time I made a big play and I freaking pumped my arm up and I'm pretty sure I tore my bicep, but I didn't tell anybody. I was just excited. I just pumped my arm. It's not like I — so if you say, 'Hey, don't pump your arm next time, make sure you stay stoic,' man, this game is built off of emotion. And if you have to contain yourself after a play is made — I'm not talking about in the moment, in the game, because you gotta keep your head — then you're really becoming something you're not."

Campbell's comments shouldn't necessarily come as a surprise. The coach has always preached emotion and tenacity to his players. He's not suddenly going to change his outlook because one player got injured in an unconventional way.

While it doesn't sound like Campbell is going to let the injury affect him, it will affect the Lions. At 7-2, the team is near the top of the NFC. But its grasp on that spot in tenuous. Both the Chicago Bears and Minnesota Vikings are also 7-2, and the 6-4 Green Bay Packers aren't far behind them.

The margin for error in the division is razor thin, and the loss of Hutchinson could be the difference between the Lions getting a first-round bye in the playoffs, or fighting for their postseason hopes down the stretch.

#2707. Tiger Woods to return to competition at Bahamas tournament

Tiger Woods is returning to competition for the first time in two years, announcing Tuesday he will be playing in the Hero World Challenge at the unofficial event he hosts in the Bahamas.

His decision was posted on Instagram, with Woods joining the 20-man field along with Rickie Fowler and 2019 U.S. Open champion Gary Woodland.

The Hero World Challenge in New Providence, Bahamas, in Dec. 3-6.

Woods last competed in the 2024 British Open at Royal Troon, where he missed the cut in his last year playing all four majors. He also played the 36-hole PNC Championship with his son, where he could ride in a cart.

Woods ruptured his Achilles tendon in February 2025 and missed the entire year. As he was starting to practice this March, he was arrested and jailed on a DUI charge.

The 20-man field at Albany Golf Club is for players from the top 50 in the world ranking, though it reserves a spot for the tournament host.

#2706. 10 Takeaways from the Celtics loss to the Magic

On the second night of a back-to-back coming off of a dominant 131-94 win over the Dallas Mavericks, the Celtics faced off against the Orlando Magic. In a game that was back and forth the entire night, Boston would fall 115-105 after a rough fourth quarter performance.

Going into the final quarter, Boston came off a third quarter that saw them play their best basketball of the night. They outscored Orlando 36-25 and showcased their defensive synergy to take the lead. The fourth was a different story as Desmond Bane started to go off, the Celtics couldn't keep up, shooting 9-22 from the field and 6-16 from three while the Magic shot 16-21 from the field and 5-8 from three. Although it was a valiant effort by the Celtics, Orlando outscored Boston 40-28. The biggest problem in the fourth quarter for the Celtics was their rebounding. The Magic out-rebounded the Celtics 16-4 in the fourth quarter and it felt like they also got to every 50-50 ball. This was mostly due to the work of Jalen Suggs who was a monster inside but it also just looked like the Celtics were gassed and were just a step behind.

One of the biggest reasons why the Celtics lost this game tonight was the fact the Neemias Queta was not in the lineup. He was ruled out pregame due to rest and it felt like with the success Luka Garza has had against the Magic in the past, the Celtics could overcome the loss of Queta but that was not the case.

Garza played solidly but his three-point shots were not falling and that limited his impact. Mitchell Robinson looked lost at times on defense and a step slow on offense while Chris Cenac Jr. was playing his first real NBA minutes of his career. All of this resulted in the Magic shooting 19-23 at the basket tonight and out scoring Boston 54-32 when it came to points in the paint. Franz Wagner was able to get downhill all night long, collapse the Celtics defense, and find a wide-open Magic player on a lot of possessions. This allowed Orlando to keep a lead over Boston for most of the night and it didn't matter what big they through out there, Boston couldn't slow down the Magic from getting to the paint.

This game really highlighted how important Neemias Queta is to the Celtics defense. Boston has a net rating of +17.4 with Queta on the floor and a defensive rating of 108.4. When asked postgame on playing without Queta, Joe Mazzulla stressed how important he is by saying, "It shows the standard he needs to play to and he makes us a different team." Does this game show that we need to start the Neemias Queta for MVP campaign, maybe not. However, it does show Queta's ability to be the defensive anchor of this Celtics team and if he was out there tonight, Boston probably wins this game.

The Boston Celtics drafted Chris Cenac Jr with the 27th pick in the 2026 NBA Draft out of Houston and we saw flashes of a guy who could be a big part of the future plan of this team in Summer League and Preseason. When the regular season started, Cenac Jr. made a few appearances with the Maine Celtics where he performed well, even having a double-double of 19 points and 11 rebounds in Maine's first game of the season. Cenac Jr. got some run during the Celtics blowout win of the Mavericks on Sunday in the fourth quarter and his athleticism was on full display with three impressive dunks.

After that performance and with Queta out, he earned some minutes in this game against the Magic and I think he took full advantage of it. In 12 minutes off the bench, Chris Cenac Jr. finished with 4 points and 2 rebounds on 2-3 shooting. He made his biggest impact during the Celtics third quarter run where he started by making a great cut from the corner to catch a lob from Payton Pritchard. On the next possession got a great defensive stop on Franz Wagner. After Jayson Tatum got the rebound, he found Cenac Jr. for another slam that had him hyped and the TD Garden crowd in a frenzy.

It was interesting how they used Cenac Jr. in this game because he did offer a completely different skill set from Robinson and Garza. He brought energy for the Celtics when they desperately needed it in the second half and they even gave him a chance in the final five minutes of the game before Orlando pulled away. Jayson Tatum said after the Dallas game that "Chris is gonna be good. Chris is already good." After the Orlando game Tatum gave tough love saying, "We're not gonna hold his hand. When we get out there, we expect him to make plays because we know what he's capable of."

I have a hard time disagreeing with him. At only 19 years, it looks like he has a bright future with Boston and I hope he gets more chances throughout the season similar to this one. Brad Stevens might have found another gem with the 27th overall pick at the center position.

Another underrated reason for Boston's loss in this game has to do with the fact that Boston was not capitalizing with their opportunities at the free throw line. The Celtics shot 9-17 (52.9%) from the charity strip in this game and in a game where you lost by 10 points, leaving 8 points on the cutting room floor is just killer. On the season Boston is slightly below average when it comes to free throw shooting, ranking 19th in the NBA at 77.4%.

In the Celtics win against the Mavericks, they put on a historic offensive display in the first quarter. They outscored Dallas 42-9 in the first quarter, the biggest scoring margin in franchise history in a quarter. Naturally due to the fact that it was a back-to-back, it felt like the pendulum of shooting luck was going to swing back in the other direction in this game and that was the case. Boston scored 41 points in the first half against Orlando which just feels kind of funny given what they did on Sunday.

Boston shot 7-27 (25.9%) from three in the first half but as a whole, the Celtics shooting wasn't that bad. They finished shooting 20-56 (35.7%) from three point range and via Sean Grande on X, this game snapped a streak of 25 straight games where the Celtics won when they hit 20+ threes in a game. You could also call Sunday's performance an anomaly since they were coming off three days rest but that is just how the NBA is sometimes. One day the shooting is the best you've ever seen and the next day it's a struggle even though you were getting similar open looks.

Paul George once again put up a solid performance for the Celtics, finishing with 22 points and 8 rebounds on 7-13 shooting from the field and 5-9 from three. It was good to see George play the second half of a back-to-back tonight after sitting the last two times Boston was in this scenario and he was a much needed boost to the offense. He had 15 of his points in the first half and felt like the only guy who was able to hit a shot from three.

When asked postgame on playing in this game and his workload, George seemed happy to just be able to be on the floor. "Just excited that, at year 17, I can still be playing back-to-backs given all the adversity and all the bad luck that I've had from an injury standpoint." It makes me happy that Paul George is finally able to have a healthy start to his Celtics tenure so far. If he continues this play he could end up as an all-star and I think Boston will take that any day of the week.

One trend we have seen from Jayson Tatum in the last two games is when the third quarter hits, he takes over on scoring. For the game, Tatum finished with 24 points but in the third quarter alone he finished with 14 points on 5-11 shooting from the field and 2-5 shooting from three. This follows his third quarter performance against the Mavericks where he had 21 points on 6-9 shooting from the field and 4-6 shooting from three.

This isn't only a two game trend however, Tatum is currently third in the NBA in third quarter scoring at 8.2 Points, only trailing Shai Gilgeous-Alexander and Jalen Brunson. He is also tied for third with 2.3 rebounds and third at 2.0 assists in 11.1 minutes (also ranked third).

This is most likely due to the fact the Celtics rotations have Tatum play the entirety of third quarters before sitting to start the fourth but the last two games just made me notice how often he starts to take over games in the third quarter. It could also just be because Tatum is already warmed up and after halftime he knows he can just empty the tank with a bench lineup alongside him. I do think in this game Joe Mazzulla waited a little too long to bring him back into the game during the fourth quarter but I don't know if that would have made a real difference anyway. It might not be the most meaningful stat but it does showcase Tatum's ability to take over games and I think it's pretty neat to see the stats back up the eye test in this scenario.

The last guy I wanted to highlight from this game who I thought had a big impact was Payton Pritchard. He finished with 21 points, 5 rebounds, and 4 assists on 7-16 shooting from the field and 4-11 from three. This was a just a solid all around Pritchard game and his offense, much like Paul George, was desperately needed at times throughout this game.

The fourth quarter was where he did most of his scoring, finishing with 13 points on 4-9 shooting. Pritchard was the Celtics number one option when Tatum went to the bench and kept Boston in the game with answers to Orlando's offense. At the end of the first quarter, we also saw a signature buzzer beater that although we see all the time from him, is still a joy to watch. His passing also looked really good in this game, setting up multiple alley-oop opportunities with Cenac Jr and Robinson. The scoring numbers are starting to rise for Pritchard as he is averaging 15 points per game over his last 5 games and has been a big reason as to why Boston had previously gone on a 6-game win streak.

In the past, I never understood the hype around Franz Wagner. Like I knew he was a good player and he was part of the Magic's duo alongside Paolo Banchero but it always felt like every time I watched him he either under performed or was out with injury. This game changed my tune on him however as he finished with 21 points, 9 assists, and 8 rebounds. Just watching him play against the Celtics, I couldn't help but notice the similarities he has to Jayson Tatum, especially as a passer.

Tatum is most effective for the Celtics when he is driving into the paint. The defense collapses and at times has all 5 guys focusing on him while Tatum makes the right pass to a wide open shooter at the three point line. In this game, it felt like Franz Wagner was doing the same thing and it was incredibly effective. He is a lot better of a passer than I gave him credit for and to think this is one of the worst games he's had in the last week or so is crazy. I always thought Banchero was going to be the Tatum clone out of this duo for the Magic but I was wrong. Wagner is becoming a legitimate star and I wonder what Orlando will do with their duo in the future.

I don't know why, but every time the Celtics play the Orlando Magic it feels like pure chaos ensues. Over the years there has been one consistent thing in this matchup and that was Boston always beating Orlando in TD Garden. That streak was snapped in this game as the Magic got their first win in Boston since December 18th, 2022.

Over the years though we have seen these teams battle and they kind of remind me of the Celtics from 2020-2023. They have their duo who are still figuring it out in Banchero and Wagner, they have their Marcus Smart in Jalen Suggs, and they have a bunch of other young depth guys who just play hard and contribute like Anthony Black and Tristan da Silva. In the regular season, Outside of Game 5 of the 2025 first round playoff series, the Celtics and Magic have played a ton of close games with an average margin of victory of 10.3 points in the regular season with Boston winning 5-4 in the last 9 matchups. I'm not sure what it is about the Magic, but you always know you're going to be in a dog fight with them every time the Celtics see them on the schedule.

#2705. NFL power rankings: Nick’s picks for how teams stack up before Week 11

The Buffalo Bills scored 21 points in the second half to blow out the Jets, 31-12. James Cook III rushed for 157 yards with one score. The top of the NFL is competitive among the top five teams, but Buffalo remains No. 1, due to solid defense and a Josh Allen-led offense.

The Chicago Bears had their bye in Week 10.

Brock Purdy went 22 of 28 for 389 yards with three touchdowns, two of which were LONG completions to Christian McCaffrey. Deebo Samuel Sr. had a long touchdown run as well. The 46-40 game was competitive, due to the performances of Dak Prescott and George Pickens, but San Francisco was too much for Jerry Jones' Cowboys.

Jacksonville beat up the Tennessee Titans, 40-23, but the game was close until the second half. Liam Coen's offense gained 459 yards; they're one of the hottest teams in the league, and their defense is playing well too. The Giants hosted the Commanders on Thursday Night Football, and they couldn't find a way to win a tight game after Dennard Wilson's defense forced two interceptions and a turnover on downs in the fourth quarter. Despite 293-yards from J.J. McCarthy, and another epic performance from Isaiah Likely, the Giants could not secure the win, allowing the Commanders to hang inch closer to the top of the NFC East.

The Steelers came out of their bye and laid a dud in Cincinnati. The offense couldn't move the football and the Steelers' defense could not slow down Joe Burrow. The Steelers' four-game winning streak is now over, and an interstate rivalry game in Philadelphia awaits them.

#2704. ESPN's ranking for Florida's Thomas Haugh in Player of the Year race

As the college basketball season enters its third week, ESPN recently released its rankings of the nation's top 10 players and early contenders for National Player of the Year honors, placing Haugh at No. 2 overall.

The race for college basketball's most prestigious individual award looks more competitive than in recent seasons, with an influx of returning stars, talented first-year students, and international prospects strengthening the national talent pool.

According to ESPN's Myron Medcalf, Haugh has emerged as one of the country's premier players after electing to return to Gainesville for another season rather than pursue a professional career. The decision came after Florida's disappointing second-round exit against Iowa in the 2026 NCAA tournament, a loss Haugh previously acknowledged played a significant role in his decision to return.

In the first two weeks of the season, Haugh has elevated his production across several statistical categories, especially from beyond the arc. The veteran forward is shooting an impressive 45% from distance while attempting five shots per game, showing significant improvement as a perimeter scorer.

One of his most notable performances came on the road against in-state rival Florida State on Nov. 10, when Haugh recorded 21 points and 10 rebounds while making three of his six three-point attempts.

So far this year, Haugh is averaging 17.3 points, 8.3 rebounds, and 2.5 assists per game, while shooting 52.6% from the field. His scoring versatility, rebounding, and improved perimeter shooting this season have helped reiterate his standing among the nation's elite players while strengthening Florida's championship aspirations.

With the Gators looking to make another deep postseason run, Haugh's continued development could be instrumental in determining whether Florida can return to the national championship conversation.

#2703. A glimpse of the team we thought the Suns could be

There's no denying that the Phoenix Suns have struggled this season, and through the first 12 games, you could point the finger squarely at the starting unit. But in game number 13, we may have witnessed the beginnings of a collective breakthrough.

We already know that the second unit possesses many of the characteristics that made the Phoenix Suns special a season ago. They're scrappy, they're disruptive, and they're connected. Perhaps some of that stems from having clearly defined roles and understanding how their individual skill sets complement one another. Whatever the reason, what has become abundantly clear through the early stages of this season is that if the starters could begin to string together productive stretches of basketball, the Suns could be a really good team.

In Denver on Monday night, particularly in the second half, we witnessed a starting unit that looked much more connected and began playing a brand of basketball reminiscent of the Phoenix Suns of a year ago. They were jumping passing lanes, attacking the glass, and playing with a collective sense of urgency. You could feel the energy typically associated with the second unit beginning to manifest itself within the starting five.

Was it perfect? Far from it. Both the Suns collectively and their starting unit specifically still have a considerable amount of work to do. But what I witnessed on Monday night gave me hope that the characteristics we expected from this basketball team can become more prominent moving forward, especially as the schedule begins to ease up.

I'm not ready to declare that the Phoenix Suns turned a corner with their victory in Denver. But they certainly started walking in that direction.

It was Collin who led the second team unit against the Lakers on Sunday, and Collin who gets the majority of the Baller vote.

#2702. Jerry Jones on Christian Parker's status: We're talking about that

Cowboys defensive coordinator Christian Parker failed to get a vote of confidence from head coach Brian Schottenheimer on Monday and he didn't get one from Cowboys owner Jerry Jones on Tuesday either.

Parker's defense has underperformed all season and they hit another low point during Sunday's 46-40 loss to the 49ers. The Cowboys gave up 584 yards over the course of the game and the lowlights of the unit's performance included three touchdowns that covered at least 66 yards.

Schottenheimer said on Monday that the Cowboys were "still in the process of going through all the film and evaluation of where we stand" when asked if there would be a change involving Parker. During an appearance on 105.3 The Fan on Tuesday, Schottenheimer's comments were brought up and Jones was asked if the team is considering any changes involving Parker's role or responsibilities heading into this week's game against the Titans.

"We're talking about that. I'm not going to talk about it here, but we are talking about those things," Jones said.

Jones gave a similar answer to a question about simplifying the defense, but having these conversations 10 weeks into the season means that there's an acknowledgement that the status quo isn't working. It also means that anything other than a significant shift to the team's defensive operations is unlikely to result in the kind of change the Cowboys need to salvage their season in the final seven games.

#2701. Of course Skip Bayless is stirring pot between LeBron James, Lakers’ Luka Doncic

Of course Skip Bayless is stirring pot between LeBron James, Lakers' Luka Doncic appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

Sports commentator Skip Bayless never shies away from voicing his opinions. He is known for being a critic of NBA star LeBron James. Bayless is now claiming the Los Angeles Lakers are happy to be rid of James.

Bayless says there was tension between Luka Doncic and James in southern California.

"They do not miss LeBron James because Luka just couldn't stand it anymore. I'm not saying he hated LeBron, but he didn't love him. And he definitely didn't love sharing the stage," Bayless said, per Double Coverage podcast.

#2700. Alexander Ovechkin announces this will be his last NHL season: 'Let's have fun'

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

Alex Ovechkin's 22nd NHL season will be his last, the Washington Capitals superstar announced on Monday."This is gonna be my last year," Ovechkin told his teammates in a video the Capitals posted to social media. "It's sad, but it is what it is. We have an unbelievable team this year. There are 82 games left. Let's make the playoffs, and let's have fun."

Ovechkin has already established himself as the greatest goal-scorer in hockey history with 929 tallies, and he still has 82 games to add to that total before he hangs up his skates.

The No. 1 overall pick in the 2004 NHL Draft, Ovechkin immediately established himself as a prolific scorer by notching 52 goals en route to winning the Calder Trophy as a rookie.

Since then, Ovechkin has won the Rocket Richard Trophy as the NHL's leading goal-scorer a record nine times. The Capitals legend's resume also features an Art Ross Trophy and three Hart Trophy wins.

The crowning achievement in Ovechkin's illustrious career is leading Washington to its first Stanley Cup in 2017-18. After coming up short in a number of postseasons prior to that, Ovechkin led all playoff performers with 15 goals and won the Conn Smythe Trophy as a result.

Following the 2025-26 season, there was speculation that Ovechkin could retire, but he signed an incentive-laden one-year deal with the Caps.Sensing that this could be Ovechkin's final season, the Capitals loaded up to give their franchise icon one more shot at the Stanley Cup. Washington signed former Buffalo Sabres winger Alex Tuch in free agency and acquired winger Jordan Kyrou from the St. Louis Blues via trade.

No team is as loaded on the wings as the Capitals, and they have one of the best rosters in the Metro Division. Washington has the ingredients to challenge the defending Cup champion Carolina Hurricanes and get Ovechkin one more ring before he rides off into the sunset.

#2699. UEFA Nations League: Olise and Kumbedi help France top Group A1 as Belgium see off Italy

Michael Olise and Saël Kumbedi helped France to a top spot finish in Group A1 of the UEFA Nations League. Zinedine Zidane's men scored two second-half goals to beat Türkiye 2-1 on the final matchday, while Belgium will now follow them in the quarter-finals after a 4-1 success over Italy.

Roberto Mancini's side missed out on the knockout stages as they failed to build on their emphatic victory over France. Olise had another solid performance for Les Bleus, although it was the first time he didn't register a goal or an assist under the new head coach.

Kumbedi suffered relegation with Wolfsburg last season, but Zidane called the 2. Bundesliga fullback for the senior national team after Pierre Kalulu's injury. After coming on as a substitute against Italy, the 21-year-old played the entire 90 minutes on Sunday.

The sought-after attacker Junior Eli Kroupi also played his first home game against Türkiye, and he capped it off by scoring France's second goal after Emirhan Topcu's own-goal at the start of the second-half. Can Uzun's Türkiye relegated to League B after losing all six games in the group.

Italy opened the score against Belgium in the other game from the group, but Marc van Bommel's men responded immediately as their big win sent them to the knockout stages. The last eight of the competition will take place in March with a two-legged format as Germany hope to get involved with a win over Netherlands on Monday.

#2698. Panthers vs Saints game review: Finally

If there is only one thing in this world that can be said with certainty, it is that following and watching the Carolina Panthers is not for the faint of heart. One must be resolute in their fandom in order to withstand the trauma inflicted on those who tune in to see what Dave Canales and boys in process blue have in store that week.

I say this because had Sunday's nail biter of match-up gone to overtime, I may not have survived. Not to say this would have been entirely the Panthers fault, as many of the aspects of the game that threatened my mortality were beyond their control. The main threat to my well being coming in the form of the little yellow flags that we all learned clash harshly with process blue.

Yet, despite the fever dream of a broadcast that seemingly consisted of 80% penalty announcements and 20% turducken propaganda, the Panthers emerged victorious and gained sole control of the NFC South. Sure, you would like the win to be a little prettier and inspirational, but after losing three straight contests beggars cannot be choosers – and the Panthers were certainly begging for a win.

Thankfully, the Panthers did more than just beg for the win. In fact, there's been a few changes, both subtle and not subtle, that have been made in recent weeks that may have contributed to Sunday's victory. For this game review, instead of rehashing the officiating debacle that we covered earlier this week let us instead focus on the details that allowed the Panthers to overcome it.

When Todd Wash took over as defensive coordinator, he did so with the promise that the Panthers would play more aggressively on defense. That the Panthers were going to play fast and if mistakes were going to be made, they were going to be made going 100 miles per hour. So far, I'd call that mission accomplished.

Starting from the Saints first drive of the game, the differences in approach were obvious and so were the dividends they paid. Instead of lining up 7 yards off the line of srimmage, the defensive backs stood right across from their receivers and challenged them every step of the way. Instead of the free slant being available that became the signature of Ejiro Evero's defense, Marco Wilson was in position to contest the route and popped the ball in the air and into the waiting arms of Devin Lloyd.

It's more than just how the secondary is playing, as the lineman seem to have much less to think about and are given the green light to rush the passer without having to be as mindful of their gaps. I would even hypothesize that their aggressiveness to attack immediately off the snap instead of reading and reacting to the offense is a likely cause of the offsides, encroachment, and neutral zone infraction penalties that have popped up the last two games. Reel in the pre-snap penalties while maintaining the level of pressure on the opposing offense and we might be on to something.

With the adjustments to scheme and philosophy there are also a few notable changes in personnel. Rookie safety Zakee Wheatley has seemingly usurped Nick Scott as the starting safety after playing 100% of the defensive snaps. With that, Scott still sees plenty of action after moving over to being more the team's nickel defender. Chau Smith-Wade, who was once penciled in as the Panthers starting nickel has taken over as the full-time starter at one of the outside corner spots.

Notably, Wheatley's rookie classmate saw a minor tick up in snaps on Sunday with 15 compared to 13 versus the Denver Broncos. It may not seem like many, but 15 was the third most amongst defensive lineman behind Derrick Brown and Tershawn Wharton. Hunter should see more than 15 snaps a game going forward if he remains the teams 3rd defensive lineman especially if the sub-package featuring Patrick Jones lining up against an offensive guard is used any less going forward. This package got plenty of use and led to many of the Tyler Shough's many struggles against the pressure, but I don't expect it to be a constant in each game.

It's not complicated, the players that are supposed to be good have to play like it. On Sunday, many of the players the Panthers rely upon rose to the occasion. Two players in particular looked like the two best on the field on either team. That was Chuba Hubbard and Jaelen Phillips. Hubbard channeled 2019 Christian McCaffrey on Sunday by playing 85% of snaps, piling up 170 yards on 32 touches with 2 scores. The comparison to McCaffrey goes beyond the statistics, as Hubbard's moves on the field had me double checking what year it was. I'm not kidding, when was the last time you saw a Panthers running back make a man miss in the open field like this and take it another 30 yards up field?

While Hubbard willed the team to victory on offense, Phillips was determined to make the Saints right tackle want to retire. Phillip's was dominant racking up one sack that counted, and another that should have counted if not for a questionable decision by the officials to call a horse caller for a tackle that occurred within the pocket. Alas, we'll just have to settle for a few tackles for a loss and 8 pressures going by NFL's NextGenStats and Sumer Sports. Not bad.

Shout outs to Derrick Brown who finally notched his first sack of the season and Monroe Freeling, the Panthers first round pick this year and frequent starter at right tackle, who played his best games a pro. Only allowing 1 pressure and having a few dominant reps in the running game is what you want to see from a first year player looking to earn their stripes.

Now, time will have to tell how 'real' the Panthers are going forward. To be blunt, the Saints are not a good team and the Panthers looked at moments like they should have won by 2 or even 3 scores despite very nearly losing. Against a better opponent, the Panthers will not have the same margin for error. Let's see how they hold up next week when they take on the Baltimore Ravens. Until then, keep pounding.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2697. Open Thread: San Antonio Spurs kick off their annual “Season of Giving”

Each year at this time, the San Antonio Spurs hosts a series of events designed to support the community. Through outreach, food distribution, and gift-giving the organization invites families to partake in a series of Spurs holiday traditions.

"The San Antonio Spurs are brightening the holiday season for local families through community-centered initiatives and player engagements as part of the annual "NBA Season of Giving," a five-week celebration running from mid-November through December when NBA teams give back by supporting and uplifting youth, families and organizations in their communities."

On Monday, November 16, the Spurs hosted families from local community organizations for a Thanksgiving-themed dinner at Frost Bank Center. Spurs staff volunteers, Hype Squad and Coyote served dinner, followed by an on-court activity for the youngsters participating in the evening.

They were joined by players Luke Kornet, Jayden Quinatance, Tarris Reed, Jr. and David Jones Garcia.

Said Patricia Mejia, Chief Impact and Inclusion Officer at SS&E,

"The holidays are a time to come together and care for one another, and we want families across San Antonio to feel that support this season. Through Season of Giving, our players, staff and partners can help ease some of the pressures families face and create opportunities to enjoy the holidays together."

The Spurs Season of Giving continues Wednesday with an H-E-B Thanksgiving food distribution.

#2695. Lions stock report: 21 risers, 5 fallers against the Patriots

The Detroit Lions' offense got back to its overpowering ways with its first 30-plus-point scoring output since Week 5, despite not being entirely sharp. Meanwhile, the defensive resurgence continued, allowing only 10 points, although that comes with a grain of salt given the early-game injury to New England Patriots quarterback Drake Maye. Unfortunately, for the second victory in the last three weeks, the overall winning feeling was bittersweet, overshadowed by the potential season-ending loss of Aidan Hutchinson. The locker room again has to endure the perils of losing another defensive captain and one of its biggest difference-makers. His absence will place an even greater onus on the offense to perform at an elite level and help carry the team.

Dan Campbell continued to echo confidence in the team's plan to break this portion of the schedule into a four-game series, or quarter of the season, from traveling to Miami, to Munich, back home without a bye against Tampa Bay, and the final (turkey) leg coming on a short week as they host the Bears as their Thanksgiving guests. Halfway through, the team has executed well, handling its business on the business trip, with the old adage that defense travels living up to its billing. Just as importantly, Lions fans have flocked to take over both Hard Rock Stadium and now seemingly all of Munich. The resounding takeaway from the trip is the power of not just the Detroit Lions, but the fandom that's part of the journey with them.

With the Lions winning 35-10, their 25-point margin of victory being their largest since Week 2 against the Bears last season, and extending their winning streak to five games, the Week 10 stock report entails far more positive risers than fallers.

It's getting to the point where Gibbs is almost a permanent fixture in the stock-up section, as he continues to barrel through a career season playing like a legitimate Offensive Player of the Year candidate. It's impossible to leave him off after leading the Lions in both rushing and receiving yards. He notched the third-most rushing yards in a game in his career (153), the second-most receiving yards of his career (99), and the second-most yards from scrimmage (252) in a game in his career. Gibbs also recorded the second-most rushing first downs (9) in a game in his career. He now has six career games with eight or more rushing first downs, and half of them have come since Week 5. Not bad for a team that once went 70 consecutive games without a 100-yard rusher from 2013 to 2018.

Gibbs was playing at a different speed than the Patriots' defense, able to dodge, duck, dip, dive, and dodge defenders with ease while displaying a fantastic feel for pacing and patience to set up his blockers, but also knowing when to floor it and explode through the hole. Both of his touchdown runs exemplified that perfectly. The team's third touchdown drive consisted entirely of six Gibbs touches for 84 total yards, right after he and Sione Vaki nearly single-handedly marched the Lions from their own 3-yard line to set up the Isaac TeSlaa touchdown.

Having days when the play design and offensive line allow him to get into a zone and take over games for different stretches will be remarkably valuable come the postseason.

The big boys up front unexpectedly got muscled around by the Miami Dolphins' defensive front last week, not long after the team let the Packers' defensive front invade their side of the line of scrimmage. But the unit responded triumphantly with the return of Christian Mahogany to the lineup.

Although they still had some squeaky wheels in pass protection early on, something they need to continue calibrating, they were dominant for the majority of the game. They consistently reset the line of scrimmage, displaced defenders, washed out defenders at the second level like a tidal wave, hit their targets on pulls, and gave Gibbs and Vaki rushing lanes to run through. They also gave Jared Goff stability in the pocket after the early leakage and were essential in the screen passing game.

Most importantly, they started to reduce negative plays. Their stuff rate allowed was only 11.8%, the second-lowest of the season behind only the Jets game, a stark contrast to the 30.3% allowed against the Green Bay Packers. They also allowed a respectable 2.74-second time to pressure, helping the offense produce its second-highest EPA/play of the season (+0.25) and highest yards per play (7.3).

It felt like a stepping-stone game in which both sides of the line, Penei Sewell-Christian Mahogany and Tate Ratledge-Blake Miller, continued to develop chemistry and complement each other on two-man blocking assignments, while Juice Scruggs maintained his exceedingly excellent level of play at the pivot. Sewell killing two birds with one stone, without even touching one of the defenders on Gibbs' sweep touchdown, was an impressive feat that only he could pull off.

It was far too fitting for Bavarian St. Brown to put on a show while in the spotlight in his mother's native Germany. What was surprising was seeing him score multiple touchdowns from deep range, becoming the first Lions wide receiver since Marvin Jones in 2020 to score multiple receiving touchdowns of at least 25 yards in a game. In doing so, he also joined the illustrious company of Calvin Johnson and Herman Moore as the only Lions players in franchise history with at least 50 receiving touchdowns. St. Brown's surprising explosiveness also led to him tying a career-high with 24.5 yards per reception in a game.

Big-play St. Brown kicked off his fireworks show and set the tone with a wild-and-wacky, slip-and-slide 56-yard touchdown catch-and-run that was reminiscent of some of Golden Tate's most thrilling plays as a Detroit Lion. He continued his smooth navigation on the greasy, yet firm, thin-grass surface by expertly swerving around blockers for a 28-yard zoom on a tunnel screen, finding an open lane on the Autobahn before getting a little help from his friends to push him over the edge. To fully maximize the Lions' offense, St. Brown also added his trademark tough-nosed blocking effort and execution, once again proving just how valuable a team player he is.

McCreary is on his fourth defensive coordinator in his last four seasons, and the Lions were also breaking in new coverage principles to start the season. While McCreary was inconsistent early on, he may finally be settling in. The interception was a nice gift right into his breadbasket, but his pass deflection against DeMario Douglas, covering the full width of the field, was particularly impressive. On the day, he allowed only two receptions for 10 yards on four targets and a 16.7 passer rating when targeted. McCreary erased Douglas in coverage, a slot player who has been playing really good football recently.

He did so while splitting time between outside cornerback in base personnel and the slot in nickel personnel. As he continues to get more comfortable in that Amik Robertson role, perhaps his best football is still ahead of him in the second half of the season.

Sione Vaki, RB: One way to stay ahead of the sticks and mitigate negative plays is simply making sure Vaki gets some carries, as he plows through the hole and consistently falls forward.

Jameson Williams & Isaac TeSlaa, WR: While the duo "only" combined for six catches on 12 targets for 69 receiving yards and a touchdown, they made some big-time catches, including TeSlaa's spectacular, acrobatic end-zone grab and Williams' toe-tap along the sideline. It was also impressive how bought-in both are when it comes to run blocking and extra-effort downfield blocking, including on St. Brown's touchdowns and multiple explosive runs by Gibbs.

Tyleik Williams, NT: Had a lower-than-typical snap count (19) but was impactful in disrupting the run game and tossing Patriots center Jared Wilson around.

Alim McNeill, DT: Another game, another sack, a handful of pressures, and a run stop. He got the best of veteran guard Greg Van Roten multiple times throughout the game.

Derrick Moore, DE: Moore is really bursting onto the scene, with back-to-back games featuring five pressures and two sacks. He also played a career-high 40 defensive snaps and will now be thrust into a much larger role as he steps up in Hutchinson's absence.

K.J. Britt, LB: Started rotating in with Malcolm Rodriguez in the second half and immediately looked like a Stephen Tulloch-sized battering ram, while also looking surprisingly fluid and comfortable dropping into coverage.

Rock Ya-Sin, CB: Ya-Sin had ideal matchups with the physical, lumbering Mack Hollins and A.J. Brown. On 22 combined coverage snaps against the two, he was targeted five times and allowed only one catch for 5 yards.

Brian Branch, S: The human torpedo. Branch was lockdown in coverage, allowing only one catch for 6 yards on four targets, and was continually flying downhill to stuff Patriots running backs in the hole or cutback lanes at the line of scrimmage.

Chuck Clark, S: I'd check your surroundings while reading this article because Clark might fly in and tabletop you, tipping you over like a wheelbarrow out of nowhere. Clark has easily stacked his two most effective games back-to-back against AFC East foes in the Dolphins and Patriots, becoming more active making plays around the football as opposed to being stuck in the mud.

Dan Campbell, Drew Petzing, and Kelvin Sheppard: All three deserve credit for handling the international logistics and having their team prepared, ready to fight, and executing excellent game plans.

Jared Goff, QB: Goff is the most polarizing player in the stock report, no matter where he lands. The lovely Pride of Detroit commenter community will inevitably find something to disagree about. But despite the offense scoring five touchdowns against the Patriots, Goff's play was inconsistent, with some uncharacteristic decision-making and throws left on the table. He'll look to bounce back against the Buccaneers' defense and a cast of old Lions friends.

Sam LaPorta, TE: He again exceeded expectations with some of his blocking assignments, including more work in the backfield as an H-back or fullback. But you'd like to see LaPorta bring down the ball Goff delivered to him in the back of the end zone or flash his hands earlier on the seam ball down the middle. Over the last four weeks coming out of the bye, LaPorta has recorded 16 targets, nine catches, 99 receiving yards (11 yards per catch), and no touchdowns.

Ennis Rakestraw, CB: Just five snaps despite D.J. Reed being out with an injury, after playing only one snap two weeks earlier against the Vikings, and it appears Rakestraw has fallen out of favor a bit. He also had a poor missed tackle attempt on Hunter Henry during his only snap of the first half, which came on the opening drive.

Nick Whiteside, CB: In his first action of the season, Whiteside played only seven defensive snaps and was flagged for defensive pass interference on third-and-18 during the Patriots' only touchdown drive.

Khalil Dorsey, CB: Not as sharp of a day for Dorsey, who missed a tackle and allowed a catch-and-run to A.J. Brown on third down. There was another rep where Mack Hollins got open over the middle against Dorsey, but DeVito couldn't pull the trigger due to pressure. However, on an earlier third-and-20, Dorsey stopped Eli Raridon for a short gain to force a punt.

#2693. Wizards hammered by Memphis Grizzlies for 8th straight loss

The Wizards played 24 good minutes against the Memphis Grizzlies. Unfortunately, the game lasted 48 minutes. Their worst 12 minutes came in the third quarter. They started the period up 10 and finished it down 14, courtesy of a 42-18 beatdown by Cameron Boozer, Zach Edey, and the rest of the Grizzlies.

Fifteen games into the season where they hoped to compete again, the Wizards have a 4-11 record, and they're currently on an eight-game losing streak. It's starting to feel like last year. And the year before. And the year before.

If you missed this one, a) congratulations, and b) here are a few tidbits to illustrate how this one went:

Washington's defensive game plan was built around defensive indifference — they were going to concede three-point shots to Boozer. He missed his first five…and then made seven of his last eight. Boozer finished with a career-high 29 points,.

Edey dominated Washington's Twin Towers lineup, notching 23 points (on 10-15 shooting) and 10 rebounds in just 21 minutes.

Javon Small had his first career double-double — 12 points, 12 assists.

The Grizzlies shot 64.2% (effective field goal percentage) and rebounded 42.9% of their misses.

That's the second consecutive game the Wizards gave up a 60+% efg and an opponent offensive rebound rate above 40%. I don't know for sure because it probably never occurred to anyone to track this combination of stats, but I suspect it's exceedingly rare to do both in a single game. I mean, it never occurred to me to pay attention until the Wizards did it Sunday night. It never occurred to me they'd do the same thing on Monday night.

By the way, the Wizards entered the game ranked 28th in strength of schedule adjusted scoring margin. They probably can't fall in the rankings — they're ahead of the Milwaukee Bucks and Charlotte Hornets. But man…this is the team that's supposed to be trying to compete.

The problem is kinda everything. Trae Young and Anthony Davis are supposed to be the bedrock. The youngsters are supposed to be making some version of The Leap. Except, Young and Davis have been up and down. Both have exhibited problems with effort and focus (more on that in the notebook section). And none of the kids are leaping.

I mean, except Kyshawn George, if you squint just enough to miss all the crazy turnovers and dumb fouls.

While I don't think Xs and Os are a problem, there's a disconnect somewhere that shows up in the team's effort, execution, and ability to maintain focus for a full 48. Last night, for example, the team looked good for half the game. In the second half, they got pummeled, and no one showed a bit of fight. They all just kinda let it happen.

Brian Keefe was frustrated enough by the Houston debacle Sunday night that he publicly challenged their effort. That's a sign of desperation — a coach whose message isn't breaking through. And then they got thoroughly outplayed by Memphis.

Six minutes into the game, Memphis had six offensive rebounds. The Wizards had five defensive rebounds. For the game, it was 17 offensive boards for Memphis, and 20 defense rebounds for Washington.

What the hell was that? In the first quarter, the Grizzlies had a quasi-fast break led by Javon Small. Four Wizards were back. Trae Young looked like he was going to pick up ball, when he inexplicably gestured to Bilal Coulibaly to take ball, and then abruptly switched out onto a Grizzlies player on the wing. That gave Small a free run at the basket (Coulibaly was standing under the basket when the "pick him up" suggestion arrived). Coulibaly fouled late, and Small got a three-point play. I rewatched the play 10-12 times, and I still have no idea why Young didn't just pick up Small.

Late in the first quarter, Young had a thoroughly disgusting shot attempt — a 29-foot heave with nine seconds still on the shot clock, and a plenty of space to drive or make a good play.

Washington's defensive indifference meant that Boozer had more firts quarter three-point attempts in Grizzlies history.

Egregious effort from Anthony Davis midway through the third quarter. He tried for a steal and missed. And then…instead of hustling back into the action, he stood around on the perimeter watching and leaving his teammates to defend 5-0n-4. Memphis got an open three.

Keefe is trying a tighter and older rotation. Last night, they played nine while trying to compete — a nine that replaced Bub Carrington with Khris Middleton.

Another effort issue with Davis. He tried a shot, fell down, and didn't get the call he wanted from the refs. Instead of getting up and sprinting back, he lollygagged on the floor complaining and never got back across halfcourt. In the five-on-four, Boozer got a wide open three.

Defensive communication issue — in the third quarter, Davis dropped off Boozer at the three-point line and drifted down into the paint. No one picked him up, and a few seconds later, Boozer got a wide open three, which he converted into his sixth made triple.

I have two notes about Kyshawn George fouls in the fourth quarter. The first came early and was utterly pointless — he grabbed Cam Spencer on an Iverson cut for no discernible reason. The second came midway through the period when the Wizards seeemd to be cutting into the Memphis lead. For no reason I could determine, George grabbed Cedric Coward and sent him to the free throw line. It was dumb, pointless, and momentum-killing.

Edey was impressive. He scored comfortably on Davis and Sarr. He big-boyed George for an offensive rebound and foul on the putback. He's massive, strong, and skilled — great touch with both hands.

Edey had a great nope against George. George made a nice dribble-drive attack in transition, shook Spencer and seemed ready to make a run straight to the rim for a bucket and maybe a free throw. Except, the 7-3 Edey was in the way. George came to a screeching half, fired a crosscourt pass, which ultimately resulted in Young driving and missing a floater.

The Wizards led by as much as 12 in the first half…and trailed by as much as 20 in the second half.

Against the Wizards, you're never more than two passes from an open three.

Below are the four factors that decide wins and losses in basketball — shooting (efg), rebounding (offensive rebounds), ball handling (turnovers), fouling (free throws made).

eFG% (effective field goal percentage, which accounts for the three-point shot)

TOV% (turnover percentage — turnovers divided by possessions)

FTM/FGA (free throws made divided by field goal attempts)

PPA is my overall production metric, which credits players for things they do that help a team win (scoring, rebounding, playmaking, defending) and dings them for things that hurt (missed shots, turnovers, bad defense, fouls).

PPA is a per possession metric designed for larger data sets. In small sample sizes, the numbers can get weird. In PPA, 100 is average, higher is better and replacement level is 45. For a single game, replacement level isn't much use, and I reiterate the caution about small samples sometimes producing weird results.

POSS is the number of possessions each player was on the floor in this game.

ORTG = offensive rating, which is points produced per individual possessions x 100. League average so far this season is listed in the Four Factors table above. Points produced is not the same as points scored. It includes the value of assists and offensive rebounds, as well as sharing credit when receiving an assist.

USG = offensive usage rate. Average is 20%. Median last season was 17.7%.

ORTG and USG are versions of stats created by former Wizards assistant coach Dean Oliver and modified by me. ORTG is an efficiency measure that accounts for the value of shooting, offensive rebounds, assists and turnovers. USG includes shooting from the floor and free throw line, offensive rebounds, assists and turnovers.

+PTS = "Plus Points" is a measure of the points gained or lost by each player based on their efficiency in this game compared to league average efficiency on the same number of possessions. A player with an offensive rating (points produced per possession x 100) of 100 who uses 20 possessions would produce 20 points. If the league average efficiency is 115, the league — on average — would produced 23.0 points in the same 20 possessions. So, the player in this hypothetical would have a +PTS score of -3.0.

Players are sorted by total production in the game.

#2691. Saints vs. Panthers: The good, the bad, and the ugly in Week 10

The Saints rookie wide receiver caught 11 of 13 targets for 80 yards in just his second career game. He made a great diving catch over the middle to move the chains and also had some tough catches in traffic. Had this been his first game, he would have tied the record for most receptions in a debut since the AFL-NFL Merger in 1970. Tyson played 45 snaps on Sunday compared to just 18 last week and looks to be fully integrated into the offense now.

The Saints rushing attack once again produce a lackluster performance on Sunday. Tyler Shough was the leading rusher for the second time this year with just 25 rushing yards. Running backs Travis Etienne Jr, Audric Estime, and Alvin Kamara ran 17 times for just 24 yards. The Panthers are far from a good run defense, as entering the game on Sunday they were allowing an average of 133 rushing yards per game which was one of the worst marks in the NFL. The Saints have no variety in their offense and have no ability to sustain drives if the passing game is shut down. Compare that to the Panthers who were able to run the ball consistently with Chuba Hubbard. The Panthers did not turn the ball over and won the time of possession battle thanks to their commitment to the ground game, something the Saints have not been able to do all year.

The Saints are the worst first half team in football. They have trailed in every first half this season, and once again failed to do anything at all on offense until the middle of the third quarter. They turned it over three times (including a turnover on downs) and punted twice. Their deepest drive into Panther's territory ended at the 25-yard line when they gained 9 yards on 1st down and then ran the ball three times for no gain, no gain, and -1 yards resulting in a turnover on downs. All this while the defense held Carolina to just 7 first half points makes it even worse. This has been a problem in every game under Kellen Moore in the last two years. This must be fixed otherwise this coaching staff may be shown the door.
